The early Puritan settlers actually believed that only 144,000 people would be saved — the elect. Since there were already more than 144,000 Puritans, the odds in that theology were not favorable. The Jehovah's Witnesses hold a similar belief, drawn from the Book of Revelation. That is excessive fear.

Too much hope — excessive رَجَاءٌ — is the opposite disease, producing complacency: "I have no problem, I am saved," and so action stops entirely. There is an interesting observation one could make about certain Baptist theology, which holds that once you accept Jesus as your Savior, you are saved regardless of what you subsequently do. The emphasis falls overwhelmingly on salvation through faith, with very little weight placed on fear of hellfire. This is why someone like Jimmy Swaggart can fall publicly, and then return to the pulpit to preach — and the congregation is still there listening. If the same thing happened to an imam, *لَا طَهَّرَ اللَّهُ* — that would simply be the end. No one would retain any respect for that person, because Muslims hold far higher expectations of those they accept as examples and leaders in their communities.

This is, incidentally, one reason why it is *مَكْرُوهٌ* for the child of an illegitimate union to lead the prayer — not because of any sin on the child's part, but because such a person is not the most fitting *قُدْوَة*, or example, for the community. There is no *إِثْم* — no blame — on such a child. None whatsoever. What the parents did is entirely between them and their Lord. *‏أَلَّا تَزِرُ وَازِرَةٌۭ وِزْرَ أُخْرَىٰ ‎* — no soul bears the burden of another's wrongdoing. There were people in the Arabian Peninsula at the time of the Prophet ﷺ who were born outside of marriage, who became Muslim, and who were counted among the finest human beings. The least of the صَحَابَة, in terms of their deeds, surpasses the best of us — simply because they were given the *رُؤْيَة*, the vision of the Prophet ﷺ in waking life, and believed in him. That alone is a bounty from Allah beyond reckoning.

So: عَمَلٌ is a desire for long life, for wealth, for all the things we generally associate with happiness in this world. It is a kind of hope — a رَجَاءٌ. But there is something else called أُمْنِيَّةٌ, and it is أُمْنِيَّةٌ that is blameworthy in Islam. أُمْنِيَّة is a hope —

The difference between رَجَاح and أُمْنِيَة is that رَجَاح always involves taking the سَبَبٌ — in other words, actually pursuing the means to obtain what you want. With أُمْنِيَة, by contrast, it is بِلَا سَبَبٍ: no means are taken at all.

Say you want to get healthy. You start watching your diet, you start exercising — that is رَجَاح. But someone who says, "I really want to get healthy," while continuing to eat junk food and sit in front of the television, doing nothing to change his situation — that is أُمْنِيَة. And that, Allah says, is exactly what the Jews and Christians have toward the آخِرَة: they have أُمْنِيَة. They are doing nothing to earn the next life; in fact, they are corrupting in the earth. That is why Muslims must be on guard, because — as my friend Abd al-Hakim Murad once told a story about a Pakistani man who overheard two or three racists in London: one said he was moving to Wales because Muslims were only one or three percent of the population there, another said he preferred Scotland at two percent, and a third was going to Ireland at one percent. The Pakistani man said, "Why don't you go to hell? There are no Muslims there." What Abd al-Hakim observed about that is that the assumption, of course, is that Muslims don't go to hell. Unfortunately, there is going to be a great shock on يَوْمُ الْقِيَامَةِ — because if all you have is أُمْنِيَة, and all your Islam amounts to is the شَهَادَةُ تَيْمٍ, what they call the شَهَادَة of birth and death — شَهَادَةُ تَوْلِيدَتِكَ or شَهَادَةُ تَوَفَّاتِكَ — your testimony recorded at birth on your birth certificate and at death on your death certificate, if that is the sum of it, then you have a serious problem.

I was once in Santa Clara; Farif was with me. A family came — their grandfather had died, and they came to the masjid to bury him. None of them were practicing Muslims, and neither had their grandfather been, but when he died they realized, "We are Muslims — how do we bury him? Let's take him to the mosque." So they brought him, and we washed the body and performed the rites. But it is deeply sad when people's entire experience of Islam is reduced to the moments of marriage and death — they need an imam to perform the نِكَاح, or they need someone who knows how to wash the body and pray over it. That is أُمْنِيَة.

رَجَاء, then — hoping in Allah — means you are actually doing what is required to be pleasing to Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى. The scholars also say that hope should be the dominant state throughout one's life, but when you reach your deathbed, you should let go of fear entirely and rest in absolute hope in Allah. At that moment there should be no fear; you should be completely immersed in a sense of Allah's رَحْمَة, trusting that He will forgive you and accept you, and holدِينٌg the best opinion of Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى.

Now, Imam al-Haythami makes another point that Shaykh al-Khadim mentions in his commentary, and it is important: بُعْدُ الأَمَلِ — this extended hope, this drawn-out expectation — is, at its root, a heedlessness about the reality of death. But he says that in and of itself it is not a wrong action. There is no obligatory command (واجِبٌ) to remember death. When the Prophet ﷺ said to remember death, and also said, "I used to tell you not to visit graves, but now I tell you: go visit them, because it reminds you of the آخِرَة" — the point is that remembering death is مَنْدُوبٌ, recommended. In the same way, ذِكْر beyond what is prescribed in the prayer is مَنْدُوبٌ:

اُذْكُرِ اللَّهَ — اُذْكُرُوا — there is the imperative, the command, but it is for نَدْب, not وُجُوب. You are not obligated to remember Allah as a فَرْضٌ; it is مَنْدُوبٌ, مُسْتَحَبٌّ, strongly encouraged, because it prepares you. So heedlessness of death is not a wrong action in itself.

<!-- page 85 -->

Similarly, making money, being engaged in the *دُنْيَا*, being active in worldly affairs — that is *مُبَاحٌ*. Allah did not prohibit it. You may trade on *حَجٌّ*; there is nothing wrong with it. You can combine *دُنْيَا* and آخِرَة on *حَجٌّ*. You do not have to go in a state of pure spiritual detachment. You can take goods, bring books, sell things — within whatever is permitted. People who shave pilgrims, bringing razors and charging for the service — they are *حُجَّاج* and they are earning money, and there is nothing wrong with that. Our *دِينٌ* does not even frown upon it. You should not say *أَسْتَغْفِرُ اللهَ* and think they are not spiritual people; that attitude is not from our *دِينٌ*.

So he says: that is permissible — *unless* it was done for *تَفَاخُر* or *تَكَاثُر*, or for *تَسْوِيفُ التَّوْبَةِ*, in which case it becomes a grave sin (*كَبِيرَةٌ*). If you are acquiring wealth in order to compete with others out of arrogance, or to hoard it in a contest of accumulation, or — *تَسْوِيفُ التَّوْبَةِ* — to keep putting off repentance, then it is blameworthy. As the *آيَةٌ* says:

*بَلْ يُرِيدُ الْإِنْسَانُ لِيَفْجُرَ أَمَامَهُ، يَسْأَلُ أَيَّانَ يَوْمُ الْقِيَامَةِ*

Man wants to keep sinning in whatever remains of his life and asks, "When is the Day of Judgment?" — as though it is far off, as though he need not think about it. The *عُلَمَاء* say in their commentaries on this *آيَةٌ* that one of its meanings is *يُسَوِّفُ التَّوْبَةَ* — he keeps postponing his repentance. This is what is called in this culture "sowing your wild oats" — the idea that when you are young and have the energy for *مَعَاصِي*, you should get them out of your system, because you won't have the energy when you are older. This is a concept in American and European culture. And this is precisely why Allah loves the young man who is in obedience — because when you grow old, you no longer have the energy for disobedience.

Some people live badly for decades, then around forty-five or fifty, death begins to feel real, they make *تَوْبَة*, and then they become self-righteous, pointing fingers at everyone else, having completely forgotten who they themselves were. This is a real problem — fifty years old, having done every terrible thing: drinking, cheating, you name it — and then at fifty realizing mortality, going to the masjid, becoming outwardly devout, and immediately beginning to judge others. That is a disease of its own.

## Negative Thoughts

Now, the next disease is called *طِيَرَة*, and it is a somewhat nuanced concept because it has a related term in the *حَدِيث* vocabulary, *طِيَرَة*. The Arabs, before Islam, when they wanted to make a decision, would go to a flock of birds and startle them — a form of divination. If the flock flew to the left, they took it as a bad omen; if it flew to the right, they took it as a positive omen. The right-flying birds they called *يُمْنٌ* — from which *Yemen*, being to the right when one faces the rising sun — and the left-flying birds they called *شُؤْم*, which is why the Levant (*الشَّامُ*) carries that sense of the left. In the Arabian Peninsula, facing the sun in the morning, Yemen is on the right (*اَلْيَمِينُ*) and الشَّامُ is to the left. You also get *شَمَلَ* — north, the left side — and *جُنُب*, south, from *جَنْب*, meaning side or flank, because you recline on your right side; so the south is on the right and the north is on the left.

This is also reflected in traditional Muslim cartography. Muslims always oriented their maps with the south at the top and the north at the bottom. I can show you maps from the classical books of *تَوْقِيتٌ* — the science of timekeeping, the *عِلْمُ الْفَلَكِ* of the Muslims — and all of them show the south at the top. This is why I have long believed that Muslims should make their own maps.

I first came to this realization years ago in New Mexico, when I was at a flea market with Hakim Archuleta. I found an old antique compass, and it was cheap. The vendor said, "There's only one problem — instead of pointing north, the arrow points south." I said, "That's fantastic." As we walked away I asked my friend Hakim, who knows a great deal about science, why it would make any difference. He said it's simply a matter of polarization — the paint is facing south because that is the *قُطْب*, the pole. That started me thinking about the question of south and north, and why we have made north dominant.

Then I came across a map by al-Idrisi — the first great cartographer of the world, a Moroccan who drew a world map with the south at the top and the north at the bottom. The European mapmakers drew directly from his work, but they recognized the significance of putting the south at the top: it placed Africa above Europe. So they flipped it, and from that point on their maps had north at the top. Marshall Hodgson makes exactly this observation in *The Venture of Islam* — he says it is unfortunate that Muslims do not recognize that they produced the first world map and oriented it with the south at the top, and that they should return to that orientation, because the north-dominant map reflects a purely Eurocentric worldview that has no historical foundation. A south-oriented map would put Africa at the top and Europe at the bottom; we would say, "I'm going down to Europe and up to Africa."

We would also have to say "up to Los Angeles and down to San Francisco." And in Arabic, this is in fact how it works — the south is up. Southern Egypt is called *صَعِيد*, Upper Egypt, while the north is Lower Egypt. In Libya as well, people say, "We are going up south" and "down to the sea," which is in the north. That is traditional cosmology, and do not think it is insignificant — the effect of spending a lifetime looking at maps that place Africa at the bottom is real. On a south-oriented map, moreover, nothing looms over Arabia. Look at the map the Australians produced when they decided they were tired of being "down under" — they simply turned it right-side up. I have a map from New Zealand, sent to me by a friend after I told her about this. It is a genuinely revealing perspective: you see how close Ireland is to Morocco, how close Germany is to England, and you begin to understand the depth of the Germanic-English connection in a way the north-dominant map never quite conveys. But that is a digression from the diseases of the heart — a worthwhile one, I think, but a digression nonetheless.

So *طِيَرَة* — the birds flying right or left — is at its root superstition, but it is also more than that, because in Arabic the word for a pessimist derives from the same concept: the pessimist is the one who always sees the worst in any situation, who will say about any plan, "It will never work." The root of *طِيَرَة* is the ignorance that all affairs belong to اللَّه alone. For the Muslim, this means having a *حُسْنُ الظَّنِّ* — a good opinion — of اللَّه. The non-Muslim has a bad opinion of اللَّه; the Muslim has a good one. That is the essential difference. So you should always cultivate this good opinion of اللَّه.

He now moves toward *سُوءُ الظَّنِّ* — bad opinion — but before doing so I want to say one more thing about *طِيَرَة*. There are really two types. The first — valid for people of sound intellect and normative experience — is when *الْحُكْمُ الْعَادِي*, empirical probability, establishes that a thing consistently causes harm. If you put your hand near a cobra, it bites you and you die. In that case, you must use your intellect; avoiding it is not *طِيَرَة*, it is rational. If something is harmful by established norm, you must avoid it, and the scholars say that if you do not, you are not considered a person of sound reason — you are acting foolishly. There are, of course, exceptional cases: one of the *أَوْلِيَاء* was walking with a student, they came upon a lion on the path, the student was terrified, the shaykh told him to take the high road and continued walking, and the lion did not touch him. But that is not a station available to everyone.

<!-- page 88 -->

Imam al-Qarafi, a great Maliki scholar, says that *طِيَرَة* is a bad opinion and *طِيَرَة* as an *action* — *عَمَلٌ* — is what results from holding that bad opinion, as long as the normative experience in which harm results does not apply. Walking under a ladder, for instance. In this culture: step on a crack, you'll break your mother's back. Children grow up avoiding the cracks in the pavement. A black cat crossing your path — thirteen years of bad luck. Breaking a mirror. Knocking on wood: "I've been healthy for years" — then you knock wood. These are all *طِيَرَة* and are in fact *حَرَامٌ* to act upon. If the Shari'a gives us specific *أَذْكَار* and practices, those are different — they come from revelation, not superstition. But knocking on wood goes back to pagan tree-worship; those who venerated sacred trees would knock on them. That is the origin. Among the Arabs in the *جَاهِلِيَّة*, things like walking between two sheep were considered bad luck. Seeing an owl was an ill omen. In some cultures, a shooting star was a bad omen. All of these are forms of *طِيَرَة*.

The Prophet ﷺ also established that no one will ever be entirely free of *طِيَرَة* — it is part of human nature. There is a book by the skeptic Michael Shermer called *Why People Believe Weird Things*, and much of it addresses exactly this: how deeply embedded superstition is in human psychology. The *حَدِيث* points to the same reality. People check their horoscope before leaving the house. People will not buy or sell stocks without first consulting their astrologer — a completely absurd premise, yet they believe in it and it gives them a sense of control. People consult the I Ching before making any significant decision.

There's people that do *رَمَلٌ* in the Sahara Desert, and there are those who flip coins or toss them into wishing wells. All of these things are really part of human nature.

So when the Prophet ﷺ mentioned that one of three things will inevitably come to you in life, one of them was ill omens — *طِيَرَة*. And he said the way to cure it is this: if you feel that bad omen rising in you, just keep going. So if, in this culture, you're about to walk under a ladder and something stirs in your heart, you just keep walking — that's how you break it. And if a plant falls on your head — no, I'm just kidding. But that really is the way you break it: you ignore it and keep moving.

A friend of mine who has strong إِيمَان told me about someone who claimed to be a وَلِيٌّ — a claim my friend didn't think was warranted — and he said something about it. The followers of this man turned to my friend and said, "Don't you know? If he makes دُعَاء against you, he can destroy you." And my friend replied, "Go ahead — make دُعَاء against me right now. I want you to." What he was trying to show them was that this is not how Muslims behave. Real Muslims don't make دُعَاء against one another, and a true وَلِيٌّ certainly doesn't. And if he truly is a وَلِيٌّ, اللَّه can bring you difficulty without him even intending it.

There is a Sheikh I know in Mauritania, Muhammad Zayn — whom some of you have met — who is from the family of the Prophet ﷺ. He is a deeply righteous man who would never willingly harm anyone. But it is well known that people who speak ill of him or bear bad attitudes toward him tend to find trouble. So you don't want to be careless with the أَوْلِيَاء. At the same time, as the Moroccans say: don't belittle any Muslim, because he might be a وَلِيٌّ of اللَّه. In other words, be generally mindful of harming any Muslim — it is simply not a good thing to do.

So that is the matter of طِيَرَة. Now the next disease is having a bad opinion of others — what is called ظَنٌّ.

<!-- page 93 -->

ظَنٌّ is suspicion, conjecture, opinion formed without any supporting facts. The worst form of bad opinion is harboring it toward someone whose outward appearance and conduct are plainly righteous. Outward appearance was something the early Muslims actually considered significant. While they also cautioned against dismissing those who may look unimpressive — because sometimes اللَّه veils His friends behind an ordinary exterior — they recognized what the Arabs call وَسْمَة: the quality of being وَسِيمٌ, someone whose face carries a kind of nobility. When an Arab delegate came to the Prophet ﷺ and then departed, the companions asked what he thought of him. He said, "His face is not the face of a liar." This is what is called فِرَاسَةٌ — the capacity to discern goodness or corruption in a person's countenance. So forming a bad opinion of someone who possesses this وَسْمَة, who outwardly presents as a righteous person, is a particularly serious form of ظَنٌّ.

اللَّه says that some forms of ظَنٌّ are wrong action. This is important because ordinarily we are not held accountable for what merely passes through the heart — but there is a هِبَة of the heart, a form of backbiting that occurs entirely within the chest: entertaining a bad and unfounded opinion of someone, and it is written against you. This is called هِبَةُ الْقَلْبِ. It is unlike the passing thoughts that cross the mind without being invited — you are not accountable for those. What does incur accountability is what the scholars call عَزِيمَة: the thought that settles and takes root. We will go into these levels in more detail when we reach the section on قَوَاطِرُ, because there are degrees — حَدِيثُ النَّفْسِ, عَزِيمَة — and عَزِيمَة is the highest degree, the one written against you. When it runs deep in your heart such that it changes how you treat someone, it becomes هِبَةُ الْقَلْبِ — a form of backbiting in which no second person is even present. It is entirely between you and your own heart. And that is not permissible. It is written against you.

So the condition for blameworthy ظَنٌّ is that your heart has reached a verdict — عَزِيمَة — judging a person based purely on suspicion, without a دَلِيل, without any evidence that actually warrants that judgment. If someone asks you, "Why don't you like that person?" and you say, "I just feel it in my heart — something is wrong," that is a different matter we will come to shortly. But consider the حَدِيث: إِيَّاكُمْ وَالظَّنَّ، فَإِنَّ الظَّنَّ أَكْذَبُ الْحَدِيثِ — "Beware of ظَنٌّ, for ظَنٌّ is the most deceptive of speech." If you tell someone, "So-and-so is a really bad person," and they ask, "What's your proof?" and you say, "I just know" — that is أَكْذَبُ الْحَدِيثِ. That is a lie, because you have no دَلِيل. Our شَرِيعَة is built on the principle: أُمِرْتُ أَنْ أَحْكُمَ بِالظَّوَاهِرِ — "I was commanded to judge by outward matters." So it is essential to look at behavior, at what is manifest.

Then he says: لَا إِثْمَ فِي الشَّكِّ وَعَدَمِ الِاسْتِنَادِ لِسَبَبٍ فَعَدَمٌ يَكُونُ مُجَرَّدًا — there is nothing wrong with having doubts about someone, or even a bad opinion of him, if that opinion is grounded in sound reason and not merely baseless. This is a critical distinction.

The difference between شَكٌّ and ظَنٌّ is this: ظَنٌّ is when your internal scales have tipped — you have made a judgment. Shakk is when both pans are level. Is he good? Unknown. Is he bad? Unknown. That is شَكٌّ: "I simply don't know, and I must be cautious." That is not حَرَامٌ. ظَنٌّ is when you come to a conclusion — without proof — that the person is bad. Everyone understand the difference? This distinction will genuinely serve you in life.

So how do we deal with people about whom we feel genuine doubt? First, if the person is a فَاسِق — and a فَاسِق is someone who commits major sins openly, or who is habitually careless about minor ones, consistently and without remorse — then اللَّه says: if a فَاسِق comes to you with a report, verify it before you act on it. You cannot take what such a person says at face value.

Now, if what you have is mere شَكٌّ — not certainty, just unresolved doubt — what سِيدِي أَحْمَد زَرُّوق advises is excellent counsel, and though it comes from the ninth century it is even more pertinent today: do not entrust your دِين, your family, or your wealth to anyone until you have tested them a thousand thousand times. That is of course مُبَالَغَةٌ — hyperbole — but his point is plain: test people. Observe whether they are genuine and sincere. If you don't yet know, don't trust.

One of the poets said: "A good opinion in these days is foolishness — think the worst of people and stay on guard." And truly, this is what the عُلَمَاء say: when the times are full of corruption, the sound position is to be cautious and distrustful of people — not from ظَنٌّ, not from having made a judgment, but simply from prudence. You don't extend trust until it has been earned, because so many people lack تَقْوَى. They will smile at your face, speak beautifully of their own faith and virtue, and then cheat you. It happens all the time.

That is also why the عُلَمَاء say that in corrupt times, it becomes obligatory for those distributing زَكَاةٌ to investigate the circumstances of claإِيمَانts. They cannot simply hand it over. When people are generally righteous, you take them at their word. But when the times are corrupt and someone comes saying, "Brother, I have five children and no money," you bear a responsibility to verify the claim. The claإِيمَانts cannot be upset or indignant about this — they must accept it. It is not about distrusting them personally. It is, as they say in this culture, that the bad ones have ruined it for the good ones. The response has to be: "Unfortunately, because this is an أَمَانَة from اللَّه, we have to verify. It isn't that we doubt you — this is a blanket policy, for that reason. And we are sorry that the reality has come to this."

So that is the matter of bad opinion of others. Having doubts about someone — شَكٌّ — is fine. Having a bad opinion of someone when there is ample evidence he is truly a bad person is simply common sense. On the other hand: when people make تَوْبَة, do not hold their past over them. One of the worst things you can do to a person is remind them of the evil they have left behind. Let people move forward. Do not cling to their past, because it is deeply unfair.

And there is another dimension here. A good opinion of اللَّه and a good opinion of the servants of اللَّه — no مُؤْمِن was ever given anything better than these two. Generally, it is praiseworthy to have a good opinion: certainly of اللَّه, and generally of His servants. ابْنُ عَمَّارٍ said that no servant was ever harmed by a good opinion, whereas a bad opinion can injure you. And in our شَرِيعَة the principle is البَرَاءَةُ الأَصْلِيَّةُ — people are presumed innocent until there is real cause to conclude otherwise.

The other point of supreme importance is the opinion one holds of اللَّه. There is a حَدِيث reported by al-Tabarani: أَنَا عِنْدَ ظَنِّ عَبْدِي بِي، فَلْيَظُنَّ عَبْدِي بِي مَا شَاءَ — "I am as My servant thinks of Me, so let My servant think of Me whatever he will." Another حَدِيث clarifies it further: فَمَنْ ظَنَّ بِي خَيْرًا وَجَدَ خَيْرًا، وَمَنْ ظَنَّ بِي شَرًّا وَجَدَ شَرًّا — "I am as My servant thinks of Me. Whoever thinks well of Me finds good; whoever thinks ill of Me finds ill." This حَدِيث is the key to understanding another, found in the Forty Hadith of Imam al-Nawawi, in which it is stated that every servant receives what he deserves, and then: فَمَنْ وَجَدَ خَيْرًا فَلْيَحْمَدِ اللهَ — "If he finds good, let him thank اللَّه" — وَمَنْ وَجَدَ غَيْرَ ذَٰلِكَ فَلَا يَلُومَنَّ إِلَّا نَفْسَهُ — "and if he finds otherwise, let him blame no one but his own soul."

The underlying idea is that if you are a true مُؤْمِن, everything ultimately works in your favor. The affair of the مُؤْمِن is all good. Even the bad that befalls you — the true مُؤْمِن believes it carries a reason, and that there is good within it. This is a fundamentally different worldview from that of the مُتَأَيِّس, the pessimist, who falls into what might be called سَخْطَةُ الْقَدَرِ — railing against destiny, saying, "Why is this happening to me? I don't deserve this." The Prophet ﷺ himself said something to this effect: "If You are not angry with me, I do not care" — and he acknowledged that ease would naturally be more comfortable, but that the difficulty of a hard moment is itself a good thing for the مُؤْمِن. That is what it means to have a good opinion of اللَّه and a good opinion of others.

## Vanity

The next disease is called عُجْب.

Imam al-Ghazali says the distinction between عُجْب and كِبْرٌ is that كِبْرٌ requires two people. Kibr is arrogance — it needs the مُتَكَبِّر, the one who is arrogant, and the one toward whom the arrogance is directed. If Zayd comes to Amr and says, "You insignificant fool — why didn't you do what I told you?" Zayd is the مُتَكَبِّر and Amr is the one being looked down upon. Ujb, by contrast, is like كِبْرٌ, except that it requires no second person. The person afflicted with عُجْب is simply impressed with himself. If he has a mirror, he is content — he looks and is genuinely dazzled by how wonderful and magnificent he is.

He defines عُجْب as follows: you magnify and exalt the نِعْمَ — the blessing — while forgetting the one who gave it. In this culture, if someone is told, "You're so beautiful," and they respond, "Thank you," as though they had done something to bring it about — that is a strange thing, if you think about it carefully. Perhaps they spent a great deal on reconstructive surgery, but even then they should be thanking the surgeon, not congratulating themselves. The point is: if Allah made someone beautiful, they should be grateful to Allah. The مُنْعِمٌ — the one who bestowed the gift — is Allah, not the person themselves. They did nothing to bring it about.

And even when real human effort is involved — when someone has a genuine talent that took years to develop — that talent, in its raw form, was still a gift from Allah. The labor it took to develop it was itself made possible by تَوْفِيق from Allah. You see this clearly within families.

<!-- page 95 -->

Consider beautiful calligraphy. It looks effortless to the observer. I once sat with Sidi Muhammad Zakariyya — the well-known American calligrapher — and watched him work. He made it look so natural that I thought, "I could do that." They say the mark of a master is precisely this: he makes it look so easy that you believe you could do it yourself — but you couldn't. Imam al-Jurjani said much the same of the master of rhetoric: when he speaks, he speaks with such ease and fluency that the listeners think, "I could do that." They could not.

What you have to bear in mind when you see Sidi Muhammad Zakariyya execute his calligraphy is the years of patient, painstaking repetition that preceded it — practicing waws and ya's and ba's, writing out passages again and again. You see the finished work and say, "سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ." And that is a gift from Allah. Now if someone given such a gift were to say, "Look at all my accomplishments — I did this myself, I pulled myself up by my own effort" — that is عُجْب of the worst kind. And there are countless people in the world who think exactly this: that whatever they have achieved, they achieved alone. "I built myself. I earned this." That is عُجْبُ النَّفْسِ.

The مُؤْمِن, by contrast, understands: if Allah gave you the تَوْفِيق to be disciplined, that too was from Allah.

One person grows up with discipline; another, in the same household with the same environmental factors, does not. The disciplined one goes on to master their studies, graduates with honors — and all of it is from Allah. As Imam al-Razi says, Allah is the maker of every maker and every made thing. Allah created the one who built the airplane and the airplane itself. Allah created the one who designed the computer and the computer itself. Once you truly grasp that, the urge to be impressed with yourself dissolves.

And then there is this: above everyone who knows something, there is always someone who knows more. This is the lesson Musa, عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ, had to learn. When he was asked whether anyone on earth was more knowledgeable than he was, he said he did not think so. So Allah sent him to الْخَضِر — who was not a prophet — and made him a student. Even then, he did not prove to be as patient a student as he could have been. The Prophet, صلى الله عليه وسلم, said that had Musa not asked those questions, he would have learned far more from الْخَضِر. He did not absorb everything he might have, yet he was still taught. And the greatest lesson he took away was this: there is always someone who knows more than you, so never forget that.

We must choose our words carefully when speaking of prophets. I am not saying Musa was a poor student — he was, I am sure, extraordinary. But he had a lesson to learn, and Allah taught it to him through الْخَضِر, عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ.

Now, regarding vanity — the reason I translated *عُجْب* as vanity is that, in the dictionary, vanity is defined as excessive pride in one's appearance or accomplishments, which maps closely onto the Arabic. The root *عَجِيبَة* means to wonder or marvel at something; *عُجْب* is to marvel at oneself or one's own achievements. The word *vanity* itself derives from the Latin *vanus*, meaning empty — and so the very source of our vanity is empty and will one day vanish.

The Romans understood this. When a general like Caesar held a victory parade through Rome, riding through the city on a chariot while crowds cheered, a slave would walk alongside him holding a laurel wreath over his head and repeating: *all is vanity*. That slave was there to remind the triumphant general — it is all vanity. This is an ancient wisdom, a *حِكْمَة*, a reminder that all these accomplishments will eventually be forgotten. Charles Lindbergh is a case in point: he received an enormous ticker-tape parade in New York City, and within ten years the American government considered him a criminal for his Nazi sympathies. The hero had fallen.

There is a story — I believe it was written by James Thurber, who wrote *The Secret Life of Walter Mitty* — about a man who saves a child, and the reporters rush in to make him a hero. It turns out he is actually a disreputable person who simply wants to profit from the incident. As he stands at the window watching the adoring crowds below, one of the reporters pushes him out. The point is that there is always another side. No matter how much good a person does, there will always be detractors — or the glory itself will simply prove hollow.

Think of the Yankees parading through New York, celebrated as the greatest team in the world, while the city itself is in moral, social, and ethical disarray. That is *عُجْب* at a collective scale — *الْمُعْجَبَةُ بِالنَّفْسِ* — and it is a deeply troubling condition.

He then notes: knowing that you are incapable, that you can neither produce any benefit nor ward off any harm on your own, should be enough to strip away all vanity. Vanity originates from nothing other than ignorance of two truths: that Allah alone is the fashioner and giver of blessings, and that you are incapable of anything by yourself. Forgetting those two things is how *عُجْب* takes hold.

This is a disease one must always be on guard against — especially when one does something well. And it is particularly relevant for those whom Allah has blessed with outward, physical beauty. Becoming vain about one's appearance is a dangerous trap, one that both men and women can fall into, though it is an especially acute danger for women.

## Fraud

The next disease he addresses is *غِشٌّ* — fraud. He defines it as concealing some fault or harm, whether religious or worldly, even when the other party is a member of a protected minority or someone who has a treaty with the Muslims. Others have interpreted it as adorning something that has no real benefit, or making an evil appear to be good.

There is a hadith in which the Prophet, صلى الله عليه وسلم, said: *Whoever tricks us is not from us.* A Muslim is not someone who engages in fraud. And fraud, he notes, can occur in worldly affairs as well as religious ones. In Islamic law, it is prohibited to sell something while concealing a known defect. If you sell a car and genuinely do not know that the engine is in poor condition, that is one matter — but if you know it, you are obligated to disclose it. Failing to do so is a disease of the heart; that is precisely where it originates.

I have noted in the commentary that this disease is conspicuous in modern advertising, which to my mind suggests that only a diseased heart could produce that kind of work comfortably. It also has a historical precedent in the sophists of ancient Greece. The sophists worshipped rhetoric and were, in effect, the first historical relativists. Relativism holds that there is no right and wrong — might makes right, and the most persuasive argument wins, regardless of its truth. That is a thoroughly modern disease as well, but the sophists were its original practitioners. They mastered rhetoric and believed the only thing that truly mattered was being convincing. Truth or falsehood was beside the point; what mattered was getting what you wanted through the art of persuasion.

For the Muslim intellectual tradition, by contrast, rhetoric is the art of presenting truth in the most compelling way — not of dressing up falsehood. It is a fundamentally different worldview. The sophists, by their own admission, set out to make the worse cause appear to be the better one. They believed that men are either wolves or sheep: smart wolves eat the dumb sheep. This is, unfortunately, a widely held view in modern corporate culture — that competition justifies whatever means are available to you. The word *sophisticated* itself traces back to the Greek root that gave us *sophist* — people who thought themselves clever and wise.

Next week, he is going to address what I think is one of the most consequential diseases in the entire book — *الْعُجْبُ الْكَبِيرُ*, or rather *الرِّيَاءُ* — and he spends nearly as much time on it as he does on some of the other major diseases. We will take that up next session.

---

Are there any questions?

*On الْخَضِرُ and مُوسَى:* Yes, مُوسَى met الْخَضِرُ before he was sent to the بَنِي إِسْرَائِيلَ. الْخَضِرُ was a prophet — that is a separate matter.

*On bad opinion versus hatred:* Having a bad opinion of someone means thinking ill of them without evidence. Hatred is something different — it is an actual emotion, a feeling. We will get into that in more detail when we reach that chapter. It becomes truly dangerous when you act on it. The Arabic terms are *سُوءُ الظَّنِّ* and *بُغْضٌ*.

*On fraud toward non-Muslims:* What he is saying is that *غِشٌّ* — fraud — applies even in dealings with a *ذِمِّي*, a non-Muslim living under the protection of the Muslim state. You cannot cheat a non-Muslim simply because they are not Muslim. You are obliged to be upright in your transactions with everyone.

*On reminding people of past wrongs:* Generally speaking, if something happened in the past and a person has moved on from it, continually reminding them of it is not fair, because it closes the door to change. If they are repeating the behavior, that is a different matter — but for past mistakes they have left behind, it is better to let it go.

*On the word* يُسْرَى *and the grammatical point about* عُسْر: There is a tradition of saying that one difficulty will not overcome two eases, and the grammatical basis for this is in the Quranic ayah. Allah says: *‏إِنَّ مَعَ ٱلْعُسْرِ يُسْرًۭا ‎* — and then repeats it. The word *الْعُسْر* carries the definite article *الْـ*, which in the second instance is what grammarians call *زُهْد* — it refers back to the same *عُسْر* mentioned before. So it is the same singular difficulty appearing both times. But *يُسْرَى*, the ease, is indefinite — *نَقِّرَه* — in both instances. Because it is a new indefinite noun each time, it counts as two separate eases. The lesson is that with any one difficulty come two eases.

*On energy and vibrations between people:* I think there is something real to the experience of feeling uplifted or dragged down by the people around you. We would express it in our tradition as someone having a good or bad soul, or being a good or bad person. The Arabic *مُنْسَجِم* literally means to be in the flow, in harmony — and you can see the analogy in music: a cacophonous sound creates a vibration that hurts the ear, while something melodious has a positive effect. So the sense that certain people carry a certain energy is not entirely foreign to Islamic thought, even if we frame it differently.

All of us have had the experience of being around someone who is deeply depressed and finding it difficult not to start feeling depressed ourselves. People do seem to have fields — auras, if you want to use that word — that are either uplifting, draining, or simply neutral.

I would also say this: feeling uncomfortable around someone is not the same as judging them to be a bad person. One of my teachers, Sheikh Hamid, told me a story from his student days. There was a fellow student who liked him very much and always wanted to be near him, but he simply did not feel a connection with that person. He went to his teacher feeling guilty about it, and his teacher said: souls are arranged in ranks. Some souls are simply not harmonious with one another. It does not mean either party is bad — it is just that some people you do not feel a natural affinity with, while with others you feel an immediate and powerful connection.

Some of the scholars say this has to do with our pre-worldly existence — the gathering of the *أَرْوَاح*, the spirits, before they entered this world. The closer your soul was to another soul on that day, the more affinity you will feel toward that person in this life. The further away, the more distant you will feel. This would explain why you can meet someone for the first time and feel as though you have known them all your life. I felt that immediately with Sheikh Abdallah الْـQadi — the moment I met him, it was as though I had always known him. Other connections develop slowly, over time. There is wisdom in both kinds.

*On distrust as distinct from bad opinion:* Having a bad opinion of a specific person without evidence is one thing. Being generally cautious and guarded as a disposition is something else. The scholars say that in difficult times, a degree of wariness is actually wisdom — it is being on your guard, not maligning anyone in particular.

Don't become paranoid — that's a psychological condition to be avoided. Seriously. You don't want to be the person who says, "Do you hear what he said? Did you see how he looked at me?" That kind of thinking is an affliction.

## Anger

The next section covers غَضَبٌ, which means anger. He opens it by describing anger as the swelling ocean of all these diseases of the heart. Just coming to its shore — not even venturing into it — you will see things that astonish you: waves overflowing, an expanse beyond reckoning. Say of it what you will, without constraint, because it is a truly remarkable thing.

There is a hadith — one with some weakness in its chain, though its meaning is important — in which a man came to the Prophet ﷺ from the front and asked, "O Messenger of Allah, tell me: what is the best thing for drawing near to Allah?" The Prophet ﷺ replied, "حُسْنُ الْخُلُقِ" — good character. The man then came from the right side and asked the same question, and received the same answer. He came from the left and asked again, and again the Prophet ﷺ said, "حُسْنُ الْخُلُقِ." Finally, the man came from behind him and asked once more. This time the Prophet ﷺ said, "أَوَلَمْ تَفْقَهْ؟ لَا تَغْضَبْ" — "Have you not understood? Do not get angry."

In another hadith, a man asked the Prophet ﷺ what the worst thing was that could occur with respect to Allah. The Prophet ﷺ said, "His anger." The man asked how they could avoid it, and the Prophet ﷺ replied: do not get angry yourself. If you do not want Allah to be angry with you, do not give in to anger. "كَيْثَمَا تَدِينُ تُدَانُ" — as you conduct yourself, so will it be shown to you.

Then there is the famous hadith in which a man said, "يَا رَسُولَ اللهِ, give me some advice." The Prophet ﷺ replied, "لَا تَغْضَبْ" — do not get angry. The man said he wanted more advice. The Prophet ﷺ again said, "لَا تَغْضَبْ." The man asked a third time, and three times the Prophet ﷺ repeated, "لَا تَغْضَبْ." This hadith, the scholars say, is the controlling hadith — the key to good character.

Now, when the Prophet ﷺ said "لَا تَغْضَبْ," the scholars of Allah — our عُلَمَاء, including Imam النَّوَوِيُّ — explained that he did not mean: do not feel anger. He did not mean: suppress the emotion entirely. Because the Messenger of Allah ﷺ himself said, "إِنَّمَا أَنَا بَشَرٌ أَغْضَبُ مِثْلَكُمْ" — I am a human being, and I get angry just as you do. His anger was visible in his face; a vein in his forehead would appear when he was angry.

So the Prophet ﷺ was not instructing us to eliminate the feeling of anger, because anger is not inherently negative. It is, in fact, essential to the human character. Many of the world's injustices would never be opposed if people lacked the capacity for anger. What the Prophet ﷺ meant, according to the scholars, was: do not let your anger take control of you. Remain in command of your anger.

Some of the عُلَمَاء have likened anger to a hunting dog: if you do not train it, it will not bring you what you need. It must be disciplined. Without anger, injustice would go unchallenged — people could act with impunity and no one would object. That is not what Allah wants. He wants us to become angry, but not for our own sake. He wants our anger to be for His sake. This is the same transformation that applies to the other diseases of the heart: you redirect them so they work for you rather than against you.

The reason the author calls anger the ocean is that it drowns many people. They sink into the sea of wrath, and it carries them toward destruction and despair.

<!-- page 99 -->

Now, before moving into the detailed discussion, I want to make a detour — though it is not really a detour, because it is directly related to all of this.

Imam الْغَزَالِيّ identified four essential qualities of the human soul. This framework also appears in earlier traditions and is not unique to Islam, but Muslim scholars recognized its truth and adopted it. فَخْرُ الدِّينِ الرَّازِيُّ adopted it. Imam رَاغِب الأَصْفَهَانِي adopted it in his book on أَخْلَاق. Imam الْغَزَالِيّ adopted it as well.

The model examines the human soul and recognizes four fundamental powers. The first is called قُوَّةُ الْعَقْلِ — what the Western tradition calls the rational soul. It is the power within the human being that is related to what a person knows and understands. The second is قُوَّةُ الْغَضَبِ — the irascible soul — the element that relates to emotions, whose foundation is anger. Imam Jalal al-Din al-Rumi once observed that the wisdom behind the prohibition of wine is that most people carry anger at their core, and when they drink, that root is exposed. People become belligerent; their inherent anger surfaces. He said it was as though Allah, in His mercy, veiled people from exposing what lies at their core. And indeed, for most people, قُوَّةُ الْغَضَبِ sits at the very root of the soul.

The third is قُوَّةُ الشَّهْوَةِ — the appetitive soul, the soul related to desire and appetite, to one's شَهْوَانِيٌّ nature. The fourth is قُوَّةُ الْعَدْلِ, which, according to Imam الْغَزَالِيّ, is the power that harmonizes the other three so that the human being functions in a healthy and بَلَاءٌnced way — none of them thrown out of equilibrium.

These four powers correspond to what both the Western and Islamic traditions call the cardinal virtues. The Muslims call them أُمَّهَاتُ الْفَضَائِلِ: wisdom, courage, temperance, and justice — or in Arabic, حِكْمَةٌ، شَجَاعَةٌ، عِفَّةٌ، وَعَدْلٌ.

When the rational soul is in بَلَاءٌnce, the result is حِكْمَةٌ. Allah ﷻ says: وَمَن يُؤْتَ ٱلْحِكْمَةَ فَقَدْ أُوتِىَ خَيْرًۭا كَثِيرًۭا — "Whoever is given wisdom has been given much good." Imam الْغَزَالِيّ says that حِكْمَةٌ is the mean between بَلَدَة — stupidity — and خِدَاعٌ — cunning. If the rational soul is deficient, the result is foolishness: people do stupid things. If it is excessive, the result is manipulation — the intellect is turned toward trickery and exploitation of others. This excess, incidentally, tends to be an essential qualification for becoming a CEO: that surplus of قُوَّةُ الْعِلْمِ directed toward manipulation.

شَجَاعَة arises when the irascible soul — one's غَضَبٌ — is under proper control. It, too, is a mean between two extremes: تَهَوُّر on one side, which is impetuousness, irrational behavior, total loss of self-control in which a person acts in rage and then regrets it; and جُبْن on the other side, which is cowardice — insufficient غَضَبٌ, too much fear to act. One extreme is deficiency (نَقْص), the other excess (زِيَادَة). The بَلَاءٌnced state between them is شَجَاعَة. This connects to the hadith: "خَيْرُ الْأُمُورِ أَوْسَطُهَا," the best of matters is the middle course, and to the Quranic description: "وَكَذَلِكَ جَعَلْنَكُمْ أُمَّةًۭ وَسَطًۭا" — We made you a middle community, a people of حِكْمَةٌ, a people of شَجَاعَة, possessing these virtues in their proper بَلَاءٌnce.

عِفَّة is the middle way of قُوَّةُ الشَّهْوَةِ. It lies between جُمُودٌ — a kind of rigidity or indifference, where a person has no appetite, no energy, no interest in the opposite sex, no engagement with their شَهْوَة — and فِسْق, where a person plunges entirely into indulging their appetites and becomes enslaved to the appetitive soul. جُمُودٌ is itself unhealthy in the شَرِيعَة; the Prophet ﷺ disapproved of it. The excess, فِسْق, is the opposite danger. The بَلَاءٌnce between them is عِفَّة, and عِفَّة is explicitly affirmed in the شَرِيعَة. Allah ﷻ says that ignorant people, observing poor Muslims who carry themselves with this quality, assume they are wealthy — "يَحْسَبُهُمُ ٱلْجَاهِلُ أَغْنِيَآءَ مِنَ ٱلتَّعَفُّفِ" — because their dignity and self-restraint make them appear self-sufficient. They are not out scrambling after money through trickery and deception, because their inner state is بَلَاءٌnced.

As for عَدْل — justice — it does not occupy a mean between two things. It has an opposite: ظُلْم. Justice is simply the opposite of oppression. When the other three powers are in بَلَاءٌnce, you are just. When they are out of بَلَاءٌnce, you become a ظَالِم — oppressing both yourself and others. If you completely indulge the appetitive soul, you have become ظَالِمٌ لِنَفْسِهِ, oppressing your own soul by subjecting it to what it was not created to do. Allah says: كُلُواْ وَٱشۡرَبُواْ وَلَا تُسۡرِفُوٓاْ — "Eat and drink, but do not be excessive." The middle. Eat what you need. It is now, in fact, a science: based on your body size, you can calculate almost to the gram how many calories you require each day. Eating significantly more or less than that is a form of ظُلْم.

And Allah, in His mercy, has placed a natural indicator within the human being: when you are in a state of فِطْرَة and health, you will feel satiated at the right point. To go beyond that point is ظُلْم against your own soul, because your stomach has a right — a حَقٌّ. The Prophet ﷺ said: "خَيْرُ وِعَاءٍ مَلَأَهُ ابْنُ آدَمَ، بَعْدَهُ" — the worst vessel the son of Adam fills is his stomach. The حَقٌّ of the stomach is that a person leave one-third for food, one-third for water, and one-third for air. That is the عَدَالَة of eating. To exceed that is to go beyond its proper limit.

The same principle applies to the sexual appetite. If it is expressed naturally and within its proper limits, the human being remains healthy. If he transgresses those limits and goes beyond them — and this is part of what might be called the Viagra culture of our time — the result is excess. As a person grows older, that dimension of the self naturally diminishes, and the soul should be turning toward the آخِرَة. But for someone who does not hold the آخِرَة as a concern, he continues pursuing شَهَوَات that he should be moving away from. What was once a physical شَهْوَة has now become a psychological شَهْوَة — the body no longer produces it, but the mind insists on it. That is the imبَلَاءٌnce.

These, then, are the elements of a بَلَاءٌnced human being.

By the grace of Allah, Imam الْغَزَالِيّ says, the only human being who ever perfected the بَلَاءٌnce of all four was the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. He alone achieved complete equilibrium in all three of the foundational powers, which resulted in his being the most just of all men. His character is essentially عَدَالَة. His very nature is عَدْل. This is why the more you emulate him in his behavior, the closer you come to being a truly just human being.

For this reason, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ did not fast every day — that would have been a deficiency in عِفَّة, a withholding from himself of what was due. Nor did he go to the other extreme. He said: "بَعْضَ أَيَّامٍ أَصُومُ وَبَعْضَهَا أَفْطُرُ" — some days I fast and some days I eat. That is the بَلَاءٌnce.

The Prophet ﷺ did not become angry when it was inappropriate. He became angry when it was appropriate, and that was his شَجَاعَة. He became angry when a situation demanded a مَوْقِف, a stand. Imam الْغَزَالِيّ says that the عَدْل of شَجَاعَة is to be angry at the right time, in the right place, toward the right person, and with the right measure. How does one learn to do that? By studying the Messenger of Allah ﷺ — because he never let his anger get the better of him to the point of praying against people. He never allowed it to lead him to strike anyone unjustly. He controlled it. And that is شَجَاعَة.

The Prophet ﷺ also possessed عِلْم — knowledge — beyond any human being, and it was perfectly بَلَاءٌnced knowledge. There is nothing foolish in his conduct, nothing imprudent, nothing that suggests any deficiency of wisdom. Nor did he deploy his intellectual gifts for cleverness, trickery, or accumulation of دُنْيَا. He used them for the sake of Allah, where and as it was appropriate.

Now, one of the things Imam al-Suyuti says in one of his books — and he notes, "I have not seen anyone make this observation before me" — is that, reflecting on what had been said about Abu Bakr being the most courageous of the أُمَّة after the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, he began to think about that quality. This reflection led him to give a talk he called "Abu Bakr and Courage," exploring this virtue. But then something else struck him: each of the الْخُلَفَاءُ الرَّاشِدُونَ embodies one of these four virtues as his dominant characteristic.

حِكْمَةٌ is most clearly سَيِّدِنَا 'Ali, because he is بَابُ الْعِلْمِ — the gate of knowledge. شَجَاعَة is Abu Bakr. And everyone knows عُمَر as the embodiment of عَدْل. The only one that might give one pause is سَيِّدِنَا 'Uthman — but when you look up the word عِفَّة in al-Fayruzabadi's قَامُوس, immediately after its definition he writes: "وَالْعَفَافُ هُوَ الْعَفِيفُ مُغَالَاةً" — and الْعَفَنُ is the one who is intensely عَفِيف. And his name is عُثْمَانُ بْنُ عَفَّانَ. That was what he was known for. Consider what he was doing on the day he was killed: fasting. His entire state was عِفَّة — mastery over the نَفْس. Even the angels were shy in his presence, because of his profound عِفَّة.

So each one of them embodies one of these four virtues as his most prominent quality — while possessing all four. 'Ali had شَجَاعَة; 'Ali had عَدْل; 'Ali had حِكْمَةٌ; 'Ali had عِفَّة. But his dominant characteristic was حِكْمَةٌ — that was his gift. Abu Bakr had عِلْم; he had شُجَّاعَة; he had عِفَّة; he had عَدْل. But what stands out in his mawaqif is شَجَاعَة, because courage is defined precisely as ثَبَتَ — remaining steadfast when hearts around you waver. Look across the events of the Seerah, and it was Abu Bakr who remained ثَبَتَ when everyone else wavered. That is why the Prophet ﷺ said: "I presented Islam to everyone, and the only one who did not waver was Abu Bakr." His heart was شَجَاعَة. It was his nature.

Now there is another remarkable observation about all of this. The صِرَاطُ الْمُسْتَقِيمِ — the straight path — literally means the upright path. اِسْتِقَامَةٌ is اِعْتِدَال. In the prayer, your prayer is only valid when you are مُعْتَدِل — and مُعْتَدِل in Arabic means both upright and perfectly بَلَاءٌnced. So the صِرَاطُ الْمُسْتَقِيمِ is the perfectly بَلَاءٌnced path. And there are two ways to deviate from it.

You either deviate through deficiency or excess — those are the only two ways to stray from the path. You are either falling short of what is obligatory, or you are going too far.

These two deviations correspond to the Jews and the Christians. The Christians went to excess. They went beyond what they were commanded.

<!-- page 122 -->

رَهْبَانِيَّةٌ — monastic renunciation — they invented it; We did not prescribe it for them. They sought it as a means of drawing closer to Allah's pleasure, yet they could not even fulfill what they themselves had initiated. That is the very nature of excess: it cannot be sustained. By its own definition, it is unsustainable. That is why excess is so dangerous. The Prophet ﷺ warned: *إِيَّاكُمْ وَالْغُلُوَّ* — beware of extremism — for Allah does not grow weary until you grow weary. When you push beyond the بَلَاءٌnce, you exhaust yourself. It is not sustainable.

So the Christian, archetypally speaking, pursues an extreme of *عِفَّة* — continence — that tips over into something other than its opposite, but something distorted: an excess that has lost its equilibrium. And what always follows an extreme? The pendulum swings to the other side. Drive something far enough in one direction and it snaps back to its polar opposite. The Christian ascetic tradition pushed all the way to one extreme of renunciation, and then flipped entirely — landing in modern American society, where the culture is all *شَهْوَة*, all appetite and indulgence. They arrived on these shores as Puritans. That was the original impulse of the Pilgrims: to escape a European society they saw as given over to *شَهْوَة*, to build a pure religious community. And they ended up producing the opposite — a civilization defined by extreme appetite. This is precisely what happens with these polarities.

You can see the same dynamic with *شُجَاعٌ* — courage. The Christian ideal says: turn the other cheek. Yet, historically, Christians have killed more human beings than any other religious or secular civilization. Now they possess weapons of mass destruction. The technologies of industrial warfare — rockets, tanks, missiles — emerged largely from this same civilization. This is the fruit of going to extremes: the ideal inverts into its opposite.

The same pattern holds with justice. Push justice to an extreme and it collapses into manipulation. The law becomes a vehicle for trickery — lawyers finding every loophole so that those with resources get whatever they want. Justice becomes its opposite: oppression. Knowledge, which was originally claimed as the servant of human flourishing — read the original Enlightenment thinkers; Bacon wrote explicitly about how Christian morality would keep technology in check and make it a force for good — that knowledge becomes a tool for getting what one wants at others' expense. This is the Roman disease: justice for us, oppression for everyone else.

<!-- page 123 -->

That is one form of imبَلَاءٌnce. The other is the way of the Jews — the way of deficiency. This is the very complaint Moses, peace be upon him, raised with the Prophet ﷺ on the night of the *إِسْرَاء*: his people could not even manage two prayers a day. That was all they were asked, and they could not do it. They said to Moses: *You and your Lord go and fight — we will sit here.* So *نَقْص* — deficiency — runs through that entire mode.

Now, these are archetypes. I want to be clear: do not take these as absolute, rigid categories. They are lenses for understanding patterns in the human soul. Within the Jewish fold you will find the Christian phenomenon, and within the Christian fold you will find the Jewish phenomenon. These archetypes can appear in a Hindu, in a Buddhist, in anyone. They are ways of reading the human condition.

This is why the one described in *ٱلْمَغْضُوبِ عَلَيْهِمْ* — those who have incurred wrath — is the one who possesses knowledge but goes astray, becomes *ضَالّ*. And it is worth noting that the Arabic root *بَالٌ* also carries the meaning of falling in love — *بَلَاءٌ* means he fell in love — because it is precisely this excessive, consuming love that leads people astray, the love of Qays, of Majnun.

The Muslim is meant to stand in the middle between these two poles. We can think of those poles as *هَيْبَة* — awe, majesty, the quality of justice — which is the root of the Mosaic tradition. Moses, peace be upon him, is the lawgiver. His tradition is built on *عَدْل*, on justice. And on the other side is *رَحْمَةٌ* — mercy — which is the root impulse of the Christian tradition. The Christian understanding is that everyone is forgiven through God's mercy regardless of what they do, because all they perceive is mercy; they have lost sight of *هَيْبَة*. Archetypally, you will find Christians who become fixated on wrath — but the dominant Christian emphasis, if you look at what they teach, is *رَحْمَةٌ*.

*هَيْبَةٌ* and *رَحْمَةٌ* must be held in بَلَاءٌnce. You need both to walk the *صِرَاطُ الْمُسْتَقِيمِ*. You need *شَرِيعَةٌ* — the law — and you need *حَقِيقَةٌ* — the spirit of the law. The law is rules; the spirit of the law is *رَحْمَةٌ*. Allah sent the *شَرِيعَةٌ* as a mercy. *مَا أَرْسَلْنَاكَ إِلَّا رَحْمَةً لِلْعَالَمِينَ* — We sent you as nothing other than a mercy to all the worlds. What the Prophet ﷺ brought was *رَحْمَةٌ*. When inward and outward are بَلَاءٌnced — inwardly *إِيمَان*, outwardly *إِسْلَام* — they produce the *مُحْسِن*, the person of *إِحْسَان*, someone whose inner and outer states have become one integrated whole.

This framework is a genuine model for understanding what the straight path actually means. Muslims, according to Ibn Taymiyyah, *رَحِمَهُ اللَّهُ* — and this ultimately goes back to a hadith — deviate in one of two ways: they deviate like Jews or like Christians, toward *هَيْبَة* or toward *رَحْمَةٌ*. They either go in the direction of the hair-splitting, rabbinical, tyrannical scholar — the type the Prophet ﷺ warned against: *إِيَّاكُمْ وَجَبَرَاتِ الْعُلَمَاءِ*, beware of tyrannical scholars — or they go in the direction of the ignorant worshipper, embodied in the Christian model, or in the extreme of *تَصَوُّف*: a state where everything is one, everyone is going to heaven, nothing needs to be worried about, pure peace and harmony. When you hear certain people who claim to represent *تَصَوُّف* and they sound more like a New Age seminar than anything connected to Islam, that is this other extreme.

The بَلَاءٌnce is to be *صُوفِيٌّ فَقِيهِيٌّ* — inwardly animated by the spirit of *تَصَوُّف*, outwardly disciplined by *فِقْه* and *شَرِيعَةٌ*. This is why Imam الشَّافِعِيُّ said: do not be a *فَقِيهٌ* without being a Sufi, and do not be a Sufi without being a *فَقِيهٌ*, because one without the other either hardens the heart or leads one astray. The بَلَاءٌnce is the joining of *رَحْمَةٌ* — the spirit of *تَصَوُّف* — and *عَدْل* — the spirit of *شَرِيعَةٌ*.

Now, our tradition is actually closer to the Christian tradition in one respect: the Christians emphasize *رَحْمَةٌ*, and when Muslims deal with one another, *رَحْمَةٌ* should predominate over *هَيْبَة*. You should be more inclined to forgive your brother than to condemn him. Forgiveness is the higher station. *وَأَن تَعْفُوٓا۟ أَقْرَبُ لِلتَّقْوَىٰ* — that you pardon is closer to *تَقْوَى*. That is a loftier station in Allah's *دِينٌ* than demanding justice. This is why it has been said that where love truly exists, justice becomes unnecessary — because when the love between people is so profound, no one wants retribution; no one wants to call the other to account.

The first *فِتْنَةٌ* among the Muslims arose precisely from this departure: *عَدْل* overrode *رَحْمَةٌ*. سَيِّدِنَا مُعَاوِيَة, *رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ*, demanded justice. سَيِّدِنَا 'Ali, *رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ*, possessed *حِكْمَةٌ* — he counseled forgiveness, recognizing that insisting on strict justice at that moment would tear the *أُمَّة* apart. The Shia position aside, we, as *أَهْلُ السُّنَّةِ*, hold a good opinion of مُعَاوِيَة. We believe, as with عَائِشَة and زُبَيْر, that his intention was genuinely to seek justice. But سَيِّدِنَا 'Ali was reaching for the higher expression of Islam — *رَحْمَةٌ* — and that is why we consider him to have been given *تَوْفِيق* in his *اِجْتِهَاد*, while سَيِّدِنَا مُعَاوِيَة was not given *تَوْفِيق* in his. That is why his group is called *الْفِئَةُ الْبَاغِيَةُ* — the transgressing party. The danger of *عَدْل* taken to its extreme is precisely that it becomes *بَغْي*, oppression. They were unjust toward سَيِّدِنَا 'Ali, *رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ*. We must be careful when speaking of all of this — we are speaking of the *صَحَابَة*, *رَّضِىَ ٱللَّهُ عَنْهُمْ*, and we love them all, سَيِّدِنَا مُعَاوِيَة included. He was a scribe of revelation; he served Islam greatly. But he made a mistake. He was a human being. He was not infallible.

Now — any questions about that?

The question is about the distinction between wisdom and cleverness, between someone who appears wise and someone who genuinely is. For instance, there are people who are clever but whose cleverness does not lead them to recognize the truth. In that case, by the standard of *شَرِيعَةٌ*, they are considered *جَاهِل* — ignorant. They only appear wise.

Take the example we mentioned earlier: عُتْبَةُ بْنُ رَبِيعَةَ, who was renowned for his sound judgment, or أَبُو الْحَكَمِ — "the man of considered opinion" — as he was called before Islam. These were men recognized for their discernment. Even أَبُو حُذَيْفَة — when his father was killed at بَدْر and thrown into the pit, the Prophet ﷺ noticed him weeping and asked whether he was troubled by what had been done with his father. He said: *No. I am thinking of how intelligent my father was, how sound his counsel was when I was young, how I always hoped he would embrace Islam — and now I am looking at where he has ended up.* That is the example.

And I think those of us who came to Islam from outside — whose parents are not Muslim — can recognize this. My own parents have given me good counsel, and I can see in them a kind of wisdom. I can see that quality. For someone born into a Muslim family, with Muslim parents, that recognition may not come as naturally. But there are non-Muslims who genuinely possess forms of wisdom and insight. The difference is that the fully realized wisdom — بَلَاءٌnced and complete — sees everything true as true and everything false as false. The only person who achieved that absolutely was the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. But the closer one draws to him, the closer one comes to the kind of wisdom that allows one to see things as they truly are.

Does that make sense? Any other questions?

Right — and others have used these qualities too. The Hellenistic tradition used them. And even modern frameworks reflect them. *الْحِكْمَةُ ضَالَّةُ الْمُؤْمِنِ* — wisdom is the lost property of the believer; wherever he finds it, he takes it. And you can see these qualities throughout the *دِينٌ*. They are called cardinal virtues because all other virtues flow from them. Every virtue can be traced back to one of these four.

Let me give an example. Take generosity. What is generosity, really? What is it that a generous person can do that others cannot? Why can't other people simply give? Because they are afraid. They fear losing what they have. So the truly generous person is, at root, a courageous person. It is *شَجَاعَة* — courage — that enables giving, where others are held back by fear. That is why أَبُو بَكْرٍ was the most generous of all the Companions: when the Prophet ﷺ asked what people had brought, أَبُو بَكْرٍ brought everything he had. عُمَر brought half. أَبُو بَكْرٍ's generosity flowed from his *شَجَاعَة*, his magnanimity.

Take another virtue. These four are called the *أُمَّهَات* — the matrices — of virtue. All virtues emerge from these four. Take mercy. What is mercy? Where does it really reside among the four? Mercy ultimately belongs to *حِكْمَةٌ*, wisdom. Because genuine compassion — like أَبُو بَكْرٍ's counsel to the Prophet ﷺ to show mercy to the people — is grounded in the wisdom that Allah gives through mercy what He does not give through wrath. There is a *حِكْمَةٌ* in showing mercy, a knowledge that mercy is itself the more effective and more elevated path. Work through any virtue and you will find it rooted in one of these four.

Imam al-Ghazālī goes into extraordinary depth in the *إِحْيَاء* demonstrating how all the virtues derive from these four matrices. The greatest minds in human history have exhausted themselves on this question — and he truly exhausted it.

<!-- page 127 -->

So how does all of this relate to *غَضَب* — anger? *غَضَب*, because it is one of these core *قُوَّاتٌ* — faculties — is rooted in something essential to human nature. If you look at the brain as it has been studied, what they call the triune brain — and this is not purely a modern observation; Imam al-Ghazālī touches on something similar — there are three dominant centers. The lowest, sometimes called the R-complex or reptilian brain in evolutionary frameworks, is the appetitive center: the seat of drives and urges — hunger, sexuality, the basic impulses. This would correspond to the appetitive faculty. The midbrain is where emotions are centered, and in this model the root emotion is *غَضَب* — anger. This is why the first emotions children display are forms of *غَضَب*: they get angry, they cry, they throw tantrums as they grow older. They learn *no* before they learn *yes*. You can see *غَضَب* rooted right there in the earliest stages of human development.

Then there is the higher brain — the cortex — which is where reason resides.

There is a beautiful hadith in Imam al-Tirmidhī in which the Prophet ﷺ said: love the one you love up to a measure, for one day he may become someone you dislike. The wisdom here is this: if you have shared every secret with someone and that person later turns against you — a very human experience, and one that devastates people — you are left completely exposed.

And also don't hate people to extremes, because one day those you hate might become people you love. So be بَلَاءٌnced in both love and hate. This, again, points to the importance of emotional equilibrium.

When emotions fall out of بَلَاءٌnce, problems follow. One of the Arabs put it memorably: don't be so sweet that people eat you alive, and don't be so bitter that no one can stand you. Be بَلَاءٌnced — that is the idea.

<!-- page 135 -->

Finally, what researchers call the new brain is the neocortex — the seat of rational thought. When all three systems function properly together, the human being is truly Adam, truly human. And this is part of the human condition: some people are excessively cerebral, living entirely in their heads, where everything is theoretical and nothing practical. Others live at the opposite extreme, like reptiles — purely reactive, with no trace of higher humanity. I once explained this in a prison, and some of the Muslim inmates responded to it powerfully. One man came up to me afterward and said, "You know that thing about the reptilian brain? There are a lot of reptiles here. These guys are like snakes, just waiting to strike." It is a striking image — people locked in a purely reactionary mode, with no humanity present. And then, of course, there are those who are overwhelmed by emotion, most commonly by anger.

I brought all of that in to put what follows in perspective. Anger is one of the great excesses of the human condition, which is why the author calls it the ocean — it really is the big one. And there are essentially three reasons people get angry.

The first relates to primal needs: food, shelter, survival, and the like. If a person does not get angry when these are threatened, he is considered *أَحْمَق* — foolish — because this is a matter of survival. If someone threatens your life, you must respond. If someone threatens your family, you must respond. That is a courage Allah loves. Allah loves *شَجَاعَة* — بَلْ قَالُوا هُوَ شَجَاعٌ — and the Prophet ﷺ said, *مَنْ مَاتَ دُونَ مَالِهِ مَاتَ شَهِيدًا*: whoever dies defending his wealth dies a martyr. That courage, that *غَيْبَة*, reflects the principle of *الْأَمْرُ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ وَالنَّهْيُ عَنِ الْمُنْكَرِ* at its most fundamental level, and if you die doing it, you died in jihad — because *الْأَمْرُ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ وَالنَّهْيُ عَنِ الْمُنْكَرِ* falls within the chapter of jihad. It is a type of jihad.

The second type is also common to human beings, but it relates to position — to what the scholars call رُؤْيَةُ النَّفْسِ, seeing oneself. This is the anger of dignity: *I am a human being; you cannot treat me this way.* It arises from a sense of being belittled, being treated with disdain, being made to feel lesser than one deserves. Sometimes that sense is justified; sometimes it is not. Some people harbor a complete delusion about their own standing — a grandiosity of soul — and their anger flows from that distortion.

The third type is specific to certain individuals and their particular attachments. A scholar, for instance, will become angry if he sees a book mistreated. An illiterate farmer will not — he doesn't grasp what the fuss is about. But that same farmer will become furious if you throw his pitchfork carelessly, because it is his tool. So this third category of anger is tied to what a person values, what is particular to them.

Imam الْغَزَالِيّ says the first type is healthy, unless taken to an extreme where a person begins to oppress others — taking their food, seizing their shelter. That becomes wrongdoing. The second type can be healthy up to a point, but the higher path is to cease seeing oneself so centrally and not to let these things provoke anger at all. The danger lies in the opposite extreme, which is called ذَكَاة — a disease he will address separately — where excessive self-effacement tips into humiliation. The Prophet ﷺ said, *الْمُؤْمِنُ لَا يُذِلُّ نَفْسَهُ*: the believer does not humiliate himself. And Allah says of the believers that they possess عِزَّة before the disbelievers — وَلِلَّهِ ٱلْعِزَّةُ وَلِرَسُولِهِۦ وَلِلْمُؤْمِنِينَ — and رُحَمَآءُ بَيْنَهُمْ. You do not present yourself before a non-Muslim in a posture of meek, Uriah Heep–style obsequiousness. You carry yourself with عِزَّة. It is with fellow believers that you practice softness and yielding.

And according to Sidi Ahmad Zarruq, if a non-Muslim says something to you that does not touch your دِين — not mocking your faith or your practice — you simply let it pass. وَإِذَا خَاطَبَهُمُ ٱلْجَهِلُونَ قَالُوا۟ سَلَمًۭا: when the ignorant address them, they say "peace." Don't get angry over things that bring no benefit. All they are doing is pushing buttons. This is why Allah says: فَاصْبِرْ عَلَىٰ مَا يَقُولُونَ وَاهْجُرْهُمْ هَجْرًا جَمِيلًا — be patient with what they say and leave them gracefully. Turn away. Don't let it consume you. Let it go. There is no benefit in it — none at all.

The fourth type involves غَيْرَهُ. When the Prophet ﷺ spoke about this, Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ declared his own jealous protectiveness, and the Prophet ﷺ responded that he himself has more غَيْرَهُ than Sa'd, and that Allah has more غَيْرَهُ than he does. In other words, غَيْرَهُ — often translated as jealousy, though it is better understood as a protective ardor for what is one's own — is a good sign. It is the impulse that moves a Muslim when he sees his religion being threatened: he becomes غَيُور for the دِين; he wants to defend it. That is a positive anger. And Allah has made غَيْرَهُ part of human nature in order to protect lineage — النَّسَبُ. Without it, men would lose their protective care for their families, and society would unravel through adultery and confusion of bloodlines. غَيْرَة in a man toward his wife is healthy and natural, as long as it does not tip into oppression or groundless suspicion. And the غَيْرَهُ a woman feels is equally natural — Allah has made it so, and indeed it is permitted. Very few women are entirely free from it, so it is not considered a fault.

Now the author says this disease has two cures. The first prevents anger from arising in the first place. The second suppresses it once it has come. Allah praises in the Quran وَٱلْكَظِمِينَ ٱلْغَيْظَ وَٱلْعَافِينَ عَنِ ٱلنَّاسِ — those who restrain their anger and pardon people. The famous story of Ja'far al-Tayyar illustrates this: when a servant accidentally spilled water on him and he became angry, the servant immediately recited that verse, and Ja'far said, "I have suppressed it." The servant continued with the next part — *وَٱلْعَافِينَ عَنِ ٱلنَّاسِ* — and Ja'far said, "I forgive you." Then the servant recited, وَٱللَّهُ يُحِبُّ ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ, and Ja'far said, "You are free, for the sake of Allah." That is what a believer should be: someone who, on hearing the Quran, responds to it immediately.

Similarly, if someone is engaged in backbiting and another reminds him with آيَةٌ, he should stop at once and not resent the reminder. I was once told a story about Sheikh Ibn al-Habib, a Moroccan sheikh, and one of his most remarkable qualities: he never spoke ill of anyone. This was reported by people who had lived with him for over seventy years, seeing him every single day — and not once in all that time did they hear him say anything bad about a person. Ever.

Not long ago, someone wrote me a letter — a man I believe to have genuine تَقْوَى — saying he had mentioned me in a letter to someone else and feared there may have been some غِيبَة in it. So he sent me the original letter and asked me to read it and tell him if I found any غِيبَة, so that he could seek forgiveness. He was questioning something he'd written. As it happened, it was simply a disagreement between his understanding and mine, which caused me no difficulty at all. But that level of scrupulousness — even when it is not strictly required — reflects something called اِسْتِهْلَال. Strictly speaking in the شَرِيعَة, you don't have to go back to the person you spoke about and confess it; in fact it is often better not to, because many people will simply become angry at you. Others, though, won't mind — it depends entirely on the person.

The Prophet ﷺ gave us another way. He said, *أَيَعْجِزُ أَحَدُكُمْ أَنْ يَكُونَ مِثْلَ أَبِي ضَمْضَمٍ* — is any of you unable to be like Abu Damdam? Every morning Abu Damdam would give away his honor as صَدَقَةٌ for people: "يَا اللَّه, if anyone has spoken ill of me, I give back to them whatever good deeds of theirs may have come to me as a result." So the Prophet said: just do that. Who cares, in the end? If you truly contemplate the grave, the questioning in the grave, the Day of Judgment — if you really sit with those realities — none of this will matter. Once you die, you could not care less that someone once remarked you get angry too easily, or said something that stung you one afternoon. It will all be completely irrelevant.

So the author presents two remedies. Then he says: *فَاذْكُرْ لِتَزْدَادَنَّ بِهَا عَلَى رَاهِرِ كَثْرَةَ مَدْحِ الْحِلْمِ وَالتَّوَاضُعِ وَالصَّبْرِ* — remember, in order to adorn yourself with the quality that removes anger, reflect on how greatly حِلْم and تَوَاضُعٌ are praised. Because a truly humble person does not get angry for himself. He may become angry for Allah's sake, but not for his own sake — because the only reason people get angry is that they are seeing themselves. If the self is not there to be seen, what is there to get angry over?

Consider the Prophet ﷺ: when the Quraysh called him *مُذَمَّمٌ* — the opposite of his name, the condemned one — he simply said, "It is remarkable how Allah turned the tongues of Quraysh, who intended to condemn me, against some man named مُذَمَّمٌ. But I am مُحَمَّدٌ." He did not get angry. He just observed: they are not talking about me; they are talking about مُذَمَّمٌ. That is the art of letting go. When there is no self to defend, there is no anger. That is تَوَاضُعٌ.

What the author is saying is this: to acquire this quality, meditate on how universally humility and forbearance are praised. Every culture has honored these virtues in its traditions. This one largely no longer does — though it once did. There are still people who value them, but a certain cultural shift introduced what might be called the ethos of chutzpah — an overweening pride, a "if you've got it, flaunt it" sensibility — which is in fact alien to traditional American culture. Historically, Americans did appreciate humility. And humility is praised by all the sages, across every religious tradition.

The Prophet ﷺ said: whoever humbles himself for Allah's sake, Allah raises him up; and whoever raises himself up, Allah brings him low.
