And the Prophet's camel, القَصْوَاء, used to outrun every camel among the Arabs, and the Companions took great pride in this. But one day she lost a race to a Bedouin, and they were all dismayed. The Prophet ﷺ laughed and said, "Don't you know that nothing is ever elevated but that Allah must bring it back down?" That is the سُنَّة of Allah in His creation. If you understand the سُنَّة, you can laugh at things like that rather than be troubled by them.

So the author says: if you truly reflect on how praiseworthy حِلْم, تَوَاضُعٌ, and forbearance are — and how the Prophet ﷺ was the most forbearing of all people, forgiving those who wronged him, never given to anger, patient through everything — and if you consider that this praise is found in the شَرِيعَة, in شِعَار, in the sayings of all the sages, and that every prophet without exception was characterized by حِلْم and تَوَاضُعٌ — none of them were known for arrogance or quick temper — then you are on your way.

And then he said the second remedy — suppressing anger when it comes — lies in recognizing that nothing happens except by Allah's will. When something arises that provokes you, take a breath and say *لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ*. This is a بَلَاءٌ from Allah, a trial, to see *أَيُّكُمْ أَحْسَنُ عَمَلًۭا* — which of you is finest in deed. The Prophet ﷺ said: *لَيْسَ الشَّدِيدُ الَّذِي يَصْرَعُ، وَإِنَّمَا الشَّدِيدُ مَنْ يَغْلِبُ نَفْسَهُ عِنْدَ الْغَضَبِ* — the strong man is not the one who can wrestle others to the ground; the truly strong man is the one who overcomes himself when anger comes. That is the شَدِيد. That is real strength.

<!-- page 142 -->

Imam الْغَزَالِيّ also observes that some people, in their foolishness, equate explosive anger with virility and courage. They see macho posturing as a virtue of character. You will even find a man who brags about his criminal exploits — how he robbed someone, fought them, dominated them — as though this were bravery. It is not. There is nothing virtuous in it whatsoever.

As for the practical means of suppressing anger, the hadith gives a sequence: if you are standing, sit down; if you are sitting, recline; if neither helps, go and make وُضُوءٌ; and if that is still not enough, begin to pray. Performing وُضُوءٌ actually dissipates the heat of anger — for anger is fire, and anger comes from شَيْطَان. It is the quality of شَيْطَان. One day the Prophet ﷺ observed a man whose face had gone red and whose veins were bulging with rage, and he said, "سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ — do you not see how, when one of you becomes angry, his face begins to resemble شَيْطَان?" And he said that had the man uttered the remembrance of Allah, it would have been lifted from him entirely.

And I was once in a conflict-resolution situation, and it was really interesting — things started getting heated and both parties stood up at the same time. They had been sitting on couches, and I had to make them sit back down. But that is the nature of anger: you start rising, and the way to overcome it is to sit down.

If you are in a room and you begin to feel anger, leave the room. If someone is making you angry, just leave. Go to another place, relax, sit down, and let your anger subside — then you can return. This is a practice that, if you actually do it, works. If you do not, you will either end up in a physical confrontation — and if the other person is bigger than you, he beats you, and if he is smaller, everyone sees you as a bully. So the best thing is simply to avoid it. There is no benefit in it, none whatsoever. It is empty and meaningless, and it is simply best to walk away.

That is the سُنَّة: cold water, silence, sitting down, and وُضُوء. All of these came in the سُنَّة. Imam al-Razi mentioned that one man with a fierce temper actually hired someone to insult him and paid him to do so, just so he could practice listening without reacting until he could master his self-control. That may sound extreme, but the point is that people took these matters very seriously, because anger is a terrible quality and no one wants to be possessed by it.

<!-- page 106 -->

There is a hadith in which the Prophet ﷺ said that anger will even afflict the best of this أُمَّة. No one illustrates this better than سَيِّدِنَا عُمَر. He was a man of fierce anger — yet if you trace his growth as a human being and as a Muslim, by the end of his life عُمَر was not someone whose anger ever got the better of him. In fact, it was quite the opposite: he became known for his forgiveness. You can see this clearly in his later years. Even when he was mortally stabbed, the first thing he asked was whether his assailant was a Muslim. When he was told no, he praised Allah that no Muslim had done this to him — he simply did not want that on a Muslim's account. He said *الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ*, and that was his nature. For all his ferocity — he was truly a lion of Allah — his anger never consumed him.

On one occasion the Prophet ﷺ was sitting with some women of the Quraysh in Madinah — this was before the verse of hijab was revealed — and when عُمَر entered, they all immediately fled into another room and hid behind a curtain. The Prophet ﷺ was laughing when عُمَر came in. عُمَر asked, *مَا أَضْحَكَكَ يَا رَسُولَ اللهِ* — "What makes you laugh, O Messenger of Allah?" The Prophet ﷺ said he was laughing at these Qurayshi women, who had fled the moment they saw him. عُمَر went over to the curtain and called them back. He asked them: "How is it that you flee from me, yet you are not shy before the Messenger of Allah, who is far more worthy of your awe?" They replied, "Because you are harsher and sterner than he is — he does not get angry." At this the Prophet ﷺ laughed and said, *يَا عُمَرُ، لَوْ سَرَكْتَ فِي جَادٍّ لَسَرَخَ الشَّيْطَانُ فِي جَادٍّ آخَرَ* — "O عُمَر, if you went down one path, شَيْطَان would take another path entirely."

That example shows what it means to master the quality of anger. He truly mastered it. Yet at the same time, his anger was rooted in *غَيْرَهُ* and in *عَدْل* — in righteous indignation and the demand for justice. He became angry at wrongdoing because he wanted to right it. Even his coming to the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and accepting Islam was driven by that impulse — he felt something was wrong and he wanted to correct it. Everything in him was rooted in his convictions. He had an extraordinarily powerful sense of justice, and the Khattab family were traditionally known for that quality, even in pre-Islamic Arab culture.

<!-- page 107 -->

Now let me address the question that came in. As for children under seven, one must be very careful about expressing anger toward them. For children over seven, however, discipline is necessary — they have to be disciplined. And here it is worth noting something relevant to the three souls and the question about a teacher needing to establish discipline. The classical scholars described three stages corresponding to the first three sets of seven years. The first seven years are governed by the appetitive soul — which is why young children are constantly eating, constantly wanting things; it is all desire and the fulfillment of appetite. The second seven years are governed by *غَضَبٌ*, and the third by the rational faculty, by *عِلْم*. This is precisely why the hadith says *لَاعِبُوهُمْ سَبْعًا* — play with them for seven years — because play is the fulfillment of the appetitive soul. The Prophet ﷺ noted that there is no *لَعِبٌ* — no play — in Islam except in certain contexts, one of which is with one's spouse. That is a wholesome and praiseworthy kind of play: *مُدَاعَبَة*, the playful affection between husband and wife, and the Prophet ﷺ himself engaged in this with his wives. So the hadith instructs us: play with your children for seven years — during this period of the appetitive soul, allow them to explore and indulge their nature.

It is part of what children are meant to do. They are discovering the world, and that discovery is entirely sensory — they have come from a spiritual realm into the realm of sensation, and so they must inhabit their bodies and experience them. This is why things that are quite traumatic often happen to children in those first seven years: they get burnt, they fall from high places. All of this is the process of learning to live inside a body and discovering that the body has limits. It is a حِكْمَةٌ of Allah that these things happen to children, because they literally teach the lesson that the body is bounded — unlike the رُوحٌ, which is a more limitless kind of entity. The body is limited, and the Sharia is a protection for the body: touch fire and you learn to stay away from it; stay away from heights; do not endanger yourself. So you allow children to discover the world. Those first seven years really are a time of discovery, and that is why the word هُدًى, whose root means to lust — رَلُمَ, to lust — captures it well. It is a lusty experience: children are very sensuous, they love to be touched, and that is part of how they develop and discover themselves.

Then comes the second stage. He said: *أَدِّبُوهُمْ لِسَبْعٍ* — discipline them for seven years, because now their حَبَبٌ needs to be disciplined. They are moving into a new stage and require firm guidance. Discipline here means that when you see them becoming angry, you do not allow it. You do not let children rage at their parents — that is a bad sign. You have to teach them to control their anger. And if you yourself cannot control your anger, it will be a disaster: you cannot give what you do not have. If children see you losing your temper constantly, they will simply imitate what they see, and then you will wonder where they got that temper. It came from you, because you are their model. So the أَدَب of those second seven years is precisely this.

Then he said: *أُصَاحِبُهُمْ لِسَبْعٍ* — befriend them for the next seven years, because this last stage is the stage of عِلْم. They are now moving into a phase where they need your guidance, your counsel — your شَوَرَ — because you will help direct their education, their vocation, and the shape of their lives. That is the traditional model.

Any questions? — It is worth noting that the word for عِلْم: what is its root in Arabic? You know the answer, but what about the word أَخْلَمَ? When they said *صَفَّاهُ أَخْلَمَانَا* — the intellect — forbearance is actually a sign of intellect. Why? Because you have your قُوَّةُ الْغَضَبِيَّةِ under control; it is directed by the rational soul. Your عَقْل is working, because the عَقْل is an عِكَالٌ — *يَقِيلُهُ* — it is what puts the irascible and the concupiscent soul in check. عَقْل means to prevent, to restrain, and that is precisely what the rational mind is supposed to do: keep the غَضَبٌ and the شَهْوَة in check. If it fails to do that, you are not عَاقِل, you are not حَلِيمٌ — you are, as they say, a حِمَارٌ. If someone becomes truly enraged, people say he is like a donkey: he is acting like a beast, not like a human being. And usually he is a حِمَارٌ because his anger has taken over.

But the crucial distinction is تَحَلُّم — and this is a very good point that was just raised, and I am genuinely glad it came up because I had forgotten to mention it. There is a hadith that is important here, because some of us will say: "My God, I cannot do any of this — it is all too hard. I am a mess. I get angry. I overindulge." The hadith says: *إِنَّمَا الْعِلْمُ بِالتَّعَلُّمِ* — knowledge is acquired; you are not born an عَلِيمٌ, you become one. How? Through effort, through مُجَاهَدَة. The عَلِيمٌ is the one who struggles to learn: he rises at night, he studies, he reads, he attends lessons, he listens, he takes notes, he reviews. That is مُجَاهَدَة. You were not born with that knowledge; it is something you acquire. حِلْم is exactly the same. The hadith says: *وَإِنَّمَا الْحِلْمُ بِالتَّحَلُّمِ* — forbearance too is acquired. And what happens when someone becomes a true عَلِيمٌ? When you encounter such a person, you do not see the student he once was. It seems as though he always had that knowledge — you did not witness all those years of study and struggle. The same is true of the حَلِيمٌ: you do not see when he was wrestling with himself; you assume he was simply born that way. But it is something acquired, something that took real effort. تَأَلُّم comes from تَفَرُّد, which is تَكَلُّدٌ — you have to work for it, and it is hard.

Now, here is a very important point. Imam الْغَزَالِيّ uses the metaphor of a calligrapher. He asks: what do you do when your handwriting is poor? And this is the metaphor for dealing with anger. You go to someone with an excellent hand, you watch him, you ask him to form the letters for you, you go home and you practice those letters again and again. At first they will be poor — but after a month or two, you begin to notice improvement. It is not mastery, it is far from it, but it is noticeably better. And someone who has never started will look at your work and say, "You have beautiful handwriting," while the master will look at the same page and say, "This is terrible" — because *حَسَنَاتُ الْأَبْرَارِ سَيِّئَاتُ الْمُقَرَّبِينَ*: you are advancing through ranks, and the standard rises with every rank. When you reach mastery, what happens? I once watched مُحَمَّدٌ Zakariyya — I took a calligraphy lesson from him, a single lesson — and I watched him work, and I thought: "I can do that. That looks easy." Why did I think that? Because he is a master. He is an American master of calligraphy who wins awards in Turkey. But I did not see the years of nights he had put into it. Now he just flows across the paper as if he were born writing perfect calligraphy. Then I picked up the pen and started, and it was like watching something jump up and down — it was very hard. He simply flowed. That is mastery.

So what do you do when you want good character? Three things. First, you learn what it is. You have to learn what bad character is and what good character is — and that is precisely what we are doing here in these sessions. The first stage is learning. Second: *وَٱلَّذِينَ جَهَدُوا۟ فِينَا لَنَهْدِيَنَّهُمْ سُبُلَنَا* — those who struggle for Our sake, We will guide them to Our paths. You have to find people of good character and spend time with them. There is also a benefit in being around people who get angry easily, because it allows you to see clearly how unpleasant it is. This is why Imam al-Shafi'i, when someone asked him how he had developed such excellent character, said: "I listened to what my detractors said about me, and then I looked into my own soul to see whether it was true. When it was, I struggled with myself until I removed it." Sayyidna 'Isa was once asked the same question — how did you develop such excellent character? — and what the tradition records is this: "I looked at the bad character of people and I avoided it." So part of it is simply مُجَاهَدَة. The third stage is to be with someone who has good character — and although that is genuinely difficult to find, we should never despair. At least we should be trying.

I will tell you this: it really works. I am serious. I am nowhere near where I would like to be, but it helped me enormously to learn these things and to struggle with myself as much as I could. You do change. You do improve. Anger — خَطَّاب — was a serious difficulty for me for a very long time, and it is much less so now. Part of that, I will admit, is simply aging. Imam Sayyidna 'Ali said that youth is a kind of madness, and the cure is to grow old. So there is that.

On anger toward parents — the principle is that you should be angry at the act, not at the actor. What disturbs you should be the action itself, but if it is your father or your mother, you must be especially careful. If you are in a position to act, then *الْأَمْرُ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ وَالنَّهْيُ عَنِ الْمُنْكَرِ* applies, provided it will not lead to a worse فِتْنَة. But with parents you must be very careful. A man came to the Prophet ﷺ and said: "My father takes my wealth and it really angers me." The Prophet ﷺ said: "You and your wealth belong to your father" — meaning that he was the instrument by which you were given existence, and that carries an immense weight. Parents hold a very high مَقَام in Islam. The best approach is to recognize that they are human beings with their limitations. I had a teacher once — not a Muslim, but a very wise person — who told me that he thanked his father because his father had taught him how not to be a father. His father had been so harsh with him that he did the precise opposite with his own children. In the end he genuinely felt that it was a gift. He was not being sarcastic — he meant it. Obviously it would be better if your father showed you how to be a father rather than how not to be one, but the idea is taking the worst situation and making the best of it. *وَمَن يُؤْتَ ٱلْحِكْمَةَ فَقَدْ أُوتِىَ خَيْرًۭا كَثِيرًۭا* — that is حِكْمَة. And that is the beauty of Islam: in any situation, it can be turned to benefit.

So, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, we are actually coming — believe it or not — to the end. There are only four sections remaining after today, and I think we will finish all the diseases of the heart next week and then move on. He has a few excellent sections ahead: one is called the cure for all the diseases of the heart together — a single, overarching remedy. The Gordian knot, you might say. Everyone knows about the Gordian knot: a massive knot in Turkey that no one could untie, and the legend was that whoever untied it would rule the land. When Alexander the Great arrived, they showed him the knot — and he drew his sword and cut straight through it. The diseases of the heart are like that, and sometimes the sword is the best answer. Then we will do the section on هَوَاجِسُ — thoughts — because one of the most obvious manifestations of the diseases of the heart is diseased thinking: impure thoughts that afflict the mind, but also thoughts that a person might believe are genuinely good yet are in fact from شَيْطَان. شَيْطَان is described in the hadith as being like a wolf among a flock: if he finds the shepherd watching from one side, he comes from another. One of the key differences between شَيْطَان's وَسْوَاس and the هَوًى of the نَفْس is that شَيْطَان's whisperings are compulsive, while the نَفْس has its own habitual pull. We know from Ramadan that the شَيَاطِين are locked up — *صُفِّدَتِ الشَّيَاطِينُ* — and yet people still struggle. That is where you begin to distinguish between شَيْطَان, the نَفْس, هَوًى, and دُنْيَا, because those are the four enemies of the human being: شَيْطَان, نَفْس, هَوًى, and دُنْيَا.

## Heedlessness

The next section is on غَفْلَة — *وَغَفْلَةٌ عَنْ رُوحِ الدُّنْيَا نَهْيًا وَنَهًى وَحُكْمٍ وَهَيْئَتِهَا* — and this is a truly important concept. Imam al-Junayd said that غَفْلَة is the root disease. And even though we have heard different candidates for the root disease proposed — عِبَادٌ, and then الْكِبْرُ, and then تَصْوِيفٌ, which is procrastination — procrastination is itself a form of غَفْلَة, so I think those scholars would likely agree. The reason you procrastinate is that you are not truly attending to the matter at hand; if you genuinely saw the urgency of it, you would act. So غَفْلَة is a serious illness of the heart.

He calls it غَفْلَة: inattentiveness, heedlessness, unawareness. The Arabic word for a simpleton — someone you can easily fool — is مُغَفَّل, a passive form: one who has been caught unaware. And so غَفْلَة, in his definition, is صَحْو. As you know, صَحْو is a vital concept in the prayer itself — the سُجُودُ السَّهْوِ. The very fact that سُجُودُ السَّهْوِ exists, and is an essential element of knowing how to pray, tells us something: the nature of the human being is forgetfulness. غَفْلَة is a kind of forgetting.

The word in Arabic for the human being — إِنْسَان — is revealing. Some say it derives from أُنْس, meaning intimacy, because the human being is a creature that needs companionship, that needs أُنْس; he is not وَشْيٌ, not a creature that can exist in isolation. Others say it comes from نِسْيَانٌ, which means to forget. If you examine the word إِنْسَان — and إِنْسَان applies to both male and female, though some linguists note that إِنْسَانَةٌ is a valid form, إِنْسَانَةٌ فِي تَنَاتُنْ, appearing in a line of Jahili poetry, though rare — the human being is إِنْسَان, male and female alike:

*الْإِنْسَانُ الْإِنْسَانُ الذَّكَرُ وَالْأُنْثَى*
*‏هَلْ أَتَىٰ عَلَى ٱلْإِنسَنِ حِينٌۭ مِّنَ ٱلدَّهْرِ لَمْ يَكُن شَيْـًۭٔا مَّذْكُورًا ‎*

So إِنْسَان is the human being. And in the Quran, when إِنْسَان is used, it sometimes refers to Adam, عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ, and the first slip of our father Adam was نِسْيَانٌ — he was caught unaware by شَيْطَان. That is why the human being must be vigilant; he must have عَاصِم, a guard. And so the disease of غَفْلَة is صَحْو — a forgetting — and *يَحْتَرِيَاتُ الْإِنْسَانِ مِنْ قِلَّةِ التَّحَفُّظِ وَالتَّيَقُّظِ*: it befalls the human being as a result of an absence of تَحَفُّظ — vigilance — and تَيَقُّظٌ — wakefulness.

When Sayyidna 'Ali, رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ, said: *النَّاسُ نِيَامٌ، فَإِذَا مَاتُوا انْتَبَهُوا* — "people are asleep, and when they die they wake up" — what he meant is that people are غَافِلٌ, sunk in heedlessness about their destiny: where they are going, and what they will be called to account for when they get there. Because if they truly knew, with real wakefulness — يَقَظَة — they could not do the things they do. That is why Imam al-Junayd said this is the core disease: if the human being truly knew what was coming, there is no way he could act as he does.

This idea is not entirely unlike the Socratic understanding, which belongs to a parallel tradition in the West — the idea that people do wrong because they are genuinely unaware; if they really knew what they were doing, they would not do it. The metaphor given in that tradition is the metaphor of the cave: human beings are like people sitting inside a cave with a lamp behind them, unable to turn around, watching shadows on the wall and taking those shadows for reality. Then one of them escapes, goes out into the real world, sees the sun and the stars and the trees, returns to the cave, and tells the others: "This is all an illusion — what we thought was real is not real; the real thing is somewhere else." And they kill him. That is the role of the prophets: they come and tell people, "You are all asleep — wake up, do you not realize?" So the concept of غَفْلَة is profound, and that is why Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى uses the word in many Quranic verses.

Another word the Quran uses is صَمَدٌ — and صَمَدٌ is similar to غَفْلَة, but there is a greater degree of awareness in it; the صَمَدٌ knows, in some sense, but the غَافِل does not. Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى says in سُورَةُ الْقَمَرِ: *‏لَّقَدْ كُنتَ فِى غَفْلَةٍۢ مِّنْ هَذَا فَكَشَفْنَا عَنكَ غِطَآءَكَ فَبَصَرُكَ ٱلْيَوْمَ حَدِيدٌۭ ‎* — "You were in heedlessness about this; We have removed the veil from you, and your sight today is sharp." There is a veil of غَفْلَة over the human being, screening him from reality. And Allah says in the Quran that there is a رِشَا — *وَعَلَىٰ قُلُوبِهِمْ أَكِنَّةٌ* — and this رِشْوَة prevents them from seeing things as they truly are. So the غَافِل is not seeing things as they truly are, while the يَقِظٌ — the one who is truly awake — sees things as they truly are.

The Prophet ﷺ prayed: *اللَّهُمَّ أَرِنِي الْأَشْيَاءَ كَمَا هِيَ* — "O Allah, show me things as they really are." And in another hadith: *اللَّهُمَّ أَرِنِي الْحَقَّ حَقًّا وَارْزُقْنِي اتِّبَاعَهُ، وَأَرِنِي الْبَاطِلَ بَاطِلًا وَارْزُقْنِي اجْتِنَابَهُ* — "O Allah, show me truth as truth and grant me the ability to follow it; and show me falsehood as falsehood and grant me the ability to avoid it." The one who is not غَافِل has precisely that state: they see truth as truth and falsehood as falsehood, they follow the truth and avoid the falsehood. And that is the مُتَّقٍ — that is him, the person of تَقْوَى: he does what Allah commanded and avoids what Allah prohibited.

So he is saying that غَفْلَة is *غَفْلُ الْمَرْءِ عَمَّا أَمَرَهُ اللَّهُ بِهِ وَنَهَاهُ عَنْهُ* — heedlessness about what Allah has commanded and what He has forbidden. That is why people do wrong: not out of pure malice in most cases, but out of heedlessness about what they have been commanded and what they have been prohibited from doing. And this is why, if you see a believer doing something wrong and you remind him, and he responds with *أَسْتَغْفِرُ اللَّهَ* — that is a good person, a مُتَّقٍ. You have brought him out of his غَفْلَة. The مُتَكَبِّر, by contrast — because كِبْرٌ is what follows after all that we have been discussing — when reminded of his غَفْلَة, will not accept the reminder. *أَخَذَتْهُ ٱلْعِزَّةُ بِٱلْإِثْمِ*: he takes refuge in his own pride and refuses. You say to him gently, and he gets angry at you: "Who do you think you are?" That is why the cure for غَفْلَة in a believer is good company — صُحْبَة. Be with people of صِدْق, of sincerity; be with people of تَقْوَى; be with truthful, sincere people who will tell you plainly when you are doing something wrong, and encourage you when you are doing something right. Those are the people who will pull you out of your غَفْلَة.

He then says: *وَهِيَ لَدَيْهِمْ أَصْلُ كُلِّ ذَنْبٍ* — those of this science consider غَفْلَة the source of all wrong actions, as Imam al-Junayd al-Baghdadi held. *وَدَوَاؤُهَا بِأَرْبَعِ دَوَاءٍ* — and the cure consists of four things that carry rectifying qualities: *فَاسْتَغْفِرِ اللهَ وَزُرْ وَصَلِّ عَلَى النَّبِيِّ وَاكْتُبْ وَاذْكُرْ*. So he gives the four things.

The first is اِسْتِغْفَار, because once you realize you have been غَافِل, you begin to see what you have done wrong as a consequence of that heedlessness, and you want to ask forgiveness. Many of the later scholars said that اِسْتِغْفَار and صَلَاةً عَلَى النَّبِيِّ are the two ذِكْرٌ of the final era — the two pillars of devotion for this last period of time. Istighfar is asking forgiveness of Allah, and it is a form of تَوْبَة. The Prophet ﷺ said that you should ask اِسْتِغْفَار at least seventy times, up to a hundred times a day — *أَسْتَغْفِرُ اللَّهَ* — so it is always a good practice. A complementary practice that most of the early Muslims observed was مُحَاسَبَة. The idea comes from what merchants do at the end of the trading day: they count the money, see what was gained and what was lost, and balance the scales. مُحَاسَبَة for the soul is the same: at the end of the day, you look at your day and ask — was there loss with Allah, or gain? If there was loss, you ask اِسْتِغْفَار. If there was gain, you say *الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ* and are grateful. *لَىِٕن شَكَرْتُمْ لَأَزِيدَنَّكُمْ* — if you are grateful, Allah increases you. So that is one way. He goes into شُكْر as well, since that is one of the مَقَامَاتٌ we will reach إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ.

The second remedy is زِيَارَة — visiting righteous people. The صَالِحِينَ occupy the lowest of four ranks: the أَنْبِيَاء and رُسُل are first, then the صِدِّيقٌ, then the شُهَدَاء, then the صَالِحِين. And yet they are all, in a sense, صَالِحٌ — a شَاهِد must be a صَلِيح, a صِدِّيق must be a صَلِيح, and the أَنْبِيَاء are all صَالِحِينَ by their very nature. صَلَاحٌ in Arabic means to be sound, and this is related to سَلَامَة — soundness, wholeness, the person who possesses a قَلْبٌ سَلِيمٌ. The definition of a صَلِيح is: *يُؤَدِّي حَقَّ اللهِ وَيُؤَدِّي حَقَّ الْعِبَادِ* — he gives what is due to Allah and he gives what is due to the servants of Allah. حَقُّ اللَّهِ is تَقْوَى: doing what He commanded and avoiding what He prohibited. حَقُّ الْعِبَادِ are all the responsibilities you carry in the world toward other people. So the صَلِيح cannot cheat — that is فِسْقٌ, the very opposite of صَلَاح. He does not lie, because the حَقٌّ of people upon you is that you be truthful to them. The صَلِيح is a person worth visiting.

Most scholars not only encourage visiting the صَالِحِينَ who are alive, but also those who have died. If you are in Medina, you go to بَقِيعْ and visit the صَالِحِينَ there — you give them سَلَامٌ, you say *رَحِمَهُمُ اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِمْ*. The Prophet ﷺ used to visit Sayyidna Hamza, and he visited the graveyard and said: *كُنْتُ نَهَيْتُكُمْ عَنْ زِيَارَةِ الْقُبُورِ فَزُورُوهَا* — "I used to forbid you from visiting graves," because of what the pre-Islamic Arabs used to do at them, "but now that they have been purified of that جَاهِلِيَّة, visit them." There is a benefit in visiting graves — it is a reminder of the آخِرَة — and there is a greater benefit in visiting the graves of the people of صَلَاح.

<!-- page 141 -->

On the question of women and graves: there is a hadith — *لَعَنَ اللَّهُ زَوَّارَاتِ الْقُبُورِ*, and in one narration *زَائِرَاتُ الْقُبُورِ* — and زَوَّرَ is an intensive form, referring to someone who visits graves excessively and repeatedly. There is a hadith that is considered مُتَوَاتِر in which the Prophet ﷺ passed by the graveyard in Medina and found a woman there weeping. He said it would be better to be patient. She replied: *مَا أَصَابَكَ مَا أَصَابَنِي* — "You have not been struck by the calamity I have been struck by" — and she did not recognize him. He left her, and then someone told her that had been the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. She immediately went and knocked at his door and said: "I did not know it was you." And the Prophet ﷺ said: *الصَّبْرُ عِنْدَ الصَّدْمَةِ الْأُولَى* — "Patience is with the first blow." Patience is when the affliction first strikes — that is when Allah expects it from you. After a year, if the grief has passed and you have moved forward, that is not صَبْر; that is simply time doing its work. The صَبْر is at the moment the مُصِيبَة occurs, and that is where Allah wants to see ثَبَاتٌ — steadfastness — and not a shaking that would cause one to do something displeasing to Him. But the point of this hadith for our discussion is that the Prophet ﷺ did not tell her to leave the graveyard — and scholars take from that the permissibility of women visiting graves, though there is indeed a خِلَافٌ on the matter.

As for the أَدَب of visiting graves: what happens at some graves nowadays has nothing to do with what is being discussed here. At some shrines — and this applies in many parts of the Muslim world — people tie knots, women who cannot conceive go to ask the وَلِيٌّ to give them a son, people make vows to the buried saint. All of that has nothing to do with a proper زِيَارَة. He is talking about visiting with أَدَب, for the blessing of the visit itself — a زِيَارَة. That is something quite different. And this confusion is part of what has given تَصَوُّف a bad name: much of what is done under its label is بِدْعَة — in fact, it really is بِدْعَة. Women sitting around in a مَوْلِد without حِجَابٌ, chatting and gossiping all day — those are all problems. In such cases, the scholars' strategy is sometimes simply to prohibit the practice altogether, because the corruption has become so pervasive. But the best, without question, is to visit the living صَالِحِينَ.

Unfortunately, in our time, this is quite rare. To find a truly righteous person, you often have to travel far and go to difficult places, because many of them have simply retreated to places that are hard to reach. Someone like Murabbit al-Hajj — رَحِمَهُ اللهُ — whom we visited in Shinqit, the father of Shaykh Abdallah — I consider him, in my estimation, from the صَالِحِينَ. When Allah says *يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَكُونُوا مَعَ الصَّادِقِينَ* — "be with the truthful ones" — He would not command us to do something if there were no such people and no way to recognize them. It would be foolish to think we cannot recognize people of صِدْق and صَلَاح. There are obviously degrees and levels, but the basic criterion is the شَرِيعَة: if someone is outwardly upright in their observance of the شَرِيعَة, that is our standard. We judge by the outward, and we judge by truth.

One of the dangers I have noticed in modern تَصَوُّف — in many Muslim countries — is that there are people who go by the name of بِرّ or شَيْخٌ or whatever, and many of them inherited the title from their father, who inherited it from his father, and many of them have very little knowledge, sometimes almost none. And they sometimes use their positions for material gain — extracting money from followers, arranging advantageous marriages for their daughters, and so on. There are a great many charlatans. And nothing is worse than a religious charlatan — nothing. There is a man in Fez, Sidi Ma'ib, whom some here know, and he once said to me: "Allah has made the world vast, and He has made the means of earning حَلَالٌ provision without number — there are untold ways of earning حَلَالٌ. So whenever you see someone using the religion to gain دُنْيَا, know that it is a bad thing." If they are using it to accumulate wealth and exploit their position, that is a serious problem — and unfortunately there are many who claim to be شُيُوخٌ or صُوفِي شُيُوخٌ who do exactly that.

That said, having a livelihood is a different matter entirely. *خَيْرُ مَا أَكَلَ الرَّجُلُ مِنْ كَسْبِ يَدِهِ* — the best thing a man can earn from is the work of his own hands. The عُلَمَاء have taken from the hadith on teaching the Quran that it is permissible to earn from teaching. And even though the early سَلَفٌ were generally opposed to taking payment for teaching, the later scholars recognized that people must have a livelihood and that the world has become more complicated. That is not the issue. The issue is amassing and accumulating wealth — using religion as a vehicle for that. Imam Malik, رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ, was given enormous amounts of wealth because of who he was, but what he did was spend it all out. Murabit al-Hajj is a fine example of this: money comes from all over, and it all goes straight back out. You can see it — a few cows, a tent, an adobe house, a few robes, some books, and constant giving. People give to righteous people because they trust them, and practicing Muslims are generous, so they want to give. The question is not receiving — the question is hoarding, accumulating beyond one's needs, refusing to let it flow out. When your needs are taken care of and you begin to hoard, that is where the problem lies.

You have to be very careful, and it has been a problem for a long time. The scholars complained about it centuries ago. One scholar — and this man himself was a genuine شَيْخٌ, a مُرَبٍّ, a person of real تَرْبِيَة and outward knowledge both — said that in his time, the ninth century, most people who called themselves صُوفِي شُيُوخٌ were complete charlatans. So it is an old problem. And that is a condition, by the way: anyone who does تَرْبِيَة must have outward knowledge. They cannot be without it.

Unfortunately, in the Muslim world today there is a profound split between تَصَوُّف and exoteric Islam — between the inward and outward dimensions of the tradition — and they have become two camps pitched against each other. Nowhere is this more visible than on the Arabian Peninsula, where you have the Najdis and the Hijazis: most of the Najdis are staunchly anti-صُوفِي, and many of the Hijazis are enthusiastically pro-مَوْلِد and pro-تَصَوُّف, and they are at loggerheads, with real hostility between them. We are out of balance as an أُمَّة, and the truth lies somewhere between the two positions. Make دُعَاء for them, and ask to benefit from their respective knowledges.

Ahmad Baba al-Timbukti — the great scholar from Timbuktu who was taken as a slave to Morocco — when he arrived in Marrakech, he was so much more learned than any of the scholars there that they quickly realized they had made a grave mistake. He mentions in his book — Dibaj al-Mudhahhab, his خَشْيَة on Ibn Farhun's طَبَقَات — visiting several of the graves of the righteous in Marrakech and asking Allah at one of them to grant him understanding of two books he was working on. He says that by Allah, he returned to those books and could read them without any difficulty. He was a great scholar, and that was the traditional practice: to ask for the blessing of their knowledges, since most of those buried in such places were scholars, and to ask *رَحِمَهُ اللهُ* — that Allah give them light in their graves and bless them.

Qadi Abu Bakr ibn al-'Arabi — whose grave is just outside of Fez — gave a fatwa permitting the visiting of the graves of the صَالِحِينَ, as did Ibn al-Hajj. For those who say it is بِدْعَة: that is simply not consistent with the position of the vast majority of our scholars, who have not only permitted it but recommended it. And these are outward scholars, jurists — well known in the tradition. Qadi Abu Bakr ibn al-'Arabi's grave is well known outside of Fez; he was poisoned by his enemies and died a martyr there, having also fought jihad multiple times. He wrote two commentaries on the Qur'an. He brought the إِحْيَاءُ عُلُومِ الدِّينِ from Imam al-Ghazali back to Morocco and Andalusia — he had studied it with al-Ghazali directly and transmitted it west. He is a great scholar, and anyone who has visited that place knows there is a great deal of بَرَكَةٌ there, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ.

But the living — if you have the opportunity to visit them, do so. And you should have high هِمَّة: desire to be one of them, so that our أُمَّة has people worth visiting. We need these people. Make the intention to be one yourself, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, and ask Allah to give you تَوْفِيق — and give all of us تَوْفِيق for that. One of the poets said: *إِذَا الْمَرْءُ لَمْ يَرْتَدِ الْعُلَا* — if a man does not reach the heights, at least he asبِرّes to them. *وَمَنْ قَنِعَ بِالدُّونِ كَانَ دَنِيًّا* — whoever is content with less than heights is himself less than heights. The greatest asبِرّation is to be close to Allah. Have high هِمَّة. And Sidi Ibn 'Ajiba said: if you do not believe that Allah can take you in this very moment and make you one of His أَوْلِيَاء, then you know nothing of His قُدْرَة. Never despair. Asبِرّe to be from the people of وِلَايَة, the people who are close to Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى.

So زِيَارَة is important. When you visit these people — those I have visited from the people of this path — you will notice that they do not engage in idle chatter. You do not go for a social call. You go for the benefit of learning something, and for the benefit of their دُعَاء. Ask them for their دُعَاء, because it may well be accepted, and if it is, you receive the blessing of it. Visit them, ask for their دُعَاء, and show humility in their presence.

I will share a story about Mulla Ramadan, who was from the صَالِحِينَ and was the father of Shaykh Sa'id Ramadan al-Buti. Shaykh Sa'id wrote a book called *هٰذَا وَالِدِي* — "This Is My Father" — describing what a great man his father was. I once asked Dr. Bukar — a Libyan scholar, I believe he is in New Jersey now — about Mulla Ramadan, because it is always strengthening to the إِيمَان to hear stories of these people. He told me — سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ — that the most remarkable thing about visiting that man was this: he said he had always found the practice of kissing hands rather unnecessary, something he had never been comfortable with. He said he went in, Mulla Ramadan extended his hand, and he felt a kind of pressure on his head — and found himself going down and kissing his hand. When he looked back, there was nothing there. He was

They would pray on the Prophet ﷺ. Imam Malik, may Allah be pleased with him, said he never spent a single night without seeing the Prophet ﷺ in a dream. There is a great blessing in it, and people bear witness to that.

<!-- page 133 -->

The fourth practice is تِلَاوَةُ الْقُرْآنِ — recitation of the Qur'an, preferably with تَدَبُّر, which means reflection and pondering the meanings rather than merely reading the words. Reciting with تَدَبُّر awakens the heart; it is one of the things that genuinely rouses it from slumber. Even reciting without deep reflection carries its own benefit. You should have a daily portion of Qur'an — ideally at least a حِزْبٌ or a جَزْء each day. If that is not possible, then at minimum recite the surahs the Messenger of Allah ﷺ recommended: يَاسِين, generally after فَجْر; and the surahs after مَغْرِب and الْمُلْك after إِحْسَاء.

For those who have not yet learned Arabic, reading the meanings in English is something, but it is limited — and this is the honest truth — because part of what the Qur'an does operates through the actual sounds of the language. Think of it like medicine. From an energetic perspective, every substance in the world resonates at a specific wavelength. Diseases resonate, and a medicine that cures a disease resonates in a way that counteracts it. The recitation of the Qur'an works through the sounds themselves, the sounds that emanate from you as you speak. We now know from material science that sound waves resonate through every cell in the body. When you recite the Qur'an, you are producing divine words that Allah revealed — and those words carry a sound.

<!-- page 134 -->

I was once with a man at a gas station. He was asking about Islam. A Muslim mechanic was there, and I was present wearing a robe, so he began asking me about it. He mentioned that he had read the Qur'an. I said, "You have never read the Qur'an unless you read it in Arabic." He said he couldn't believe that. I said it was a fact. He asked why, and I said, "Just listen." Then I recited the opening of Surat al-Rahman to him — and وَاللَّهِ, by the One besides whom there is no god — when I finished, he simply said, "I understand now." He was not joking. Non-Arab people, عَجَمِي people, are often stunned when they hear the Qur'an, even if they are not Muslim. They are amazed by the sounds.

You have to recognize that the Qur'an is not only its meanings — it is also the sounds, which transcend and participate in the meaning in a realm we do not fully comprehend, because everything in reality is meaning. Allah has taken meaning all the way down to the phonemes of the Qur'an. In modern linguistics, phonemes are considered meaningless in isolation; they acquire meaning only when joined into polysyllabic forms. Someone once wrote a nasty critique of Dr. Hamid Algar, complaining that his speech was filled with polysyllabic sentences. Dr. Algar pointed out in his rebuttal that a sentence that is not polysyllabic is, by definition, meaningless. And yet even individual phonemes carry meaning in Arabic. We may not know what that meaning is, but the meaning goes all the way down. Those are the four practices he identifies.

<!-- page 135 -->

Now, Allah warns the Prophet ﷺ about sitting with those whose hearts He has placed in a state of heedlessness. Allah clarifies elsewhere in the Qur'an that when people deviated, He caused their deviation to increase. Those who turn away from the truth, Allah places in a state of غَفْلَة. غَفْلَة, then, is itself a punishment. A Muslim who is in غَفْلَة is experiencing a consequence of his own disobedience toward Allah ﷻ. It is a serious condition — you do not want to be among the غَافِلِينَ.

And yet, in all honesty, all of us will move in and out of غَفْلَة. That is simply the nature of existence in this world. غَفْلَة ends the moment you die. What matters is that you spend more of your life in a state of wakefulness than in a state of heedlessness. You want to be someone who is not in غَفْلَة.

This is why سَيِّدِنَا Umar said that the best remembrance of Allah is when you reach a limit He has set. In other words, when you are on the verge of committing a sin — none of you drink wine, but imagine you were about to — and in that moment you remember Allah and think, "I am about to put fire into my stomach," and that remembrance becomes a زَجْرٌ, a مَنِيع, a deterrent that prevents you from the حَرَامٌ — that is the best ذِكْرٌ. When the desire for disobedience rises and the remembrance of Allah holds you back: that is what he means. So there are degrees of غَفْلَة, and what he is saying is that the disease is the غَفْلَة that prevents you from fulfilling what Allah has commanded and avoiding what He has prohibited. Anything beyond that, remembering Allah is مُسْتَحَبّ.

Allah has prescribed remembrance at certain fixed times of day. He did not make remembrance throughout the entire day an obligation, because not everyone is capable of that. He said: remember Me at appointed times, أَوْقَاتٌ مُعَيَّنَةٌ. But He also encourages us to make much remembrance of Allah — and that is نَفْل, recommended, not obligatory. The more you remember Allah, the less likely you are to fall into the غَفْلَة you do not want. And again, the disease of the heart is specifically the غَفْلَة that prevents you from fulfilling your obligations or refraining from what has been prohibited.

As for the opposite — those in whose hearts Allah has written إِيمَان — one of the meanings here relates to writing itself: to inscribe without diacritical marks or voweling signs. That is one dimension of the meaning. The إِيمَان that Allah writes into the heart is of that nature.

## Rancor

The next disease is غِلّ. Ghil is a disease of the heart related to rancor — resentful malice. The root word is a ثُلَاثِي, a three-letter root that can go three ways: غِلٌّ, غِلٌّ, and غِلّ. The غِلٌّ or the غُلّ is a قَيْد, a shackle. The people of the Fire have a غِلٌّ that binds them. The Prophet ﷺ was sent to relieve humanity of their burdens and the things that were binding them — that is what the غِلٌّ is. And غِلّ is when the heart becomes bound, as though shackled, to treachery, malice, or the desire to betray or harm someone. There is a lock holding it in place, just as a handcuff has the mechanism that holds it closed.

What holds the غِلّ together is resentful malice — something that results from being angry at a person and keeping that anger inside the heart until it festers and eventually becomes a desire to harm that person. This is why among the things اللَّه mentions in the Qur'an is the supplication: do not place in our hearts غِلّ toward the believers. You do not want غِلّ in your heart toward any believer, because it will kill your heart. Literally kill it.

<!-- page 138 -->

One thing اللَّه does in جَنَّة is remove غِلّ entirely. مُؤْمِنِيْن may die still carrying غِلّ, but you do not want to bring it with you. اللَّه says in the Qur'an: وَنَزَعۡنَا مَا فِی صُدُورِهِم مِّنۡ غِلٍّ — "And We will remove whatever غِلّ is in their hearts," and He makes them brothers, reclining together on couches.

In the دُنْيَا, سَيِّدِنَا عَلِيٍّ had an experience of this. A man who had fought against him — it may have been at the Battle of جَمَال — came to him and apologized. And that is the verse سَيِّدِنَا عَلِيٍّ recited: We removed from their hearts. He was saying, الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ, اللَّه will take that غِلّ out of the heart. You can carry rancor toward a person and اللَّه can remove it. So ask اللَّه to remove the غِلّ from you.

He then tells us how to do it: أَحْسِنْ إِلَيْهِ — do good to that person. That is what the man did when he went to سَيِّدِنَا عَلِيٍّ. He humbled himself and apologized, and that was his إِحْسَان. If you have غِلّ toward a مُؤْمِن, do good to him. The nature of إِحْسَان is captured in a حَدِيثٌ: جُبِلَتِ الْقُلُوبُ عَلَى حُبِّ مَنْ أَحْسَنَ إِلَيْهَا — by their nature, hearts are inclined to love those who do good to them. اللَّه has built this inclination into the heart regardless of a person's will. If you do good to someone, اللَّه has made his heart naturally drawn toward you.

<!-- page 139 -->

Now some of the Arabs noted — and an Arab poet said — that when you do good to a noble person you win him over, but if you do good to a lowly person it can make him more rebellious, more agitated. So there are people like that. But you have done your part. If you do good to someone in order to cure your own heart, that is what اللَّه is asking of you. You do your best. If the other person does not respond, you have done what was in your power. You are then left to make دُعَاء for them and to remember that اللَّه can forgive them.

He also says this will cause your enemies — meaning شَيْطَان and his minions who love to sow غِلّ among the believers — to despair. شَيْطَان loves to do this. He loves to set the believers at each other's throats. I once asked a شَيْخٌ why it is that so many disbelievers seem to live peacefully together without much conflict, while even sincere Muslims often fight one another. He said: because شَيْطَان is not occupied with the disbelievers anymore. They are already in كُفْرٌ — he can leave them alone. But he is obsessed with the people of إِيمَان. He works on them constantly, trying to make them stray. That is why, as has been transmitted, شَيْطَان has despaired of being worshipped on the Arabian Peninsula, but he has not despaired of causing فِتْنَةٌ and dissension among the people. We have to be vigilant against him.

And تُقْنِتُهُ — the way you cause شَيْطَان to despair when غِلّ begins to arise in the heart is to reject it and to do good to that person at precisely the moment you want to harm them.

Then remember what came in the authentic حَدِيثٌ about Mondays and Thursdays. According to that حَدِيثٌ, the Prophet ﷺ said that اللَّه forgives people on those two days, and when the angels bring the records, they say: there are two people here who are in conflict, both believers. And اللَّه says: leave them — leave them until they reconcile with one another. What this means is that if you carry غِلّ toward a fellow believer, اللَّه is not forgiving you. If you are at war with another Muslim, that forgiveness is withheld. All you have to do is extend the greeting with sincerity. That is all. Do not turn your back. If you say السَّلَامُ عَلَيْكُمْ and they do not reply, you have fulfilled your obligation. It is then in their hands. Do what you can — that is all you are responsible for. But this is a frightening حَدِيثٌ. You do not want animosity toward another believer, because it will prevent you from receiving اللَّه's forgiveness.

## Boasting & Arrogance

<!-- lecture 2 -->

Now, to — أَعُوذُ بِاللَّهِ — this is the significant one: فَخْر and كِبْرٌ. كِبْرٌ. فَخْر.

<!-- page 141 -->

فَخْر is, at its core, boasting. Boasting is a disease of the heart. فَخْر differs from مُبَاحَة and similar things in that فَخْر, as understood here, is boasting about something you have not yourself earned. It is boasting about lineage — saying, for instance, "I am a شَرِيف," or trading on what your father or ancestors did.

One of the most striking moments illustrating this is from the Battle of Uhud. The Arabs would shout out, "أَنَا ابْنُ فُلَانٍ" — "I am the son of so-and-so" — as a form of war cry, a display of lineage. The Prophet ﷺ would respond: "أَنَا ابْنُ الْعَوَاتِكِ" — "I am the son of the women named عَاتِكَة." The Arabs did not boast through their mothers, so he was doing the opposite of what they intended. Many of his grandmothers bore the name عَاتِكَة, and that was his response to their boasting of fathers.

<!-- page 142 -->

On the matter of boasting about parents, الْخُدَّام mentions — and the saying goes — لَئِنْ فَخَرْتُ بِآبَاءٍ ذَوِي شَرَفٍ، لَقَدْ صَدَقْتَ وَلَكِنْ بِئْسَ مَا وَلَدُوا: if you boast about ancestors who truly had honor, what you say may even be true — if they were from بَنِي هَاشِمٍ, for instance, then yes, it is factually true — but what a wretched child they left behind. If you are boasting about your parents and have yourself accomplished nothing, then as the Arabs say: وَلَيْسَ الْفَتَى مَنْ قَالَ كَانَ أَبِي، إِنَّمَا الْفَتَى مَنْ قَالَ هَا أَنَا — the man is not the one who says "my father was." The man is the one who says "here I am." You do something yourself.

Jalal al-Din al-Rumi has a poem in which he names all the great figures who came before, and then says: but do not be content with the stories of those who went before you — go out and create your own story. Be one of those people that those who come after you will speak about. This is part of our teaching: the idea of being genuinely significant — not for the sake of دُنْيَا, but for the sake of being someone who actually does something.

This is boasting. Saying "my father did this" — it is what small children do. Children say, "My dad is a fireman." "Well, my dad is an astronaut." It is childish. Mentioning a parent's إِحْسَان, something good they did for you — that is a different matter entirely, and there is nothing wrong with it. But boasting is something we all recognize.

<!-- page 143 -->

Now he says — the mountain, فَوْدُهَا شَامِخٌ, this high mountain — deem that mountain insignificant if you desire to bring it down to the ground. كِبْرٌ: arrogance. In Arabic, كِبْرٌ with a فَتْحًا gives you كِبَرٌ, meaning old age. كِبْرٌ with a سُكُونٌ over the بَا gives you arrogance. The root is كَبُرَ or كَبِيرَة — two related roots. كَبُرَ means to grow, to be great; كَبِيرَة means to grow old. كَبِيرٌ means great — Ibrahim, peace be upon him, said: "بَلْ فَعَلَهُۥ كَبِيرُهُمْ" — "the great one did it." So كَبِير is greatness. كِبْرٌ is something that lives in the heart. When it manifests in outward behavior, you call it تَكَبُّر. كِبْرٌ and تَكَبُّر are the same disease — تَكَبُّر is simply the action of it. تَكَبَّرَ عَلَيَّ: he was arrogant toward me, meaning he did something that made it visible.

<!-- page 144 -->

There are many signs of كِبْرٌ. And I will tell you honestly — of all these diseases, this is the one I find most frightening. Because you have some degree of it. All of us do. It exists on a continuum: it can be severe, it can be mild, and you will rarely encounter someone who has none of it at all. When you do meet such a person, you feel it — their absence of كِبْرٌ is palpable. But nearly everyone carries some. And the most frightening thing about it is the حَدِيث in which the Prophet ﷺ said:

لَا يَدۡخُلُ الۡجَنَّةَ مَنۡ كَانَ فِي قَلۡبِهِ مِثۡقَالُ ذَرَّةٍ مِنۡ كِبۡرٍ

No one will enter Paradise — or, as the حَدِيث puts it, *كَمَا قَدْ* — no one will enter Paradise who carries even the weight of an atom of *كِبْرٌ*, of arrogance, in his heart. Arrogance, then, is literally a بَاrrier to Paradise.

This is grounded in the Quranic verse: سَأَصْرِفُ عَنْ آيَتِىَ ٱلَّذِينَ يَتَكَبَّرُونَ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ بِغَيْرِ ٱلْحَقِّ — "I will divert My signs from those who show arrogance on the earth without right." The scholars note that the phrase بِغَيْرِ الْحَقِّ implies that there are situations in which *تَكَبُّر* is actually appropriate. This was precisely the case with Abu Dujanah's famous *تَبَخْتُرٌ* — his swaggering gait on the بَاttlefield — which carried a *حَقٌّ*, a legitimate justification. In that context, what would ordinarily be reprehensible became praiseworthy.

So Allah says He will divert His signs from those who are arrogant on the earth without justification, without any legitimate right. And He also says: كَذَلِكَ يَطْبَعُ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ قَلْبِ مُتَكَبِّرٍۢ جَبَّارٍۢ — Allah will seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant. And: إِنَّهُۥ لَا يُحِبُّ ٱلْمُسْتَكْبِرِينَ — He does not love those who harbor *اِسْتِكْبَار*. *اِسْتِكْبَار* is essentially the same as *كِبْرٌ*, but with an additional nuance: the person actively considers himself great. يَسْتَكْبِرُ — he thinks he is great. And at its root lies *عُجْب*, that self-admiration he discussed earlier — مُعْجَبٌ بِنَفْسِهِ, thoroughly impressed with himself.

There is also the حَدِيث in which Allah says, as reported by the Prophet ﷺ: يَقُولَ اللَّهُ تَعَالَى الْكِبْرِيَاءُ رِدَائِ وَالْعَظَمَةُ إِذَارِ فَمَن نَازَعْنِي فِيهِمَا قَصَمْتُهُ كِبْرِيَاءُ — Greatness is My cloak, and majesty is My lower garment. Whoever tries to compete with Me over either of them, I will break him. These are two attributes Allah has reserved exclusively for Himself, and He does not permit any created being to lay claim to them.

This brings to mind something relevant to the subcontinent: there are certain names commonly given there that would proبَاbly be better avoided. Ibn al-Hajj observed that Shaytan entered this *أُمَّة* through two different doors. With the eastern Muslims, he entered through names of grandiosity. With the western Muslims, he entered by causing them to mispronounce good names — dropping the *عَبْد* — so that, for example, in parts of West Africa, عَبْدُ الْكَبِيرِ becomes simply الكَبِيرُ, or عَبْدُ الْقَادِرِ becomes الْقَادِرُ. And in Sudan, the pronunciation shifts similarly — الْقَادِرُ, which sounds as though it derives from غَدَرَ, meaning the treacherous one, when of course Allah's name is الْقَادِرُ. Ibn al-Hajj identified this as one of Shaytan's stratagems.

As for the eastern Arabs, Shaytan led them toward names such as فَخْرُ الدِّين شَمْسُ الدِّين جَمَالُ الدِّين. This is why Imam Yahya al-Nawawi, whose father named him مُحْيِي الدِّينِ, flatly refused that name and insisted on being called Yahya. He would not accept it at all. Names in this category — أَكْبَرُ، عَظِيمٌ، عُزْمَةٌ and the like — carry that same problem. The issue is that such names constitute a *تَزْكِيَة*, a self-endorsement. The Prophet ﷺ had a wife named بِرَّ and renamed her زَيْنَب, and said: لَا تُزَكُّوا أَنْفُسَكُمْ — do not declare yourselves pure. He also said: أَصْدَقُ الْأَسْمَاءِ حَارِثٌ وَهَمَّامٌ — the truest names are Harith and Humam, because every person يَحْرِثُ — he toils — and every person carries concern. Those, then, are the most honest names. And the best names are those that include عَبْدَ اللَّهِ or عَبْدَ الرَّحْمَنِ, or names like أَحْمَد. If your name happens to be one of the others, that is simply what your parents gave you — so live up to it as best you can. As was pointed out with some humor, the trouble is when someone bears a lofty name yet turns out to be مِلْعَقَةُ الدِّينِ — a spoon of the religion, just eating all the time.

Now there is a حَدِيث — and it needs verification, though the transmission appears to be there — that is simply astonishing: يَحْشَرَ الْمُتَكَبِّرٌُونَ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ فِي مِثْرِ صُورَ الْذَرَّ تَطَّأُهُمَ النَّاسُ ذَرًّا فِي مِثْرِ صُورَ الرِّجَالِ يَعْلُوهُمْ كُلُّ شَيْءٍ مِنَ الصَّغَارِ ثُمَّ يُسَاقُونَ إِلَى السِّسْنِ فِي جَهَنَمٍ يُقَالُوا لَهُ بَوْلَسٍ. It is reported in al-Tirmidhi: the people of arrogance will be raised on the Day of Judgment transformed into atoms, and people will walk over them. Then they will be driven to a prison in Jahannam called بَلَاصْ. SubhanAllah. The word *بَلَاصْ* is strikingly close to what Arabic speakers call the police — خُذُوا إِلَى الْبَوْلَسِ، جَبَّارُونَ خُذُوا إِلَى الْبَوْلِسِ. Whether that detail is precisely established in al-Tirmidhi needs to be confirmed, but if it is, it is extraordinary. The word *ذَرّ* can mean either an atom or an ant — both readings exist.

Moving on to the nature of *اِسْتِكْبَار* itself: there are two types. The worst form of *تَكَبُّر* is refusing to accept the truth and refusing to submit to the worship of Allah — أَعْظَم التَكَبُّر امتنع قبول الحق وإذعان للعبادة.. Everything else falls beneath that in degrees of severity.

A companion once came to the Prophet ﷺ and said: يا رسول الله إني امرأ أحب الجمال — O Messenger of Allah, I am a man who loves beautiful things. I wear fine shoes and a nice garment. He then asked: أَهَذَا مِنَ الْكِبْرِ — Is this from *كِبْرٌ*? The Prophet ﷺ replied: لا إن الله جميل يحب الجمال وإنما الكبر بطر الحق وغمص الناس — No. Allah is beautiful and loves beauty. *كِبْرٌ* is, rather, rejecting the truth and looking down on people.

This is an important حَدِيث, because the man first explained his motivation: he loved beautiful things. The scholars clarify that the problem arises when a person dresses or adorns himself in order to تَكَبَّرَ عَلَى النَّاسِ — to demonstrate superiority over others. This is painfully familiar in contemporary Muslim culture. Go to some Muslim weddings and you will see people consumed with outdoing one another — "عَبْدُ اللهِ and Khadija are going to show everyone what a real wedding looks like." That competitive one-upmanship is a form of *كِبْرٌ*, because the underlying drive is تَتَكَبَّرُ عَلَيْهِمْ — to assert superiority. It springs from *كِبْرٌ*.

So *اِسْتِكْبَار* has two faces. On one side, a person wants to achieve greatness and be great. Imam Raghib al-Isfahani says that if this drive is oriented toward something genuinely great, exercised at the right time, and for the right reasons, then it is praiseworthy. *اِسْتِكْبَار* in this sense is not inherently negative — and this returns to the broader principle of transforming these diseases into virtues. A desire to be great for the sake of Allah is a good thing. A desire to accomplish great things is a good thing. The *صَحَابَة* had towering *هِمَّةٌ* — tremendous aspiration. The Prophet ﷺ's *هِمَّةٌ* was the highest of all, and he lifted them all toward it. They set their sights on al-Thuraya, the Pleiades. They reached for the heights in everything they did, and Allah made them great people. That is praiseworthy *هِمَّةٌ*.

The blameworthy kind, by contrast, is يظهر من نفسه أنه أفضل من غيره. — when a person tries to project the image that he is better than others. That is what is censured. The one exception is demonstrating to non-Muslims that Muslims are better — because Allah actually commands this. Allah commands us to show them. Someone raised this very point the previous night regarding the Christian response to human nature. The Christian answer, in simplified terms, is that human beings are irredeemably corrupt by original sin — there is nothing to be done about it. Allah's answer is: show them this is not true. كَذَٰرِكَ جَعَلْنَاكُمْ أُمَّةً وَسَطًى — We made you a just, بَاlanced community — لِّتَكُونُوا۟ شُهَدَآءَ عَلَى ٱلنَّاسِ — so that you may be witnesses to mankind, demonstrating that all other ways fall short. وَيَكُونَ ٱلرَّسُولُ عَلَيْكُمْ شَهِيدًۭا — and the Messenger is a witness over you.

The Prophet ﷺ was a witness to us — he proved it was true. His Companions were witnesses to us — they proved it was true. Their followers were witnesses in turn. But each generation becomes more diluted, until eventually there are no more *شُهَدَاء*, no more living witnesses. This is one reason why so many people in the end of times fall into disbelief: they no longer see the truth embodied anywhere, so they lose it.

One of the signs of the end of times, according to Imam al-Biqa'i, is that Allah will hand the *دُنْيَا* over to the disbelievers, and Muslims will aبَاndon Islam in pursuit of what the disbelievers have. He read this in the verses of Surah al-Zukhruf: had Allah not wished to prevent everyone from becoming disbelievers, He would have made their houses of silver and given them staircases to climb. Look around today — that is almost precisely what you see. People in Mali and Niger watch television and see those gleaming houses and want them. It is a real and serious problem.

Can the sealed heart be penetrated? Yes. Allah says that even rock can be split and water can flow from it — seals can be broken. The scholars say they are broken by the power of the *وَارِد*, the spiritual influx. A prophet, through the sheer spiritual force he carries — by the permission of Allah — can penetrate even the sealed heart. Sidi Ahmad Zarruq says that when someone with a sealed heart hears the truth, several factors determine whether it gets through: the stature of the one delivering it, the intrinsic strength of the truth itself, and the force with which it reaches the heart. If these converge with sufficient power, it can shake the heart and crack the seal. But if nothing follows — no *تَوْبَة*, no action taken — the crack can close over again. This is what happens when someone attends a *خُطْبَة*, feels something stir in the heart, then steps outside and returns to the same life: it seals بَاck up.

Now, before concluding, here is a brief survey of the different types of *كِبْرٌ* and their distinguishing qualities.

The first type: *كِبْرٌ* that is *مُسْتَقَرٌّ* — fixed and settled in the heart. The person genuinely believes he is better than others. There is a حَدِيث in which a man was being praised in the Prophet's ﷺ presence as a person of virtue. When the man entered the gathering, the Companions said: يا رسول الله هذا الذي ذكرناه لك — O Messenger of Allah, this is the man we mentioned to you. The Prophet ﷺ said: إني أرى في وجهه صفعة من الشيطان. — I see in his face a trace of Shaytan. The man gave *سَلَامٌ* and sat down. Then the Prophet ﷺ turned to him and said: أسألك بالله حدثتك نفسك أليس في القوم أفضل منك. — I ask you by Allah: did your *نَفْس* tell you, when you walked into this gathering, that no one here is better than you? The man said: Yes. So outwardly he appeared to be a righteous man, but the Prophet ﷺ saw the *كِبْرٌ* embedded in his heart — and wanted the Companions to understand how subtle and hidden *كِبْرٌ* can be.

Imam al-Ghazali says that among all people, scholars are in the greatest danger of *كِبْرٌ*. And that includes students of sacred knowledge — people in exactly the situation under discussion. This is one of the gravest spiritual hazards of pursuing religious learning. If a person fights it, however — if he sees the *كِبْرٌ* within but forces himself to resist it — he is at least preventing the bitter fruits of that tree from ripening. The tree is there, the roots are there, but the fruit does not emerge.

The second type is where *كِبْرٌ* moves from the interior to the exterior: the person begins to manifest it in behavior, looking down on others, turning his face away, frowning in people's faces. Frowning at someone is a sign of *كِبْرٌ*. Refusing to give someone the time of day is a sign of *كِبْرٌ*. The Prophet ﷺ never did this. Adi ibn Hatim said he knew the Prophet ﷺ was not a king precisely because an old woman stopped him on the street and he simply stood there and listened to her — that was how he was. This second type includes the *عَابِد*, the devout worshipper, who looks down on others because of his worship — he becomes *مُتَكَبِّرٌ* through his religiosity.

The third type is *تَكَبُّر* on account of *حَسَبٌ* and *نَسَبٌ* — one's personal qualities and one's lineage. These two traditionally went together. Western culture has the concept of *noblesse oblige*: those born into nobility are obliged to behave nobly. Most traditional cultures hold something similar. Among the Arabs, if you were a *شَرِيف* of the Ta'i clan, you were expected to be generous — generosity was the hallmark of Ta'i. If you were Qurayshi, you were expected to carry dignity, to be a *زَعِيمٌ*. If you were Yemeni, you upheld the qualities associated with Yemen. Each group had its defining virtues, and *نَسَبٌ* carried an obligation to embody them.

But *نَسَبٌ* also becomes a source of arrogance. People with noble lineage begin to see themselves as superior to others. Some Arabs, simply because they are Arab, look down on Indians — this is common. *الْحَقُّ يُقَالُ* — to tell the truth — there are many Arabs who think this way. One Arab once said plainly: I never thought a non-Arab could truly be a real Muslim. He said that directly. Similarly, in Pakistan, Indians who did not make *هِجْرَة* may be seen as lesser. If you are Hyderaبَاdi, certain Hyderaبَاdis consider themselves automatically superior to all other Indians by virtue of being from Hyderaبَاd. Americans carry their own version — "we're number one." In Hausaland, many Hausa people are convinced of their superiority over the Yoruبَا and Igbo, and proبَاbly over the Fulani — though the Fulani, for their part, see themselves as superior to the Hausa. In Mauritania, the Kunta see themselves as better than the Massouma. In Greater Syria, the Damascenes consider themselves superior to the people of Aleppo — they even guard the name *شَامِي* jealously, reluctant to let anyone else claim it. This is a human disease, widespread and indiscriminate.

This is a disease of the heart — thinking yourself better simply because of some quality you happen to possess, like being from Damascus. Or being a Nejdi. The Nejdis deem themselves superior to the Hijazis; they call them أَخْلَافُ الْحُجَّاجِ. You find this kind of arrogance embedded in the language, in the terminology of every culture. It is all تَكَبُّر.

Allah says, إِنَّ أَكْرَمَكُمْ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ أَتْقَىٰكُمْ — the most honored of you before Allah are those with تَقْوَى. Yet this disease deludes a person into assuming that someone else couldn't possibly have تَقْوَى — how could he? He's a Hindi. He's a Nejdi. He's whatever.

This is not a modern phenomenon. It goes بَاck a long way. Even among the صَدَأ — those who trace their lineage to the family of the Prophet ﷺ — you find تَكَبُّر. And of all things to feel superior about in terms of lineage, that would have to rank first. I'd put it at number one in نَسَبٌ. And that is precisely the danger, because sometimes these distinctions do carry genuine weight.

There is something real and honorable about descending from the family of the Prophet ﷺ — it is a tremendous distinction. But there are حَدِيث that should put fear in your heart. One حَدِيث says: إِذَا أَبْطَأَكُمُ الْعَمَلُ لَمْ يُسْرِعْ بِكُمُ النَّسَبُ — if your deeds hold you بَاck, your lineage will not carry you forward. In other words, if you have the نَسَبٌ but no deeds to go with it, it will get you nowhere. A fine car with no fuel. If you're a شَرِيف, it's a Mercedes — but deeds are still the petrol. You are not going anywhere without them. There are many حَدِيث warning of this danger.

<!-- page 144 -->

Now, as for the cure — one of them is to reflect on where you actually came from. Allah says that you are from مَاءٍ مَهِينٍ, a vile fluid. Your very origin is a vile fluid. One of the سَلَفٌ, speaking of someone who was being arrogant, said: سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ يَحْمِلُ بَيْنَ جَنْبَيْهِ الْعَذِرَةِ — glory be to God, he carries between his two sides nothing but filth. Where does the تَكَبُّر come from? He is a بَاg of blood like everyone else. Every culture has its own crude ways of making this point, which I won't go into — but people everywhere understand it. Nobody likes تَكَبُّر.

The problem is that the مُتَكَبِّرٌ never sees his own arrogance. That is why, when someone is arrogant with him, he loathes it — even though he himself is guilty of the same thing. This is the meaning of the saying: التَّكَبُّرُ فِي وَجْهِ الْمُتَكَبِّرٌِ عِبَادَةٌ — to show arrogance in the face of an arrogant person is itself a form of worship. It is throwing it بَاck at them. You give them a taste of their own medicine — but it should be understood as a دَوَاءٌ, a remedy, just enough for them to feel what it is like.

That is كِبْرٌ. It is a serious problem. And if you feel that you are a righteous person — that itself is كِبْرٌ, right from the start. That is why, when you look at the early generations, they were people who genuinely saw themselves differently. One of the companions of Abu Hanifa was sitting with him when a man moved his foot aside out of deference. The companion asked why he had done so. The man said, out of respect for someone like you. He replied: وَاللَّهِ لَا أَرَى أَحَدًا هُنَا أَشَرَّ مِنِّي — by God, I see no one in this gathering worse than me.

That is how those people were. It was not performance. They genuinely saw themselves that way.

Look at Sayyidina Ali. There is a sound حَدِيث in which a man asked him: who is better, you or Abu Bakr? He said, Abu Bakr. The man asked, then what about you and Omar? He said, Omar. Then the man asked about himself, and Ali said: ما أنا إلا رجل من المسلمين. — I am just a man among the Muslims. No claim, no station.

For us, looking at this from the outside, it reads as humility. But for the Imam himself, he was not calculating: *I should say this in order to appear humble.* No — that way of thinking is itself a disease. Ibn Ata'illah says: if you are aware of your humility, you are مُتَكَبِّر.

This is why the character of Uriah Heep in Dickens' *David Copperfield* is so remarkable. He is a complete hypocrite who is forever saying, "In my very 'umble opinion, sir" — always performing lowliness, always deferring — while simultaneously stealing money and scheming behind everyone's بَاck. The point is: if you think of yourself as humble, you are مُتَكَبِّر, because you are observing your own lowliness from a position of height. Humility means to be low. If you can see how low you are, you must be looking down from somewhere above. That is كِبْرٌ by definition.

We are diseased. We simply have to accept that and work with it. Our scholars have said: if you cannot yet be like the true people of virtue, at least imitate them — because there is benefit even in the imitation. The Imam himself acknowledges this: pretending at humility is better than being an outright مُتَكَبِّر.

<!-- page 145 -->

One of the things Imam al-Ghazali, رَحِمَهُ اللهُ, says is this: if you want to master calligraphy, you go to the master calligrapher and you repeat what he does, over and over again. It feels completely unnatural at first, but eventually you become a master calligrapher. Interestingly, a neurosurgeon at Stanford said something that maps onto this perfectly: the brain learns through repetition. Whatever you do most consistently becomes embedded in the brain — like driving. Most people drive in a nearly subconscious state; they are not aware of their own driving. That is the ideal of humility — it becomes so woven into your nature that the brain is no longer conscious of it.

When you first start learning to drive, you are actually more careful. Those poor learners with the dual-control car on top, checking the mirror, checking the blinker, anxious and alert — they are often safer than the experienced driver who is singing along to music, talking on the phone, and thinking about other things. The experienced driver is the one who gets into the accident, precisely because it has all become unconscious. And what the neurologist pointed out was striking: stroke patients and those with dementia often retain the things they did most repetitiously. They may forget almost everything else, but they can still drive — because those neural pathways are so deeply fixed that they remain.

This is the idea behind repetition in spiritual practice: إِنَّمَا الْعِلْمُ بِالتَّعَلُّمِ — you acquire it through learning, through تَكْرَار, doing it over and over again. You must force yourself to act with humility until it genuinely becomes your nature. Most of us do not have these qualities naturally — if we did, we would not need to study any of this. That is why we are studying it: we all recognize these diseases in ourselves.

Now the fourth type is التَّفَاخُرُ بِالْجَمَالِ — arrogance on account of physical beauty. One of the cures for this is the old adage: beauty is only skin deep. Beauty is an illusion. First, no one retains their beauty forever — everyone loses it in the end. Second, Allah gave it to you; it is not something you earned. So taking arrogant pride in beauty is a particularly shallow and foolish form of كِبْرٌ.

What I find genuinely fascinating is a study that examined what makes people beautiful. Researchers looked at large numbers of faces and found that what people consistently identified as beautiful was, in fact, average — statistically. An average nose, average eyes, average mouth — all placed together on one face. Beautiful people are, in a real sense, mean-looking. They represent the median. I thought that was remarkable: beauty is the average. And there is a kind of truth in it — the median holds something. It is a foolish thing to take pride in.

There is also a deeply sobering حَدِيث involving Aisha, رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهَا, in which a woman entered and the Prophet ﷺ asked who it was. She indicated the short one. The Prophet ﷺ said: اغْتَبْتِهَا — you have بَاckbitten her. The scholars explain that the reason it constituted بَاckbiting was that it arose from تَكَبُّر: she had, in that moment, a sense of her own greater height, an إِعْجَابٌ with her own stature — مُعْجَبَةٌ بِقَامَتِهَا — and looking at the other woman from that place of self-regard made the remark an act of تَكَبُّر. The Prophet ﷺ rebuked her for it.

The fifth type is كِبْرٌ بِالْمَالِ — pride in wealth. This afflicts kings with their treasuries, merchants with their goods, and people who own vast tracts of land, fine clothes, horses, or — in our time — expensive cars. They look down on the poor, and even on other wealthy people who are less wealthy than themselves. Think of Leona Helmsley, who famously said that only the little people pay taxes. I once read a remarkable interview — I cannot recall exactly who it was — one of those figures this culture calls "beautiful people." They were being airlifted by private helicopter to a ski slope while a lodge for less-wealthy skiers sat below, and someone looked down and said, "Look at all the little people down there." At that very moment, the helicopter tipped and they crashed. The point is: she got the message. Some people would not have seen it that way. So be very careful about how you regard others, because we are all little people. The homeless man on the street looks up at the CEO and sees a little person; the CEO looks down at the homeless man and sees a little person. It is all perspective. That is why the مُتَكَبِّرٌٌ are transformed into ants on the Day of Judgment — because they looked at everyone else as ants to be crushed. It is a very dangerous thing.

<!-- page 194 -->

The sixth is كِبْرٌ بِالْقُوَّةِ — pride in physical strength. An example is Rukana al-Muttalibi, who challenged the Prophet ﷺ to a wrestling match. The Prophet threw him twice, and still he did not accept Islam. There is a narration suggesting he did eventually embrace Islam — إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ he did, though I cannot say with certainty. The point is that he had never been thrown by anyone, and that fed his arrogance. Such a person will inevitably grow old. The strength will leave him. He will no longer be able to do what he once could.

The seventh is تَكَبُّرٌ بِالْأَتْبَاعِ — pride derived from one's followers, supporters, students, and servants. A teacher who has many students may look down on a teacher with fewer. Someone with many powerful friends, influential relatives, or a connection to royalty — even if he is not the king himself — may feel superior because of that association. This is a very dangerous form of كِبْرٌ rooted in social standing and prestige.

And then there is تَكَبُّر through knowledge, which the imam identifies as among the most insidious forms of all. The reason is precisely that knowledge is so honored in the tradition. People naturally revere and defer to the learned. So when someone genuinely possesses knowledge — especially a significant amount of it — people show him respect, and that deference becomes dangerous for the heart. He begins to think, "I am somebody. I matter." This is what Shaytan does to him.

Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى says in the Quran: how has man been destroyed — what makes him such an ingrate? And then Allah says: let man think about what We created him from. He came from a fluid. He did not even exist before that fluid. He was not even a thought on anyone's tongue fifty years ago. He was nothing, nonexistent. Then he came into existence, and when he first arrived in this world, he needed his diaper changed seven or eight times a day — all he did was defecate, urinate, sleep, cry, and nurse.
