That is the human being. Then he grows, reaches the peak of his strength, and becomes arrogant. And then it reverses — because the trajectory is curvilinear. If you live long enough, you end up where you started. People reach that forty-year peak and think everything is settled, everything is fine. Then Allah says:

مِن نُّطْفَةٍ خَلَقَهُۥ فَقَدَّرَهُۥ

He created him from a نُطْفَة — an ejaculated fluid — and then measured out everything for him. Man did not arrange any of that himself. And then Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى says:

<!-- page 195 -->

أَوَلَمْ يَرَى الْإِنسَانِ أَنَّا خَلَقْنَاهُ مِنْ نُطْفَةٍ فَإِذَا هُوَ خَصِيمٌ مُبِينٌ

Has man not considered that We created him from a نُطْفَة, and now he has become an open adversary against Us? It is astonishing. Allah created the human being, and then that human being rises up in rebellion against his Creator — and he is nothing. Allah can stir up a hurricane — like Hurricane Mitch — and it is just a small wind in one corner of one planet in one solar system within a vast universe. It is nothing. And yet for those struck by it, entire homes, wealth, everything — gone. From a wind that Allah sent. And Allah says in the Quran that when people see such a wind approaching, they think it is a good wind bringing rain — and it turns out to be a torment.

The point of all of this is that we are simply foolish to be in rebellion against Allah. He gave us a tongue, two eyes, the capacity to think — all of these gifts. He guided us and showed us two roads, telling us plainly: at the end of this road is a painful punishment, and at the end of that road is a reward. Choose. And yet people choose the road of ruin, and at the root of that choice is تَكَبُّر.

Look at the Sira — I guarantee you, every single person who refused to submit, the reason was تَكَبُّر. It is unmistakable. The reason people cannot submit is تَكَبُّر.

Consider the prayer. There is a kind of servitude embedded in it that some people find deeply offensive. Many Western people struggle profoundly with the concept of being a slave of God — عَبْدُ اللهِ. They recoil from it: "I am free." And yet that same person is a slave to his desires, a slave to his appetite, a slave to his petty ambitions — completely unable to control them. He is enslaved to all of these things. But a slave to Allah? No. He will gladly be a slave to his own passions but not to Allah. It is a thoroughly delusional state.

<!-- page 196 -->

So the imam is saying that the way to uproot this disease is: first, to know your Lord, and second, to know yourself. That is, admittedly, an enormous undertaking. Some scholars say the only way to know your Lord is to know yourself — and Allah points us in that direction: *in yourselves, do you not see?* If you reflect deeply on the self, what you come to realize is that you are completely bound. Even عِيسَى ٱبْنَ مَرْيَمَ was not too proud to acknowledge that he was عَبْدُ اللهِ — he accepted it. And the Prophet ﷺ said, *أَنَا سَيِّدُ وَلَدِ آدَمَ وَلَا فَخْرَ* — I am the best of the children of Adam, and I say this without boasting. The ulama explain that he said this not out of pride, but because he was commanded by Allah to inform the Companions of his station — and precisely because the جَاهِلِي Arabs used boasting as an act of arrogance, he was careful to add *وَلَا فَخْرَ*, making clear this was not that. The ulama draw a further إِشَارَة from this: not only was there no فَخْر in it, but his true source of honor was his عُبُودِيَّة to Allah — his being the most perfect slave of Allah — not his being the master of creation. That is where his glory lay. This is why there is a حَدِيث — some scholars question its chain, but مُلَّا عَلِي الْقَارِي affirmed that its meaning is sound — that his empowerment before Allah was his glory.

So: know yourself, know your Lord — and whoever truly grasps both is humbled, feels insignificant, يَتَوَضَّأُ وَيَهُونُ. *مَنْ تَوَاضَعَ لِلَّهِ رَفَعَهُ اللَّهُ* — whoever humbles himself for the sake of Allah, Allah raises him up.

He also makes another point: *مَقَامُهُ يَنْفِي مَقَامَ الشُّكْرِ* — the station of كِبْرٌ makes genuine gratitude to Allah impossible. The way you show gratitude to Allah is through عُبُودِيَّة, and if you are مُتَكَبِّرٌ, you are not an عَبْد — because an عَبْد by his very nature cannot be مُتَكَبِّرٌ. That is what makes him an عَبْد. And if you are not a true عَبْد, you cannot be a شَكُورٌ. It is categorically impossible. Conversely, the nature of humility is that it leads you to gratitude, because when you are genuinely humbled before Allah, you see everything in its true proportion — you see how generous Allah has been, how much He has given you that you do not deserve. And that sense of being undeserving is precisely what generates gratitude. If you feel you deserve something, gratitude does not arise. As the Moroccans say: *لَا شُكْرَ عَلَى الْوَاجِبِ* — there is no thanks for what is owed. If you feel entitled, you simply think: *لَا، أَعْطَانِي مَا هُوَ حَقِّي* — he only gave me what was mine. But once you recognize you are undeserving, everything changes.

That is why he says: *حَقِّرْهُ* — see this towering mountain of كِبْرٌ for what it actually is: insignificant and meaningless. When you see it that way, you will bring it down. This is also why we say *اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ* — and traditionally, whenever Muslims saw something impressive in themselves or in others, they would refer it بَاck to its source: *اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ*. It is a constant reminder. *اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ مِنِّي، اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ مِنْكُمْ، اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ مِنَ الدُّنْيَا وَمَا فِيهَا* — Allah is greater than me, greater than you, greater than this world and all it contains. He is الْكَبِيرُ الْمُتَعَالِي. And it is to the مُتَعَالٍ that He says: *‏ذُقْ إِنَّكَ أَنتَ ٱلْعَزِيزُ ٱلْكَرِيمُ ‎* — now taste it; you are the عَزِيزٌ now. So taste the punishment. This is the تَحَكُّم of the Quran.

So the last topic remaining is ذُلّ and دَاءٌ — but we will need to prepare for that, so we will take it up next week, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ.

اللَّهُمَّ صَلِّ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَعَلَى آلِ سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ كَمَا صَلَّيْتَ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا إِبْرَاهِيمَ وَعَلَى آلِ سَيِّدِنَا إِبْرَاهِيمَ وَبَارِكْ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَعَلَى آلِ سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ كَمَا بَارَكْتَ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا إِبْرَاهِيمَ وَعَلَى آلِ سَيِّدِنَا إِبْرَاهِيمَ وَفِي الْعَالَمِينَ إِنَّكَ حَمِيدٌ مَجِيدٌ. اللَّهُمَّ لَا عِلْمَ إِلَّا مَا عَلَّمْتَنَا إِنَّكَ أَنْتَ الْحَكِيمُ الْعَلِيمُ.

اللَّهُمَّ عَلِّمْنَا مَا يَنْفَعُنَا وَانْفَعْنَا بِمَا عَلَّمْتَنَا وَزِدْنَا عِلْمًا. اللَّهُمَّ انْشُرْ عَلَيْنَا رَحْمَتَكَ وَانْشُرْ عَلَيْنَا حِكْمَتَكَ يَا ذَا الْجَلَالِ وَالْإِكْرَامِ. وَصَلَّى اللَّهُمَّ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَعَلَى آلِهِ وَصَحْبِهِ وَسَلَّمَ

اللَّهُمَّ عَلِّمْنَا مَا يَنْفَعُنَا وَانْفَعْنَا بِمَا عَلَّمْتَنَا وَزِدْنَا عِلْمًا. اللَّهُمَّ انْشُرْ عَلَيْنَا رَحْمَتَكَ وَانْشُرْ عَلَيْنَا حِكْمَتَكَ يَا ذَا الْجَلَالِ وَالْإِكْرَامِ. وَصَلَّى اللَّهُمَّ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَعَلَى آلِهِ وَصَحْبِهِ وَسَلَّمَ

---

Now, this section just covered brings us to the end of that class. We are nearly done with the first half of the book. The second half — which I mentioned to some of you last night — deals with the opposite: after treating the diseases of the heart, it moves to adorning the heart with the states and stations of certainty. We will إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ begin that in February.

We have been looking at فَخْر and كِبْرٌ — serious illnesses of the heart. One of the things worth noting here is the concept of the mean, what is called الْوَسَطِيَّة in Arabic. Virtues are means between two extremes — with one important exception: justice, which is simply the opposite of injustice. It has an opposite, but not two flanking extremes in the same way.

Now, the next disease the imam addresses is one that sits at the opposite extreme from كِبْرٌ. The virtue is humility. An excess of it — زِيَادَةٌ — slides into arrogance. A deficiency — نَقْص — slides into aبَاsement and self-humiliation, which is not a virtue in Islam. One might argue it is regarded as a virtue in certain strands of Christian thought, but in Islam it is not. The imam calls this ذُلّ.

In the Quran, Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى mentions that ذُلّ was among the afflictions visited upon Bani Israel as a consequence of their disobedience. So aبَاsement is often the result of disobedience — Allah afflicts people with it as a response to their arrogance. The sun never sets on the British Empire — and then it sets. The British lion becomes the tail of America. For Anglo-Saxons who always viewed the Americans as colonials — as the lesser people — those lesser people now dictate British foreign policy. That is what Allah does with peoples. And it will come to the Americans as well, just as it comes to everyone. Their turn is coming; there are already many signs of it.

So ذُلّ can be a social phenomenon afflicting an entire people. Many Muslim countries find themselves in this condition today. You will find Muslims who will do unbelievable things — lie, cheat, scheme — just to obtain a visa to a European country or to America. Inside their own countries, they conduct themselves in ways that are simply shocking in terms of بَاsic human dignity. All of it traces بَاck to this inner lowliness, this humiliation. And yet — despite that — you can also find a people crushed by great tribulations who nevertheless maintain their human dignity entirely. That is something else altogether.

*اَلْمُؤْمِنُ لَا يُذِلُّ نَفْسَهُ* — the believer never humiliates himself, never aبَاses himself. This is a حَدِيث of the Prophet ﷺ. So when we speak of humility, we do not mean humiliation. You humble yourself; you do not humiliate yourself.

He then mentions: *أَذَلُّ وَبَاعَ* — to be lowly, to be brought low. And the Prophet ﷺ said: nothing raises itself except that Allah will aبَاse it. That is the sunna of Allah in His creation. If you elevate yourself, Allah will aبَاse you. These are wisdoms to be treasured.

Before Allah, you should have ذُلّ. Before Allah you should be ذَلِيل — you should carry a sense of ذُلِّ وَالْهَوَانِ وَبَاءَ. That is the proper orientation before Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى. This is why Sidi Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani said that he looked upon all the doors leading to Allah and found them crowded — except one: the door of ذُلٌّ وَتَوَاضُعٌ, humility and lowliness. And so he called out to people: *هَلُمَّ ارْعَ رَبَّكُمْ* — come, you want to reach your Lord? This is the door. No one is there. It is hard on the نَفْس, which is precisely why the path is empty.

He then warns against the opposite failure: do not aبَاse yourself before creation. In fact, he says: *وَاكْبُرْ عَلَى الْغَنِيِّ الْمُسْتَكْبِرِ* — hold yourself with dignity before the rich and arrogant. It has been said that among the noble things of this world is a *غَنِيٌّ مُتَوَاضِعٌ* — a wealthy man who is humble. That is genuine nobility. And *شَرِيفٌ سُنِّيٌّ* — a شَرِيف who follows the Sunna. These are among the things mentioned as truly distinguished. But the *غَنِيُّ الْمُسْتَكْبِرِ* — the arrogant rich man — is unfortunately the norm.

*إِنَّ ٱلْإِنسَنَ لَيَطْغَىٰٓ* — *أَن رَّآهُ ٱسْتَغْنَىٰٓ* — Haven't you seen? Man transgresses when he deems himself self-sufficient. When he becomes wealthy, he imagines he has no need of anyone — and that imagined independence causes him to overstep the boundaries of Allah.

<!-- page 246 -->
And that is why, like Nimrod — because Allah gave him dominion over the earth, he thought he was God. He argued with Ibrahim. And this is what wealthy people often do: they think that because they have wealth, it gives them the right to hold opinions on everything, to be an expert on everything. It is a serious matter.

So he said: *التَّكَبُّرُ فِي وَجْهِ الْمُتَكَبِّرٌِ عِبَادَةٌ* — it is a حَدِيث, and in one رِوَايَة the word *عِبَادَة* appears. That is the second person.

## Displeasure with Blame

The next disease is *كَرَاهَةُ الذَّمِّ* — dislike of blame. *ذِمّ* means to blame someone. By human nature, we do not like to be blamed; it is something inherent in us. We teach this to our children early on: we praise them for certain things and blame them for others, and they quickly learn that praise is pleasant — it comes with smiles, gifts, warmth, hugs — while blame is painful, accompanied by frowns, anger, raised voices, and sometimes physical punishment. So from very early on, children learn to want praise and to dislike blame. The problem is that this natural aversion becomes a disease: *كَرَاهِيَةُ ذَمٍّ*.

He says: *كَرَاهَةُ ذَمِّ بَنَانٍ مَأْلُوفٍ* — it is a well-known disease. *وَالنَّظَرُ إِلَى الْعِبَادِ وَالْوُقُوفُ مَعَهُمْ حِجَابٌ عَنْ مَقَامِ الْإِحْسَانِ* What he is saying is that displeasure with blame is a well-known disease of the heart. Preoccupation with what people think of you — craving their praise and resenting their criticism — becomes a barrier between you and the station of إِحْسَان, because your actions begin to be driven by the desire to be praised or the fear of being blamed.

<!-- page 248 -->
One example from the seerah class: Sa'd ibn Mu'adh رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ. When the Prophet ﷺ asked the Aus to accept his judgment regarding Bani Quraydah — since Bani Quraydah were confederates of the Aus, and the Khazraj had secured the release of Bani Nadir through the intercession of Abdullah ibn Ubayy, the Aus wanted the same outcome for their confederates. The Prophet ﷺ put the matter to Sa'd. Now, what Bani Quraydah had done was actually far graver than what Bani Nadir had done — they had joined the enemy army during the siege. Bani Nadir had attempted to assassinate the Prophet ﷺ, which is a more serious crime in the larger picture, but the treachery of Bani Quraydah during battle was of a different order. In any case, when Sa'd came forward, people were urging him: please intercede with the Prophet, get them off just as Bani Nadir were spared. And he said — and this is the important point — that he had reached a place in his *مَوْقِف* where he had no fear of the blame of any blamer. He was near death. He said: *الْآنَ وَجَدْتُ نَفْسِي فِي مَقَامٍ لَا أَخَافُ فِيهِ لَوْمَةَ لَائِمٍ* — I have found myself in a position where I am not concerned with what people will say.

What the Aus were worried about was public perception: our confederates were all killed while the Khazraj got theirs off. Sa'd ibn Mu'adh said: I am about to meet my Lord. I am not concerned with what people will say.

<!-- page 249 -->
The point is that, in reality, you are in that very moment at every instant of your life. Death is simply veiled from you — that veil is *طُولُ الْأَمَلِ*, which is another disease in itself. It makes you think you will be around. But that is a complete illusion; you have no guarantees whatsoever. So what the example of Sa'd illustrates is *مَقَامُ الْإِحْسَانِ* — all he could see was Allah. Creation did not enter into it.

That is the whole point of *كَرَاهِيَةُ الذَّمِّ*: when you start worrying about what others will think, it often prevents you from doing the right thing — because very often, the right thing is precisely what everyone else will oppose. That is the nature of this world. Taking a courageous stance usually means taking a stance you will be blamed for. And courageous positions are often only recognized much later.

Geronimo is a good example. When he was fighting the US cavalry, he was portrayed as an evil, horrific barbarian. Now he is seen as a hero by both whites and Native Americans alike — a great freedom fighter defending his land. At the time, even his own people turned against him; most had already surrendered, and he kept fighting until he was finally betrayed by those around him. Malcolm X is another example. Once enough time passes and a person is no longer perceived as a threat, the very forces that opposed him will co-opt his legacy — that is what they do. But at the time, he was taking positions that virtually the whole of society was against. Had he been worried about their opinion, he could never have spoken the truth. That is what happens when people are consumed by concern for what others think: they lose the ability to take the courageous positions that are unpopular precisely because they are true, and that attract attack for that very reason.

Then he says that overcoming this barrier comes through the realization that there is no benefit or harm except from Allah. This is, as you will notice, a recurring cure for many of these diseases — genuinely recognizing two names of Allah: *النَّافِعُ وَالضَّارُّ*. *النَّافِع* is the One who benefits, and *الضَّرُّ* is the One who harms. These are attributes of Allah alone; no human being possesses them. Only Allah can truly benefit, and only Allah can truly harm. So if concern about what people think is preventing you from doing what you should do for the sake of Allah, you are not seeing reality. You are in a delusional state — one that is not only harmful to your iman but is simply out of accord with how things actually are.

This is why the Prophet ﷺ said to Ibn Abbas رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ, giving him this counsel: *إذا استعنت فاستعن بالله* — when you seek help, seek it only from Allah. *وإذا سألت فاسأل الله* — and when you ask, ask only from Allah. And then he said: know that even if all of creation were to gather together to harm you, they could not harm you except with what Allah has decreed for you. And if they gathered to benefit you, they could not benefit you except with what Allah has decreed. Creation can do nothing to you — they can neither harm you nor benefit you.

Now, this obviously does not mean ignoring the *أَسْبَاب* — the Prophet ﷺ wore two coats of chain armor at the Battle of Uhud. Those are *أَسْبَاب*. He knew better than anyone that no harm and no benefit come except from Allah, yet that did not negate using one's intellect and engaging with the world of *أَسْبَاب*. It also does not negate diplomacy, because as the poet said: *لَقَدْ خُطَّهَا حَقٌّ وَلَٰكِنْ مَا كُلُّ حَقٍّ يُقَالُ* — I said the truth, but not every truth needs to be said. There are times when discretion is the wiser course.

I know of a Libyan man — an Azhari graduate — who was part of an official delegation in Iraq at a large gathering. Someone was making a toast to the Ba'ath Party, and this man stood up and said: *لَعَنَ اللَّهُ حِزْبَكَ وَمَنْ دَعَا لَهُ* — may Allah curse your party and those who pray for it — and he went on cursing. The rest of the delegation were horrified and tried to cover for him, telling the hosts he was not quite right in the mind and not to pay him any attention. In the end, nothing happened to them. But the point is: he probably should not have been there supporting those people in the first place, and the outburst, however sincere, was not the most sagacious move.

*كَلِمَةُ حَقٍّ أَمَامَ سُلْطَانٍ جَائِرٍ* — speaking the truth in the face of an oppressive tyrant — the Prophet ﷺ called that the greatest jihad, and if you die for it, you are a shaheed without question. But that does not mean every moment calls for it. If there is long-term benefit to be weighed, if this is a situation where *الْحَرْبُ خُدْعَةٌ* — war is strategy — then you have to measure things carefully.

Then he says: *يَسْتَمِدُّ الْحَرَامُ مِنْهُمَا جَرَّاءَ إِرَاءٍ مُحَرَّمٍ كَمَا الْغَزَّالِيُّ فَصَّلَ الْغَزَّالُ* — the impermissible dimension of this disease is what leads in turn to the prohibited, as Imam al-Ghazali has clarified. In other words, while not liking to be blamed is natural, what becomes haram is whatever that aversion leads you to do that is itself haram. For instance: if you are obliged to enjoin the right and forbid the wrong, but you hold back out of worry about what people will say — out of fear of *لَوْمَةُ لَائِمٍ*, the blame of the blamer — and that fear causes you to abandon an obligation, that is where the disease becomes sinful. Or, say you are at work and it is time to pray, but you start thinking about what your co-workers will think if you ask to step out — because praying at work is not exactly popular in America. If that concern stops you from praying, that is clearly *كَرَاهِيَةُ الذَّمِّ* in a form that is not acceptable. Being concerned about people's opinion when doing something genuinely wrong is natural and appropriate. The disease — the haram — is when that concern prevents you from fulfilling your obligations.

<!-- page 253 -->
Another important point about this disease: real iman does not become established in the heart until, as the tradition of Ibn Mas'ud states, *لَا يُؤْمِنُ أَحَدُكُمْ حَتَّى يَسْتَوِيَ عِنْدَهُ الذَّمُّ وَالْمَدْحُ* — blame and praise become equal to him. That is, you are acting for Allah, and you genuinely do not care what people think. If someone praises you — fine. If someone blames you — fine. You did it for the sake of Allah, and Allah knows that. That is what you are concerned with: not the eyes of others. That is the perfection of sincerity.

On a related note: one of the things I observed in West Africa that struck me as genuinely wise was that in the desert tradition, they never have young children perform displays of their Quran memorization — no "come on, let's hear you recite this" followed by everyone applauding. They do not put children on display like that. I think that practice, however well-intentioned it may seem, is actually harmful, because it plants the early roots of رِيَاءٌ — the desire for praise, for showing off. Parents naturally want to show their children off, and that desire is understandable.

<!-- page 254 -->
Even Sayyidina Umar رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ — when Ibn Umar was in a gathering and the Prophet ﷺ asked which tree resembles the believer, standing firm in the desert, and everyone started guessing — the Qalha, the Zainab, all sorts of trees — Abdullah ibn Umar, who was a young boy sitting among the great صَحَابَة, immediately thought: it is the palm tree. But he was too shy to speak up in front of Abu Bakr, Umar, Uthman, and the senior companions. So he stayed quiet. When no one got the answer, the Prophet ﷺ told them it was the palm tree, and afterward Ibn Umar told his father that he had known. Umar said: I would have loved for you to have said it — he would have been proud for his son to have spoken up in that gathering.

## Antipathy Toward Death

The next disease is antipathy towards death: *كَرَاهِيَةُ الْمَوْتِ*. He says about it: *كَرَاهَةُ الْمَوْتِ بِحَيْثُ يَنْفِرُ مِنْهُ وَيَأْنَفُ إِذَا مَا يُذْكَرْ، حَتَّى كَأَنَّهُ بِذَوْقِ كُلِّ نَفْسٍ لَهُ الَّذِي أَتَى ذُو الْجَهْلِ* — antipathy towards death, this dislike of it, is when one flees from it and becomes annoyed whenever it is mentioned, as if he is entirely ignorant of Allah's words: *كُلُّ نَفْسٍۢ ذَآئِقَةُ ٱلْمَوْتِ* — every soul shall taste death.

Death is our inevitable end in this world. Each of us will taste it. This dislike of death he is describing is the kind where one runs from it.

<!-- page 255 -->
*‏قُلْ إِنَّ ٱلْمَوْتَ ٱلَّذِى تَفِرُّونَ مِنْهُ فَإِنَّهُۥ مُلَقِيكُمْ ۖ ثُمَّ تُرَدُّونَ إِلَىٰ عَلِمِ ٱلْغَيْبِ وَٱلشَّهَدَةِ فَيُنَبِّئُكُم بِمَا كُنتُمْ تَعْمَلُونَ ‎* — say: this death you are fleeing from will certainly meet you, and then you will be returned to the Knower of the seen and the unseen, who will inform you of what you used to do.

Death is inevitable, and fleeing from it is futile. Some people observe that this culture is a death-denial culture. Muslims are less afflicted by this than non-Muslims — non-Muslims actively avoid mentioning death, find it uncomfortable in conversation, consider it almost bad manners to bring it up. I was once in a federal building getting a passport, riding up in an elevator with two men who looked like bureaucrats. One said to the other: I'm just waiting for that guy to die — he's about to drop dead any minute, and I'm really looking forward to it. The other asked why. He said: it'll just be nice to be in a world without him. Then the second one said: maybe we'll die before him. And the first said: no, no, he's almost gone.

That exchange is a revealing example of death denial in action — death is only something that happens to sick people, to old people, to the man on his deathbed. The healthy, the young — they operate as though death simply does not apply to them.

You know, what if we're in Los Angeles and an earthquake strikes? There is a deep غَفْلَة that people fall into regarding death — a profound heedlessness. They don't truly believe they are going to die.

<!-- page N -->

And if they do acknowledge it, it's mere lip service: *Oh, that's a long way off.* So when someone hears death mentioned and it genuinely disturbs them — *I don't want to talk about that. Let's change the subject. This is depressing. This is morbid* — that attitude is foreign to our tradition.

The Prophet ﷺ once passed by a group of people laughing in a gathering and said, "Mix your gatherings with remembrance of the destroyer of pleasures." In other words, the remembrance of death brings a kind of sobriety and gravity to a gathering that has become too frivolous — not that people cannot enjoy themselves, but gatherings can drift into pure levity with no substance and no تَطْهِير. That is precisely what the Prophet ﷺ was warning against: sitting in مَجْلِسُ الْغَفْلَةِ.

The Prophet ﷺ also said that there is no hour in this life you will regret in the آخِرَة except one spent without remembering Allah. And in another narration, he said that anyone who sits in a gathering and then rises from it without having remembered Allah is like one who rises from a dead carcass — جِفَاءً, carrion. We were created to worship Allah, and we are naturally drawn to intimacy and companionship, so our gatherings should not be purely for entertainment. They should carry some purpose: discussing matters of importance, making دُعَاء for one another, and so forth.

Then the author states that this — hating death — is counted among the diseases of the heart, and he says: be content with what Allah the Exalted has decreed for you. If you are in a state of genuine acceptance of death, it will not disturb you — and accepting death is accepting the decree of Allah. One shaykh once told me in a gathering: "We are all on death row. We are simply waiting for the executioner." اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ. We have all been condemned to death; we are just waiting. But once you realize that the One who condemned you to death is the very One who gave you life in the first place, you can be content — because He has done it for a wisdom.

From another perspective, death is a celebration for the believer. Shaykh Ibn al-Habib said that in death, for the مُؤْمِن, there is a thousand-fold repose. He said that as long as you are in this world, there is not a single cell in your body that cannot suffer pain or disease. Once you leave this world, all of that ends. There is a رَهَا in death. He also said on another occasion that death for the مُؤْمِن is like being moved from the third-class compartment to first class — the conductor comes and says, "No, no, you shouldn't be here. Come up to first class." And do you say, "No, no, I want to stay in third class"? Of course not. It is like being bumped up to first class on an airplane. You're not upset. الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ. For the مُؤْمِن, death is not a bad thing.

That is also why our mourning period is only three days. I had a professor who taught a course called Death and Dying, and he said that one of the worst things about this country is the mourning industry — that it does people a disservice by prolonging and commercializing grief. Many people go into mourning for years and spend years in therapy trying to recover. All of that is love of the دُنْيَا and attachment to it. The people of Allah, when they accept Allah's decree — if Allah takes a beloved person, a child, anyone — خَلَاصٌ. It is painful; the Prophet felt pain and wept. But it was Allah who did it, and we must be pleased with what Allah does. That is why the مُؤْمِن is able to pass through such trials.

And there is something remarkable about Muslims in this regard. No people — as far as I know, certainly not the Europeans — can face death the way Muslims can. When calamities befall Muslims, you see their true character. When the same calamities befall non-Muslims, many fall apart, some take their own lives. Muslims, because of their strength with Allah and their divine connection, are able to do what others cannot — including face death.

Maurice Bucaille, the French physician, said that one of the things that first drew him toward Islam was watching how Algerians died in France compared to the French. He was profoundly impressed by the way North Africans faced death. Having worked on hospital wards myself where people were dying, I can say: you see people in this culture who simply do not know how to die — they have no idea. But watch a devout Muslim, and you will see someone who has been preparing for death his entire life. When it comes, he is ready.

Shaykh Uthman Bashir Uthman, رَحِمَ اللهُ, was a close friend of mine. He was in his early eighties, had grown up in Eritrea, and eventually came to the United States. I told him about Darul Islam and he said we had to go there. We went together — I came along because he couldn't speak Arabic — and afterward he returned to Medina, where he died shortly after. He told me that he had always known he would die in Medina, because when he was a young man in Eritrea, his shaykh had told him: "There will be great فِتْنَةٌ in this country. The communists will take over and you will have to flee. But Allah, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, will open a place for you in Medina. You will live there, die there, and be buried in بَقِيعٌ." I heard that from his own lips.

This is what they call كَشْف — something that happens for certain people of Allah. It is not revelation; rather, Allah grants them a kind of opening through which they perceive things and can sometimes convey them to others. At the time, he had always assumed his shaykh was simply saying kind words. But sure enough, the communists took over. He was a wealthy businessman and had to flee. A place was opened for him in Medina; he received citizenship there and lived his remaining years traveling in and out. He told me, "I truly feel, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, that I will die in Medina."

When the time came, they had him in a hospital in Jeddah. He had cancer. He was very weak, hooked up to equipment, barely able to move. Then one day he told his son, "We have to go to Medina today." The doctor said he absolutely could not leave. He said, "I am leaving today. My son will drive me." The doctor was adamant — he was too ill, he needed to stay. He said no. They took him that night, drove all the way to Medina. He arrived, spent the entire night saying لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ. Just before فَجْر, he told his son a few things to convey to certain people, then said the شَهَادَة and died. He was buried in بَقِيعٌ, in the old section next to Imam Malik and Nafi', in a spot where people are no longer usually buried — but when they arrived, they found a grave already dug, and they placed him in it. His جَنَازَة prayer was held on جُمُعَة at the Prophet's Mosque ﷺ, and he was the only person that day with a جَنَازَة there.

I tell that story because it is an example of how Muslims who know how to die, die.

There is also a story — and Allah knows its full truth — told about Farid al-Din al-Attar, who was a perfumer. The account of how he made his تَوْبَة goes like this: a man once came begging for alms, and al-Attar told him to go get a job. The man asked what he did for a living. Al-Attar said he sold perfumes. The man replied, "The most important thing you will ever do is die. And I know how to do that." Then he said, *أَشْهَدُ أَنْ لَا إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا اللهُ، أَشْهَدُ أَنَّ مُحَمَّدًا رَسُولُ اللهِ* — and died. Whether or not the story is literally true, there is a truth within it. There really are people who know how to die. When the moment comes, they know exactly what to do. And there are others who do not — when the time comes, they fall into complete confusion.

This is one of the blessings of remembering death often. Our scholars, following the Prophet ﷺ based on his حَدِيث on the remembrance of death, have also recommended passing through the states of the آخِرَة in one's mind: visualizing the moment of dying, being washed, being wrapped, being placed in the grave, having the earth cover you, being questioned by the angels, the resurrection, the مِيزَان. To reflect on those stages as a spiritual practice. It is a deeply sobering exercise — one that can rouse you from the sleep we are all walking through in this life.

The author then adds: أَمَّا إِذَا قَلَاهُ لَا لِذَاتِهِ وَلَا لَلْإِنْسَرَامِ عَنْ لَذَّاتِهِ بَالْخَوْفَ قَطْعِهِ عَنْ إِسْتِعْدَادِهِ بِطَاعَةِ اللَّهِ إِلَىٰ مَعَادِهِ — if someone dislikes death not merely because it is death, and not because it will sever him from the pleasures of the دُنْيَا, but out of fear that he has not had enough time to prepare for what lies ahead, then that is a legitimate reason. The Prophet ﷺ encouraged people to desire a long life on that basis: if you are a person who has done wrong, you have time to make amends; if you are a person doing good, you have more time to increase your good deeds and strengthen your صَحِيفَة — your scroll — for the يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ. He mentioned that طُولُ الْأَمَلِ, desiring a long life, is a good thing when one is engaged in righteous deeds.

The Prophet ﷺ also said to Aisha رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهَا: رضي الله عنه من أحب الله أحب لقاءه — whoever loves Allah loves to meet Allah. She responded, يا رسول الله الموت نكره الموت — O Messenger of Allah, we dislike death. The Prophet ﷺ indicated that this was natural — but said that what truly mattered was the dislike of the آخِرَة itself, of meeting Allah.

The point is that disliking death is obviously natural. Allah has placed it in our bodies at a cellular level. That is why, when life is threatened, you enter a heightened state of functioning and do things not consciously chosen in order to protect yourself or others. Allah has placed that in us and it is a good thing. That is not the dislike being addressed here. The Quran describes Ibrahim ﷺ: when he offered the angels food and they did not eat, فَأَوْجَسَ مِنْهُمْ خِيفَةًۭ — he felt a twinge of fear, a natural human response. That kind of instinctive fear is not what is being criticized.

Then the author says: أَوْ فَوَّضَ الْأَمْرَ إِلَى مَوْلَاهُ فَمَا يَشَاءُ أَرْضَهُ أَوَبْقَاهُ — or, if he fully entrusts his affair to his Master, and whatever Allah wills — whether to take him or to grant him more time — contents him. In other words, you are simply at peace with whatever Allah chooses to do with you. Both of these stances are good stances.

That is why the author says: فَذَانِ مَمْدُوحَانِ مَحْمُودَانِ وَالْكُرْهُ لَا يُبَعِّضُ مِنْ كَدَّانِ — both attitudes toward death are commendable and praiseworthy. A dislike of death in no way distances you from its proximity. Even if you dislike it, that dislike changes nothing — it will not push death any further away. It is far better, for the sake of your own inner state, to accept it.

Then the author says: ذَاكِرُهُ يُكْرَمُ بِالْقَنَاعَةٌِ وَبِنَشَاطِ قَلْبِهِ لِلطَّاعَةِ — the one who remembers death often is honored with certain qualities, among them قَنَاعَةٌ. Because when you genuinely believe you could die at any moment, there is a natural inclination to be present with what you have rather than perpetually deferring enjoyment to some imagined future when you'll "finally get it together" — which, as they say, never actually happens.

Contentment is vital. The Prophet ﷺ said: القَنَاعَةٌُ كَنزٌ لَا يَفْنَى — contentment is a treasure that is never exhausted. And he prayed: اللَّهُمَّ ارْزُقْ آلَ مُحَمَّدٍ الْقَنَاعَةٌَ وَالْكَفَافَ — O Allah, grant the family of Muhammad contentment and sufficiency. Just give them enough to live on and make them content with it.

The قُنُوعٌ — the person in a state of قَنَاعَةٌ — is the only truly wealthy person. A poor man who is content is in reality a rich man. A rich man who is discontented is in reality a poor man. Because فَقْرٌ is want — and قَنَاعَةٌ is the absence of want. If you have no want, you are wealthy, because wealth is defined by the absence of need. If you have no need, you are rich. That is why the Prophet ﷺ said: الْغِنَى غِنَى النَّفْسِ — real wealth is the wealth of the soul. لَيْسَ الْغِنَى عَنْ كَثْرَةِ الْعَرَضِ — it is not having an abundance of things. وَإِنَّمَا الْغِنَى غِنَى النَّفْسِ — real wealth is the contentment of the soul.

And so when you remember death, Allah grants you قَنَاعَ — contentment — as one of its blessings. This is all the more significant in an age when so many false needs and wants are being constantly planted in people's hearts. That is precisely what media does: يَغْرِسُ — it sows the seeds of طَمَعٌ, of desire, of شَهْوَةٌ, embedding all of these things deep within.

He also says that remembering death gives you energy — it directs the heart's activity toward obedience. Thinking about death makes you want to be more obedient to Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, producing a kind of نَشَاط, an energized readiness to do good deeds. وَبِبِدَارِي تَوْبَةٌٍ وَيُبْتَرَى.

Beyond that, remembering death moves you to hurry toward تَوْبَةٌ. If a slip occurs and you do something wrong, the awareness of death prompts you to make تَوْبَةٌ quickly — to rectify the wrong. تَوْبَةٌ corrects bad actions, and that is itself a gift that comes from remembering death. When you do something wrong and then reflect, سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ, I am accountable for this — أَسْتَغْفِرُ اللَّهَ — that awareness drives you to seek Allah's forgiveness سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى.

The opposite holds equally true. The one who forgets death receives the three opposites of these gifts. He will always want more and will never attain قَنَاعَةٌ. He will يُرْسِلُ جَوَارِحَهُ فِي المَعْصِيَة. — give free rein to his limbs in disobedience — because death and the يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ and the حِسَابٌ are not on his mind. And when he does wrong, he feels no remorse, because he is not concerned with what awaits him in the Akhirah.

## Obliviousness to Blessings

The next disease is one of the most important in the entire book, because شُكَر — gratitude — is truly essential to the deen of Islam. One of the meanings of the Arabic word كَافِرٌ is كُفْرَانُ النِّعْمَةِ: the denial or concealment of blessing. This is precisely why a Muslim can fall into a form of كَفَّرَ, for there is كُفْرٌ دُونَ الْكُفْرِ — a كَفَّرَ that falls short of leaving Islam altogether. The Prophet ﷺ warned women, for instance, against كُفْرَانُ الْمُعَاشَرَةِ — being ungrateful for the companionship of marriage, failing to recognize the نِعْمَةٌ of a husband who provides protection and care.

The next disease, then, is called oblivion to blessings. مِن عُيُوبِ النَّفْسِ نِسْيَانُ النِّعَم — and the root of it is غَفْلَةٌ, heedlessness toward the blessings one has been given. The Quran states: وَمَا بِكُم مِّن نِّعْمَةٍۢ فَمِنَ ٱللَّهِ — every blessing you possess is from Allah. People often say, count your blessings — but those who actually do so are rare. And Allah Himself declares: وَإِن تَعُدُّوا۟ نِعْمَةَ ٱللَّهِ لَا تُحْصُوهَآ — if you try to enumerate the blessings of Allah, you will never encompass them.

<!-- page 208 -->

In light of this, he says: وَبِدَوَامِ ذِكْرِهَا — keep this verse ever before you: وَمَا بِكُم مِّن نِّعْمَةٍۢ فَمِنَ ٱللَّهِ. The Quran opens with بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ, and some of the مُفَسِّرُونَ explain that الرَّحْمَٰنُ refers to the giver of جَلَائِلَ النِّعَمِ — the great, obvious blessings — المُنْعِمِ بِجَلَائِلِ النِّعَمِ — while الرَّحِيمِ refers to المُنْعِمِ بِدَقَائِقِ النِّعَمِ — the giver of subtle, hidden blessings.

The blessings of الرَّحْمَٰنُ are easy to recognize: health, for instance, is plain enough. Tell someone, "Thank God you're healthy," and they immediately agree — الحمد لله على الصحة والعافية الحمد لله على الإيمان. But then there are دَقَائِقُ النِّعَمِ — the subtle, intricate blessings. Consider the eye: it is constantly lubricated. Some people must use artificial drops because they suffer from dry eye, and when the eyes are dry, every blink — which normally cleans and protects the eye — becomes painful. Those eyelashes, that rapid blinking reflex that keeps even a stray hair from causing harm, the sockets that set the eye slightly back within the skull to shield it from blunt injury — all of these are hidden blessings most people never consider. People who cannot produce tears must use drops to relieve the pain. People who cannot close their eyelids, or who lose their eyelids entirely, exist — and only then does one grasp what had been taken for granted.

Or consider the eye's capacity for color — the full range of the visible world it opens to you. Then consider balance: people who sustain damage to the eighth cranial nerve in the ear begin to lose their balance and cannot remain upright. Balance itself is a نِعْمَةٌ from Allah. The way you eat — the digestive juices working silently within you, breaking down food and separating its components — these are hidden نَعَمْ you are entirely unaware of. The cerebellum, and the whole unconscious operation of the brain. Breathing: you do not have to remember to breathe. These are all نَعَمْ Allah has granted you, and most of the time you are not even conscious of them.

<!-- page 209 -->

Take even the thumb. Other animals lack the opposable thumb. Allah says قَدْ كَرَّمْنَا بَنِي آدَمَ — We have ennobled the children of Adam — and some scholars say one dimension of that ennobling was the thumb. Tellingly, the Arabic word for thumb is إِبْهَام, from the same root as مُبْهَم — something enigmatic, something strange — because no other creature has it. The thumb is a tremendous نِعْمَةٌ from Allah. Conquerors used to cut off their enemies' thumbs so they could never fight again — lose your thumb, and you lose most of your capability.

Then consider all the pleasures Allah has given you. Taste buds are a نِعْمَةٌ. Allah could have made your food like rocks and your water brackish. He says exactly that in the Quran — He could have made your water unpleasant to drink and you would still have needed it. But He made it sweet. He could have made all food taste the same, yet He gave lemons to those who love sour, and honey to those who love sweet, and then gave you the taste buds capable of savoring every one of these different things.

All of this points to the importance of reflecting on the نَعَمْ. Imam al-Ghazali says: look at your food. Allah says that in the Quran — فَلْيَنظُرِ ٱلْإِنسَنُ إِلَىٰ طَعَامِهِۦٓ — إِنَّا صَبَبْنَا الْمَاءَ صَبًّا ثُمَّ شَقَقْنَا الْأَرْضَ شَقًّا — We poured down rain, then split the earth open. You can see it happen: when the rain reaches the seed, اخْتَلَطَ بِهِ نَبَاتُ الْأَرْضِ — it mingles with what is in the earth, and then the earth breaks open. فَالِقُ ٱلْحَبِّ وَٱلنَّوَىٰ — Allah is the Splitter; He causes the earth to burst forth. So Allah says: فَلْيَنظُرِ ٱلْإِنسَنُ إِلَىٰ طَعَامِهِۦٓ — إِنَّا صَبَبْنَا الْمَاءَ صَبًّا شَقَقْنَا الْأَرْضَ شَقًّا فَأَنبَتْنَا فِيهَا حَبًّا وَعِنَبًا وَقَبًّا وَزَيْتُونًا — the grapes, the olives, the dates, the grain, the fodder for animals from which we benefit in turn. And there is إِبًّا in there too — the pasture that animals eat, from which we benefit indirectly: the animals consume it, and we eat the meat, receiving that nourishment at one remove. Allah is telling us: look at these things, reflect on them, and recognize that all of it is from Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى. The more you do that, the less inclined you will be to forget the نِعْمَةٌ of Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, and the more you will honor it.

<!-- page 210 -->

The importance of this cannot be overstated. He says: وَذِكْرَ الْآيَاتِ الْمُرْجِفَةِ غَيْرِهَا — keep before you the other verses that should put fear in the heart. إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَا يُغَيِّرُ مَا بِقَوْمٍ حَتَّىٰ يُغَيِّرُوا۟ مَا بِأَنفُسِهِمْ — Allah does not change the blessings of a people and turn them into their opposites until the people themselves change, by failing to show gratitude for them. That is how the scholars interpret this verse. Allah gives you a نِعْمَةٌ and will not take it away until you show ingratitude for it. Allah says: ذَلِكَ بِأَنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَمْ يَكُ مُغَيِّرًۭا نِّعْمَةً أَنْعَمَهَا عَلَىٰ قَوْمٍ حَتَّىٰ يُغَيِّرُوا۟ مَا بِأَنفُسِهِمْ — that is because Allah will not change the blessing He has bestowed upon a people until they change themselves through ingratitude.

A poet captured this perfectly: إِن كُنتَ فِي نِعْمَةٍ فَارْعَهَا / إِنَّ الْمَعَاصِيَ تُزِيلُ النِّعَمَ — if you are in a state of نِعْمَةٌ, guard it, for disobedience strips نِعْمَةٌ away. Disobedience to Allah costs you your blessings. This is why Waki', when he was old and still remarkably agile, was asked the secret of it, and he replied: ثِرْكَ الْجَوَارِحْ حَفِظْنَاهَا لِلَّهِ فِي صِغَرِنَا فَحَافَظَهَا لَنَا فِي كِبَرِنَا — these are limbs we guarded for Allah's sake when we were young, so Allah has guarded them for our sake now that we are old. If you protect the نِعْمَةٌ Allah has given you, that is how you maintain it. قَيْدُ النِّعَمِ الشُّكْرُ — the thing that binds نِعْمَةٌ to you is gratitude. And Allah has announced: لَىِٕن شَكَرْتُمْ لَأَزِيدَنَّكُمْ — if you are grateful for My blessings, I will increase you in them. تَأَذَّنَ رَبُّكُمْ — your Lord has declared it.

So oblivion to نِعْمَةٌ is a disaster — a genuine disaster. And related to this is what is known as اِسْتِدْرَاج: Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى allows someone to continue in disobedience while continuing to increase their blessings, so that the person concludes that Allah must be pleased with them. This is the error the Quran describes: a man given abundance says رَبِّي أَكْرَمَنِي — my Lord has honored me. But when things become tight, he says رَبِّي أَهَانَنِي — my Lord has humiliated me, has demeaned me. People read ease as a sign that Allah loves them, and hardship as a sign that Allah hates them. This is a profound mistake.

<!-- page 211 -->

This brings us to an important distinction: what actually is a نِعْمَةٌ? How do we identify one? Consider the following categories. There are things that benefit you both in the short term and the long term — عِلْمٌ and حُسْنُ الْخُلُقِ, good character, are examples. Something that benefits you in your دُنْيَا and your akhirah is a pure, unadulterated نِعْمَةٌ. Then there are things that harm you in both the short term and the long term — ignorance and سُوءُ الخُلُقِ, bad character, are examples: ضَرَرٌ فِي الحَالِ وَالمَآلِ.. Then there are things beneficial in the short term but harmful in the long term — شَهَوَاتٌ often fall into this category. Overeating, for instance: enjoyable while it is happening, but harmful in the long run — plain and simple. Beneficial short term, harmful long term. And then there is the opposite: things that are difficult in the short term but beneficial in the long term, like restraining the شَهَوَاتٌ. Stopping before you are full is hard — يَضُرُّكَ فِي الحَالِ — you do not enjoy it in the moment, but the long-term benefit is real.

The ignorant person mistakes the third category — short-term benefit with long-term harm — for a genuine نِعْمَةٌ, and is drawn to it. This is, at its root, a failure of intelligence. The difference between an intelligent person and an ignorant one is that the intelligent person always looks at the عَاقِبَةٌ — the long-term consequence. Children cannot do this naturally. There is a well-known study that illustrates this beautifully: a child is brought into a room with a cookie on the table. The researcher says, "You can have one cookie now, or if you wait, I will give you two when I return." Consistently, the more intelligent children chose to wait — willing to forgo the short-term reward for the greater long-term gain. That is intelligence. And that is precisely what Islam teaches: الْعَاقِبَةُ لِلْمُتَّقِينَ — the ultimate outcome belongs to the God-fearing. The disbelievers think in terms of the short term, the present quarter. The Muslims are meant to think long term.

There is a worldly example of this too. The Jews, for instance, have historically been skilled at long-term thinking — at planning for future benefit. This stands in painful contrast to where many Muslims find themselves today. Many Muslims are dangerously short-sighted, which is a sign of a loss of the intelligence we once had — intelligence that came from guidance. Because Allah does not grant us تَوْفِيق through anything other than Islam. Sayyidina Umar said: نَحْنُ قَوْمٌ أَعَزَّنَا اللَّهُ بِالإِسْلَامِ فَإِذَا طَلَبْنَا الْعِزَّةَ فِي غَيْرِهِ أَذَلَّنَا اللَّهُ — we are a people whom Allah gave dignity through Islam, and if we seek dignity from anything other than Islam, Allah will humiliate us. The rules are simply different for Muslims than for others. There are universal سُنَنٌ — patterns Allah has embedded in creation for everyone — but Allah will not give Muslims تَوْفِيق except through Islam. It is impossible otherwise. The disbelievers may enjoy temporary successes through their disbelief and disobedience, but in the long term they must ultimately fail. They have no other option.

<!-- page 212 -->

Another point he raises — there are two categories of نِعْمَةٌ: أُصُولٌُ النِّعَمِ and فُرُوعُ النِّعَمِ. The أُصُولٌ are the roots — the foundational blessings — and they are the most important. They include things like health, إِيمَان, Islam, safety of body, security in one's home and community, and general well-being. These are the core نَعَمْ. The فُرُوع are the branches — the embellishments that Allah adds on top.

Allah سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى says of Talut in the Quran: when the Bani Israel objected to his kingship on the grounds that he had no wealth, Allah replied that He had chosen him and given him بَسْطَةًۭ فِى ٱلْعِلْمِ وَٱلْجِسْمِ — an abundance in knowledge and physical stature. This is something Allah uses to embellish certain people with. This is why Sayyidina Umar used to say: إِذَا بَعَثْتُمْ رَسُولًا فَابْعَثُوا حَسَنَ الْوَجْهِ — if you send a messenger, send someone with a good face. The reason is practical wisdom: there is an innate human inclination toward people who are attractive and personable, and Allah has placed that inclination in hearts for a purpose. This is why the Prophets were all, without exception, striking to look at. Leadership has long been associated with height as well — tall stature tends to draw followers, as history repeatedly shows. Napoleon was a conspicuous exception to that pattern, compensating with cleverness where he lacked height. But even Napoleon, for all his brilliance in certain respects, ultimately proved to be deeply short-sighted in the end.

But there is wisdom in all of this — it continues to unfold. I think everyone understands the point, so let's move on.

A man once said, in the Prophet's presence, "اللَّهُمَّ إِنِّي أَسْأَلُكَ تَمَامَ النِّعْمَةِ" — "O Allah, I ask You for the completion of blessing." The Prophet ﷺ replied, "أَتَدْرِي مَا هُوَ تَمَامُ النِّعْمَةِ؟" — "Do you know what the completion of blessing is?" The man said he did not. The Prophet ﷺ said: "دُخُولُ الْجَنَّةِ" — to enter Paradise.

That is the completion of blessing. A good life in this world is a blessing from Allah, and إِيمَان is the greatest blessing of the worldly life. But the completion of that blessing is to enter Paradise with Allah, سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى — فَادْخُلِي فِي عِبَادِي، وَادْخُلِي جَنَّتِي: "Enter among My servants, enter My Paradise." That is the ultimate نِعْمَ.

Islam itself is the نِعْمَ Allah gave us in this world — وَأَتْمَمْتُ عَلَيْكُمْ نِعْمَتِى: "I have completed My blessing upon you" — the blessing of Islam.

There is also something Sheikh Muhammad al-Hajjar shared with me. I met him in Medina, and as we walked together toward his car, right beside بَابُ السَّلَامِ, he said: "مَنْ أَصْبَحَ مُعَافًى فِي بَدَنِهِ، آمِنًا فِي سِرْبِهِ، عِنْدَهُ قُوتُ يَوْمِهِ، فَكَأَنَّمَا حِيزَتْ لَهُ الدُّنْيَا بِحَذَافِيرِهَا" — whoever wakes up in good health, safe in what he possesses, and with enough to see him through the day, it is as though the whole world has been handed to him. That is from the Tirmidhi. Reflect on it: if you woke up this morning with عَافِيَة — good health — free from fear, and with enough provision just for today, you effectively have everything in the دُنْيَا. That is a blessing every one of us should be grateful for.

Now, there are branches of blessing — what we might call ornamental نِعْمَ. A house, for example, is a need. But extra furnishings and comforts beyond what you need are فُرُوعُ النِّعْمَةِ — accessory blessings. You can live without them. And the more blessings you have, the greater the accounting you face. The Prophet ﷺ once visited a house where there was meat, dates, and cool water, and Allah said: ثُمَّ لَتُسْأَلُونَ يَوْمَئِذٍ عَنِ النَّعِيمِ — "Then, on that Day, you will surely be asked about the blessings." The Prophet ﷺ said, "هَذِهِ هُوَ النَّعِيمُ" — "This is the blessing we will be asked about."

When some of the Companions heard this, they came to the Prophet ﷺ and said: "يَا رَسُولَ اللهِ, we have no blessings to be asked about" — they were not eating meat and dates. Jibril, عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ, then came with a message from Allah: Does he wear sandals? Does he drink cool water? The point is that you can walk without shoes, so having them is a blessing. You can drink water that is not cool, so the coolness itself is a blessing you will be asked about. Follow that thread, and there is no end to it.

Sayyidina Ali said: "أَعْقَلُ النَّاسِ الزُّهَّادُ" — the most intelligent people are those who practice زُهْد, doing without. "وَأَحْمَقُ النَّاسِ الْبَخِيلُ" — and the most foolish person is the miser. Why? Because, as he put it: "حُرِمَ مِنْهُ فِي الدُّنْيَا وَعُقِبَ عَلَيْهِ فِي الْآخِرَةِ" — the miser is denied enjoyment of his wealth in this life, and then punished for it in the next. He hoards without enjoying, and the hoard becomes a burden in the hereafter. That is the definition of foolishness.

The زُهْد, by contrast, occupies a genuine spiritual station — a مَقَام. And the Prophet ﷺ himself embodied a kind of balance in this: by modern standards, he would be considered living well below the poverty line. What he had was so little it is almost unimaginable. Yet he was not impoverished in any meaningful sense — his needs were met, and he worried about nothing. Aisha, may Allah be pleased with her, said that two months would sometimes pass without smoke rising in the house of the Prophet ﷺ. Someone asked her what they were eating, and she said: "الْأَسْوَدَيْنِ" — dates and water.

Someone asked Marad al-Tarhaaj whether a person could be a زُهْد while possessing great wealth. He replied: "كان عثمان أزهد الناس وكان عبد الرحمن بن عوف زاهدا" — Uthman was among the most ascetic of people, and Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf was also a زُهْد, yet both were enormously wealthy. So زُهْد is a condition of the heart, not the pocket. If you have wealth and could lose it all tomorrow and remain the same person — that is a sign of زُهْد. But if losing it breaks you, and people say, "He used to be wealthy and now look at him" — that means the wealth had taken root in his heart. The زُهْد is the one who, if it is there, says "الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ," and if it is gone, says "الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ." That is why the Prophet ﷺ chose to go hungry one day and be full the next — to ensure he never grew attached to either state. It is all about non-attachment.

<!-- page 199 -->

## Derision

The next disease is الْحُزْء — derision, mockery, making fun of people. When Musa, عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ, commanded the Bani Israel to sacrifice a cow, they responded: "أَتَتَّخِذُنَا هُزُوًۭا" — "Are you mocking us?" He replied: "أَعُوذُ بِٱللَّهِ أَنْ أَكُونَ مِنَ ٱلْجَهِلِينَ" — "I seek refuge in Allah from being among the ignorant." From this exchange, the scholars derived that اِسْتِهْزَاءٌ — mockery — is rooted in جَهْل, ignorance. To ridicule people is an act of ignorance. This includes lampooning, caricature, comedic ridicule — any form of targeting someone to make others laugh at their expense.

What the author says about its cure is that it is the same as the cure for arrogance, because mockery is a form of arrogance. To mock someone, you must first look down on them. That is كِبْرٌ. This is why the Moroccans have a saying — though it traces back to Sayyidina Ali — "لا تحقر عبدنا عسى يكون لله وليا" — do not belittle anyone, for he may be a وَلِيٌّ of Allah.

Even if you see a man drunk and vomiting in the street, you do not know what his seal will be. Imam al-Qurtubi made a striking point on this: when Umar was still bowing to idols in Mecca, he was already beloved to Allah, سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, who knew what the seal would be. The Muslims watching Umar at that stage might have mocked him — "What a fool, a polytheist" — but they did not know Umar. They were seeing one moment in a story still unfolding. That is why it is profoundly dangerous to judge someone by where they stand right now, or to mock them for it.

This is why Allah, سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, says in the Quran:

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا يَسْخَرْ قَوْمٌ مِّن قَوْمٍ عَسَىٰ أَن يَكُونُوا خَيْرًا مِّنْهُمْ

"O you who believe, let not one people mock another people — perhaps they are better than them."

وَلَا نِسَآءٌۭ مِّن نِّسَآءٍ عَسَىٰٓ أَن يَكُنَّ خَيْرًۭا مِّنْهُنَّ

"And let not women mock other women — perhaps they are better than them."

<!-- page 200 -->

It is worth recalling here that the Prophet ﷺ was commanded not to curse the idols of the polytheists:

لَا تَسُبُّوا الَّذِينَ يَدْعُونَ مِنْ دُونِ اللَّهِ فَيَسُبُّوا اللَّهِ عَدْوًا بِغَيْرِ عِلْمٍ

"Do not revile those they call upon besides Allah, lest they revile Allah out of ignorance and hostility."

This is the principle of سَدُّ الذَّرَائِعِ — blocking the means to harm. Muslims burning Israeli flags or American flags in public — what does that provoke? They go and burn Qurans, or they stomp on a Saudi flag that bears لا إله إلا الله محمد رسول الله. You incite them into an act that is not only harmful to them but sacrilegious to us. We do not want people cursing Allah. There is no good reason to do it.

This is also why the Prophet ﷺ, even in his greatest victories, was never boastful. When he entered Mecca, his head was bowed low — not the posture of a conquering hero, but of a man in total humility before his Lord. That is the truly great leader. The other kind — the triumphant swagger, the chest-thumping — that is جَاهِلِيَّة. That is Abu Jahl. We are meant to be above it, not in a superficial way, but at a deep level.

You can, of course, make دُعَاء against the oppressors. The Quran contains supplications against the ظَالِمِينَ. But there is a difference between that and public spectacles of contempt. And making دُعَاء for people's guidance is مُسْتَحَبّ — recommended, though not obligatory.

Some scholars even cautioned against cursing "Israel" by name, because it is the name of a prophet — the Prophet Ya'qub, عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ. Beyond that, many Jews have embraced Islam throughout history and continue to do so. They are human beings, not demons. The Serbs who carried out atrocities are human beings too. Even Wahshi, who killed Hamza — even Hind, who mutilated his body — they both became Muslim. People can make تَوْبَةٌ. People change. And those who have committed terrible acts often carry those acts with them for the rest of their lives. More veterans of the Vietnam War have taken their own lives than were killed in the war itself — many people do not know that. They paid, and keep paying. They have hearts. As Allah says:

إِن يَمْسَسْكُمْ قَرْحٌۭ فَقَدْ مَسَّ ٱلْقَوْمَ قَرْحٌۭ مِّثْلُهُۥ

"If you are wounded, they are wounded just as you are." We all bleed red.

<!-- page 201 -->

As for reading the Quran while being among the oppressors it condemns — the Prophet ﷺ said: "رُبَّ قَرِئَنَّ الْقُرَانَ وَالْقَرَانَ يَلْعَنَهُ" — perhaps a man recites the Quran while the Quran is cursing him. Because Allah says: أَلَا إِنَّ لَعْنَةَ اللَّهِ عَلَى الْكَاذِبِينَ — "Verily, the curse of Allah is upon those who lie." If you are reading the Quran and you are a liar, you are reading a curse upon yourself.

And that brings us to the final point on this disease. The author says its treatment follows the same path as the treatment of كِبْرٌ, which we have already covered. But he adds one more element of self-knowledge: recognize that the purpose of mockery is to humiliate someone else. That is what drives اِسْتِهْزَاءٌ. Yet in doing so, a person humiliates himself before Allah and earns in return only ignorance. Allah says: لَا تَنَابَزُوا بِالْأَلْقَابِ — "Do not insult one another with offensive labels" — and the consequence is that evil comes back upon you.

<!-- page 202 -->

## Comprehensive Treatment for the Heart

Now we move to the final and comprehensive section: a treatment plan for the diseases of the heart as a whole. The author addresses the root causes of those diseases, the source of good and bad character, and treatment through the ذِكْرٌ of اللَّهُ. That will be the last section, and as I mentioned, we have essentially covered the full ground of the preceding material.

One more thing before we close — and I am glad I remembered it. Does anyone know the seven deadly sins? Any former Catholics in the room?

Pride. Anger. Envy. Sloth. Greed. Gluttony. Lust.

Now, of all those seven, which ones did the author cover? Pride — yes. Anger — yes. Envy — yes. Sloth — essentially, in its spiritual dimension. Greed — yes. That is five. But gluttony and lust — did he cover those?

He mentions شَهْوَة in relation to causes and contributing factors, but he does not treat it as a standalone disease. And that is deliberate. Both gluttony and lust are فِطْرَة inclinations — built into human nature by design. This is why even in the Catholic tradition they were placed in a different category; the other five were considered graver spiritual failings than these two.

Imam al-Ghazali addresses gluttony and lust in their own dedicated book — كَسْرُ الشَّهْوَتَيْنِ, "Breaking the Two Desires." Part of that work has been translated beautifully by Abd al-Hakim Winter — T.J. Winter — with excellent notes, and I would strongly recommend it as a companion to what we have been studying here.

The connection between the two is important: gluttony tends to fuel lust. Feed the appetite excessively, and the other desire grows alongside it. The link is not absolute — people who fast rigorously can still struggle with lust — but the general pattern holds. This is why, when the Prophet ﷺ spoke to young men who could not afford to marry, he said: "فَعَلَيْكُمْ بِالصِّيَامِ" — "Then you must fast" — "فَإِنَّهُ لَكُمْ وِجَاءٌ" — "for it will act as a shield." The وِجَاءٌ was a small pouch placed on a goat to prevent it from impregnating others — a form of restraint. Fasting functions the same way: it dampens and subdues that desire.

Both hunger and sexual desire are part of human nature. They are not meant to be eliminated — you do not want to eradicate شَهْوَة any more than you want to eradicate شَهِيَّة. They share the same Arabic root: desire for food and desire for intimacy. The goal is not suppression but mastery — balance, not obliteration. And the book is worth reading for anyone who wants to work through these two more deeply.

I mention all this because, looking at the seven deadly sins, the author covers five of them in considerable depth. Those two he leaves largely untouched here, though they are not absent from his broader work. In any case — إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ — we are approaching closure on the diseases of the heart. What remains now is the comprehensive treatment plan: the general remedies, the root causes of corrupt character, the sources of virtue and vice, and the central role of ذِكْرٌ in healing the heart.

So he says, وَقَبُّوا أَمْرَاضِ الْقُلُوبِ الْجَامِعُ فَهُوَ نَهْيُ النَّفْسِ عَمَّا تَتَّبِعُ — that the comprehensive and general treatment plan for the diseases of the heart is to prohibit the self from its own desires, coupled with — and then he gives us: وَالصَّغَبُ وَالسَّهْرُ اللَّيَالِ وَالصَّمْتُ وَالفِكْرَةُ وَهُوَ خَالِي — hunger, nighttime vigil, silence, and meditation in private.

He says, رَحِمَهُ اللَّهُ, that in order to treat the self — the heart itself — you must prohibit the self from what it desires. There is a حَدِيث in which the Prophet ﷺ said: لا يؤمن أحدكم حتى يكون هواه تبعاً لما يأتوا به — that none of you truly believes until his هَوًى, his caprice or desire, conforms to what I have brought.

The way you purify the heart, then, is this: all those diseases he just enumerated are forbidden — they run contrary to the guidance اللَّهُ and His Messenger have given us. So the Messenger of اللَّهُ ﷺ tells us that faith is incomplete, that the heart is not sound, until your هَوًى accords with what he brought — which is guidance. And the way to achieve that is by withholding the self from precisely those things that conflict with guidance, until you no longer desire them.

This is the meaning of the verse: وَنَفْسُ كَطِفْرِي إِن تَهْمِلْهُ شَبَّعَ لَحُبَّ الرَّضَاعِ وَإِن تَفْتَمْهُ يَنْفَطِمِي — the نَفْس is like an infant: if you neglect it, it grows up craving to suckle; but if you wean it, the craving fades. Think about it — there are people in this culture, like followers of La Leche League, who breastfeed until the child is four or five years old. The child just keeps wanting it, and they keep going. But if you wean a child at two years, or one year, or six months — whatever age you choose — the desire passes. The Sharia actually prefers weaning at two years, and there is wisdom in that.

There was a study done in Israel — this is a digression — that performed a statistical analysis comparing breastfed children with children who were not breastfed. Correlations in studies like that are always the tricky part, but the finding was an increase in IQ of three points for every month of breastfeeding. There are obviously many variables in a study of that kind, but it is interesting, because the Prophet ﷺ said in a حَدِيث: إِيَّاكُمْ وَالْحَمْقَىٰ فَإِنَّهَا تُغَذِّي — beware of the wet nurse who lacks intelligence, for she is nourishing the brain. Traditionally, people sought out women of sound mind and good character to serve as wet nurses. And Halima — Halima Sa'diyah, رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُ — is a remarkable example of this. Read her حَدِيث. If you do not know Arabic, read it in translation. In every حَدِيث collection that includes it, the scholars have to annotate nearly every word because her Arabic is so elevated. She was extraordinarily eloquent. عَلَيْهَا السَّلَامُ — she is the nursing mother of the Messenger of اللَّهُ ﷺ.

This is what he is pointing us toward. And it is the same teaching found in the Quran: وَأَمَّا مَنْ خَافَ مَقَامَ رَبِّهِ وَنَهَى النَّفْسَ عَنِ الْهَوَى فَإِنَّ الْجَنَّةَ هِيَ الْمَأْوَى — the one who fears standing before his Lord, and then actually restrains the self from what it desires — for such a person, Paradise is the abode.

So this is the work of مُجَهَّدُ قَمْعِ النَّفْسِ — literally suppressing the desires of the نَفْس. It is self-discipline. مَنْ دَانَ نَفْسَهُ — you must discipline the soul. Discipline the soul and you will find happiness. Fail to discipline it and you will be miserable. Look at all the self-help literature in this culture — it is overwhelmingly about people who cannot control themselves.

This is precisely why Islam gives us prayer: to train us in discipline over our time and our days. It gives us ritual washing: to discipline us in cleanliness. It gives us fasting — اَللَّهُمَّا صِرِعَتَ سَيْنِ مُحَمَّدٍ — which is entirely about learning discipline: disciplining the tongue, the stomach, the genitals. Everything Islam brings relates, at its core, to learning how to discipline yourself.

Because if you have no discipline over time, you will not pray on time. Especially in this culture, where there is no أَذًى ringing out and no mosques on every corner, you truly have to stay vigilant. Whenever you travel to a Muslim-majority country from here, it is striking how effortless prayer becomes. The difference is enormous. Here, maintaining that discipline requires active effort — and that effort itself is part of the training.

So you discipline the soul, discipline the tongue. You learn to catch yourself when you feel the urge to backbite — stopping and saying inwardly, I don't want to do this. You have to learn to do that. It is a process. But if you do it enough, it reaches a point where you simply are not doing it anymore. And that is where the هَوًى finally comes into alignment with what the Messenger brought. That is what he is telling us.

Now he gives us the means. What are the core things the soul craves? صَقْلٌ — hunger. The stomach. كَسْرُ الشَّهْوَتَيْنِ — control the stomach and you gain control over the genitals as well. The stomach is the primary driving impulse. If you can learn to master your eating, that is why one of the early masters, as quoted by Imam al-Qushayri, said: for me to lift my hand from the plate while I am still hungry is better than spending an entire night in prayer. Because if a person can discipline that one thing, everything else becomes easier.

People get very agitated when food is delayed. They blame it on hypoglycemia — and there are people who genuinely have that condition, I do not want to dismiss that — but there are also people who simply become angry if food is not on time. You see it in restaurants; waitstaff deal with it constantly. Where is my food? And that is America: fast food, drive-throughs, get it now. The infantile impulse — because the very first thing a newborn does is cry for food. The mother hears the cry and her milk flows. The baby gets the breast and immediately relaxes into a state of complete contentment. That is the infantile pattern, and it is what we must all work to discipline within ourselves: the ability to do without food, to not become grumpy over it, to recognize that missing a single meal will not kill you. People say, "I'm starving." You are not starving. There are people in the world who are genuinely starving — truly dropping from hunger — but missing a meal is not that.

This is why the early shuyukh focused on hunger above almost everything else in their teaching. This does not mean becoming anorexic. The point is simply: learn discipline. Two meals a day is enough — I can say that with confidence. Morning and late afternoon. If you want a third, make it something light in the evening. But two genuine meals are sufficient.

There is a man mentioned — he was from Yemen, from the tribe of Bani Hanifa, actually of the Hanafi persuasion, and I cannot recall his name at this moment — who came to the Prophet ﷺ, and the story is in al-Bukhari. The Prophet ﷺ gave him a bowl of milk. He drank it. The Prophet asked if he wanted more. He said yes. The Prophet kept giving, bowl after bowl, until the man had drunk seven bowls. The next day he embraced Islam, and that evening when he returned, he could only manage one bowl. The Companions who had watched him drink seven the night before were astonished. They asked the Prophet ﷺ about it. He said: وَالْمُؤْمِنُ يَأْكُلُ فِي مِعًى وَاحِدٍ وَالْكَافِرُ يَأْكُلُ فِي سَبْعَةِ أَمْعَاءٍ — the disbeliever eats from seven intestines, وَالْمُؤْمِنُ يَأْكُرُوا۟ مِنْ مَعِينٍ وَاحِدٍ — but the believer eats from one. So إِيمَان actually reduces the appetite.

Now, people today eat far more than people have eaten at virtually any other point in human history. There is a massive surplus of food, driven by agribusiness. Meat consumption in particular has risen dramatically. Ask your grandparents — if they are still alive — how often they ate meat. If they were not from a wealthy family, the answer in most Muslim-majority countries was probably once a week, if they lived in a city; far less if they lived in a village. Many Yemenis, for instance, ate meat only on Eid. This is within living memory.

And in this country too, people who are older remember a time when you did not have snacks available around the clock. Children did not have constant access to food between meals. You ate when it was mealtime, and that was that. There were no convenience stores on every block selling cold drinks and packaged food at any hour. All of this is part of a modern culture that in this regard is making people behave more like animals — اللَّهُ mentions in the Quran that such people are like cattle, simply grazing. Some of those co-op-style bulk food stores actually post signs near the open bins that say "No Grazing." That sign says something. Sociologists call it "food contact," and research suggests the average American has around twenty food contacts per day — twenty separate occasions during the day when food is consumed or handled. Some nutritionists even recommend this pattern of small, frequent eating as healthier, but if you look at traditional cultures worldwide, people ate at set times and did not eat between meals. The absence of refrigeration alone enforced much of that. A Bedouin from Mauritania can go days on very little food, because that kind of discipline has been built into his life.

As for the American military: they now have self-heating field rations — you pour in water, a chemical reaction activates, and the food is hot. They have Indian curries for Sikh soldiers, and various options for different ethnic groups, with special requests accommodated. All of this erodes fortitude. And for every one soldier in the field, seventeen support personnel are required. The plans at the time called for every soldier to eventually carry a personal PC. It is difficult to imagine an army of that kind maintaining its edge — but that is a whole other discussion.

The point is that fasting instills discipline. Muslims, from the practice of Ramadan alone, have the experience of going extended periods without food — they are used to it. The tragedy is that Ramadan itself has been compromised: Muslims now often eat more during Ramadan than in any other month. I have seen it — the night is not given to worship but to feasting, massive spreads lasting until dawn. Walimas and dinner invitations have become extravagant productions, to the point where a person feels unable to invite anyone over without preparing something elaborate. The Prophets of اللَّهُ, peace be upon them, warned against all of this. The Prophet ﷺ would sometimes host guests with dates and water. You do not have to go that far, but when you are a guest, be content with whatever is served and do not take it as an insult if the spread is modest. If someone keeps things simple, assume the best — perhaps they are following the سُنَّة. حُسْنُ الظَّنِّ, a good opinion of people, is always the wiser path. You never lose by assuming the best.

So, regarding hunger — there is much more that could be said, but practically speaking: if food is something you struggle to discipline yourself around, begin by reducing your portions. Try to eat with others rather than alone — eating alone leads to eating more, and there is less بَرَكَةٌ in solitary meals. The more people sharing a plate, the more بَرَكَةٌ descends, and the less food each person needs. Sheikh Khadir, when he returned to Mauritania, was once asked what had struck him most about life there. Among the things that shocked him most, he said, was that everyone ate from separate plates. When he described this to people back home, they were genuinely baffled — they simply could not imagine it. He said he embarrassed himself repeatedly, because a large communal plate would be set down and he would start eating from it, not realizing that people were waiting to serve themselves small portions onto their own dishes.

So eat together. There is بَرَكَةٌ in it. Aim for two meals a day if you can manage it. If you cannot, at the very least avoid heavy meals — eat what is enough, and stop. And keep in mind: the higher the quality of the food, the less of it you need. Part of the reason people here eat such enormous quantities is that the food is nutritionally poor. I am confident that in a typical American restaurant, the single serving placed in front of one person would feed ten people in West Africa. Ten people. And most diners here clean their plates.

So, you know, if the food is good, you'll need less of it. If the food is poor — empty calories, fast food, that sort of thing — you'll eat more and still feel unsatisfied. By good food and bad food I mean genuinely nutritious food: wholesome grains, vegetables, things like that. I wouldn't set foot in a fast food restaurant myself. Even if it's a McFish burger or whatever it is, I'd rather skip it. I might even say the cardboard box it came in is more nutritious. Throw the hamburger out — at least cardboard has roughage.

اللَّهُمَّ وَفِّقْنَا. If you want to bring the heart to life, give some time to the night. They say even two رَكَعَاتٌ is enough. Sheikh Muhammad Ali ibn Malik said: never abandon قِيَام, even if it is only two رَكَعَاتٌ.

Sheikh Abdul Khadim, حَفِظَهُ اللهُ, adds that in this age, when so many people find these practices difficult, it is far worse to do it sporadically than to do it modestly but consistently. One night you get up and pray ten رَكَعَاتٌ with long سُوَرٌ and spend هَلْf an hour or more — and then the next night nothing, and the night after that nothing. It is far better to get up just five minutes before فَجْر and pray two رَكَعَاتٌ, every single night without fail. Make it a habit. Give some prayer to the night, do it consistently, and you will see results. A little here and a little there produces nothing. Ahmad Zarruq compared it to drilling a hole — a little bit here, a little bit there, and you will never strike water.

So give some portion to prayer. A few things about the prayer itself.

When the Prophet ﷺ arrived in Medina, 'Abdullah ibn Salam — may اللَّهُ be pleased with him, the rabbi who embraced Islam — narrated that the Prophet ﷺ gave a khutbah after descending upon Quba. He said: O people, spread peace — greet one another with peace. Feed people. وَصَلُّوا بِاللَّيْلِ وَالنَّاسُ النِّيَّامِ And pray in the night when others are sleeping. تَدْخُلَ الْجَنَّةَ بِالسَّلَامِ You will enter جَنَّة without any difficulty.

Just look at how straightforward that is. أَفْشُوا السَّلَامَ — spread peace. Do not be a cause of division. Be someone who unifies, who brings people together. أَطْعِمُوا الطَّعَامَ — feed people. Be generous. When you feed someone, you are showing you care about them. So: spread peace, be concerned about others, وَصَلُّوا بِاللَّيْلِ وَالنَّاسُ النِّيَّامِ — and then tend to your own soul. Correct yourself. If you do those things, تَدْخُلُواا الْجَنَّةَ بِالسَّلَامِ — and that تَدْخُلُوا is a جَوَابُ شَرْطٍ: if you do those things, that is the condition. خَلَاصٌ. That is all. If every one of us took that single حَدِيث and made it our practice, that is a guarantee from the Messenger of اللَّهُ ﷺ that we will enter Paradise.

They said of one man who did some questionable things during the day but prayed at night: سَتَنْهَاهُ — it will eventually stop him, if he keeps at it.
