On the other hand, there is another حَدِيث, quite frightening, about a woman who prayed at night and gave صَدَقَةٌ but was cruel to her neighbors. كَانَتْ تُؤْذِي جِيرَانَهَا — she used to treat her neighbors badly. And the Prophet ﷺ said: هِيَ فِي النَّارِ. She is in the Fire. And it is a صَحِيحٌ حَدِيث. Then of another person, they said: he does not pray at night, he does not give صَدَقَةٌ, he does not do these other things — وَيُحْسِنُوا إِلَى جِيرَانِ — but he is good to his neighbors. سَيَدْخُلَ الْجَنَّةَ. He will enter جَنَّة.

So you can pray all night if you like — but without good character, it counts for very little. Keep things in perspective. Tahajjud is immensely important, and اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى has encouraged it for all believers. He says: وَمَنِ اللَّيْلِ فَتَهَجَّدْ بِهِ نَافِرَةً لَكَ عَسَىٰ أَن يَبَعَثَكَ رَبُّكَ مَقَامًا مَحْمُودًا — And from the night, spend a portion of it in تَهَجُّد as a نَافِلَة, a voluntary act. Perhaps your Lord will raise you to a praiseworthy station. So this practice helps purify the heart.

And اللَّهُ says of the believers: مُؤْمِنُون تَتَجَافَتْ جُنُوبُهُمْ عَنِ الْمَضَاجِعِ — they lift their sides from their beds at night when they would far rather sleep. Sleep is something the نَفْس loves. We all love sleep. And that is precisely why restraining the نَفْس from sleep — in moderation — is spiritually powerful. Not all night. You do not want to push yourself into a sleepless psychotic state. You do not deprive yourself entirely. Keep it within reasonable bounds.

In the same way, when they speak of hunger as a discipline, they do not mean the emaciated ascetic whose spine is visible — the image people call the starving Buddha, that famous statue. That is not our tradition. The point is to learn to govern the food-driven نَفْس. The same applies to night prayers: you do not spend the entire night in prayer. There was a ta'ifa among the صَحَابَة who spent a great deal of the night in prayer — not all of it, but a great deal. According to al-Raghib al-Isfahani, a ta'ifa is fewer than a thousand, so it was a number of the صَحَابَة who sustained this, though not all of them.

Consider the well-known حَدِيث about the man the Prophet ﷺ said was a man of Paradise. One of the صَحَابَة followed him for several days and observed nothing remarkable. So he asked him directly: what are you doing? The man replied: all I know is that every night before I sleep, I make sure there is no rancor in my heart toward any Muslim. He was not praying at night, was not fasting, was not doing anything outwardly exceptional. Not all the صَحَابَة prayed through the night.

But اللَّهُ says of a group among them: كانوا قليلا من الليل ما يهجعون — in one reading, they would not sleep at night; in another reading, كانوا قليلا من الليل ما يهجعون — they slept only a small portion of the night. Both readings are valid. The point is that for some among them, this was their particular practice.

For us, I think what is rational and reasonable is a steady, consistent practice — something regular. Shaykh Abdullah al-Haddad advises that given the nature of this age, it is good to recite the shorter سُوَرٌ that carry great reward. I mentioned last night the line: إذا العشرون من شعبانا ولت فواصل شرب ليلك بالنهار — when your life is drawing toward its end, start doing as much as you can. He actually recommends even just reciting قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ten times in short رَكَعَاتٌ to accumulate great reward.

So even short رَكَعَاتٌ count. If your practice is to pray some portion of the night and you happen to oversleep until just before فَجْر, it is still permissible and acceptable to pray those رَكَعَاتٌ as تَهَجُّد before your فَجْر prayer — as long as you will not miss فَجْر itself and as long as there is still some darkness before the إِسْفَار sets in. You can easily pray ten رَكَعَاتٌ with قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ and proper طُمَأْنِينَةٌ in seven or eight minutes — they will not be rushed, they will be sound رَكَعَاتٌ, and you can complete them in ten minutes or so. A minute per rakaat is quite manageable.

For women who cannot pray at certain times, it is perfectly acceptable to get up in the night and read something beneficial, or to meditate, or to make دُعَاء. You can recite قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ in every rakaat. The سُوَرٌ that carry particular benefit — إِذَا زُلْزِلَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ زِلْزَالَهَا، قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ، الْمُعَوِّذَتَيْن، قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ الْفَلَقِ، قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ النَّاسِ، لِإِيلَافِ قُرَيْشٍ، أَلْهَاكُمُ التَّكَاثُرُ — those are the سُوَرٌ the Prophet ﷺ used frequently. وَٱلتِّينِ وَٱلزَّيْتُونِ is another. He recited إِذَا زُلْزِلَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ زِلْزَالَهَا often. وَالضُّحَى وَاللَّيْلِ إِذَا سَجَى as well. And it is not a بِدْعَةٌ to recite the same surah in every rakaat of a voluntary prayer.

You shouldn't make it a regular practice, and there is some خِلَافٌ about this. The Shafi'is, I believe, would permit it, while the Malikis would consider it a بِدْعَةٌ. So be careful — for some imams it may be a بِدْعَةٌ, for others it may be acceptable. For Imam Malik, it is a بِدْعَةٌ to recite قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ at every Maghrib prayer, whereas Shaykh Abdullah al-Haddad, in one of his treatises, mentioned that it was a good thing to do, and there is a حَدِيث indicating that one of the صَحَابَة did exactly that. So you have to be a little careful with these matters.

<!-- page 238 -->

There is also the حَدِيث about Sayyidina Abdullah ibn Umar, may اللَّهُ be pleased with him. The Prophet ﷺ once walked past him and said, نعم الرجل لو كان يقوم الليل — "What an excellent man — if only he would spend some time at night in prayer." The scholars have clarified that this is not simply a compliment; the statement contains the word لَيْتَ, a particle of wishing, as in لَيْتَ لَيْتُهُ كَانَ يَقُومَ اللَّيْل. What the scholars took from this is that a person can still be a good person even without praying at night — like Abdullah ibn Umar — but how much better would he be if he did? The believer who hears that desires perfection. And so, when Abdullah ibn Umar heard those words — and he himself is the narrator of the حَدِيث, which he related to his son — he never abandoned قِيَامُ اللَّيْلِ again after that.

The scholars also drew from this حَدِيث that it is permissible to praise someone who will not be made conceited by that praise. The Prophet ﷺ knew that Abdullah ibn Umar would not hear those words and think, "I'm already excellent, so I don't need to pray at night." Rather, it spurred him on to do more — which is exactly what happened.

Then there is the well-known حَدِيث of Aisha, which is a صَحِيح حَدِيث, in which she describes how the Prophet ﷺ would pray so long at night that his feet would crack and become slightly swollen. What the physicians call venous stasis — when you stand for extended periods, blood can pool in the feet and cause mild edema. This is why people who are on their feet all day, such as nurses, often wear compression stockings to help the blood return upward. It happens to many people.

So Aisha said, "يَا رَسُولَ اللهِ, why do you do this when your Lord has forgiven you everything — past and future?" And he replied, "Should I not be a grateful servant?" In other words, that very fact should make me want to show even more gratitude. Worship, then, is a way of expressing gratitude to اللَّهُ. The scholars say this حَدِيث shows that one of the greatest ways to give thanks for the نِعْمَةٌ of اللَّهُ is to do some night work — سَهَرُ اللَّيَالِي. And they say: من قرب المعالي سهر الليالي — whoever seeks high things stays awake at night.

For students especially, the night is the best time to study. If فَجْر falls at a particular hour, divide the night from Maghrib onward into six equal parts. Each part, in a twelve-hour night, would be roughly two hours. The last sixth of the night — the final two hours before فَجْر — is the best time for قِيَامُ اللَّيْلِ. If we take, for simplicity, Maghrib at six p.m. and فَجْر at six a.m., then four a.m. to six a.m. would be that optimal period. That said, any prayer after Isha counts as قِيَامُ اللَّيْلِ, and some scholars say even after Maghrib.

For people who find it genuinely difficult to rise in the early morning — those with what the tradition calls a "heavy liver," meaning a slow constitution — it is perfectly valid to pray some رَقَائِق before going to bed. You should do something in the night hours. Interestingly, many people find it easier to wake up around three or four in the morning than right before فَجْر, because the sleep immediately before فَجْر is recognizably the heaviest sleep of the night. This is a حِكْمَةٌ from اللَّهُ, and it also corresponds to what science tells us: cortisol levels in the brain are at their peak at that time. Ibn Ata'illah said the wisdom in this is that اللَّهُ wants you to be able to pull yourself out of sleep precisely when it is most difficult to do so.

As for the number of رَقَائِق: eight and ten are both established by صَحِيح حَدِيث, and I was told ten is the best. You pray two by two — two, four, six, eight, ten — and it is better done after Isha, unless you are someone who by nature rises every night without fail, in which case you may wait. Sayyidina Abu Bakr, who was very strong in his spiritual practice and certainly prayed the night prayer, used to pray before he went to bed.

What if you are staying up late — do you have to sleep first and then get up? No, you don't have to. Obviously it is better to sleep and then rise, because the Prophet ﷺ said that was the best way — the way of Dawud, peace be upon him, who would sleep for a portion, rise for a portion, sleep again, and rise again. That is harder on the self than simply staying up, and he maintained that practice just as he maintained his fast of every other day. It is true مُجَاهَدَة — real mastery over the نَفْس — because it is genuinely difficult to fall asleep and then get up again.

And never forget — أَعُوذُ بِاللَّهِ — that شَيْطَان is always nearby. شَيْطَانٌ يَعْقِدُ عُقَدًا ثَلَاثَةً — he ties three knots on your head while you sleep, in the unseen world, and whispers, أَمَامَكَ لَيْلٌ طَوِيلٌ — "You have a long night ahead of you, just relax." In winter especially, this is all too convincing. Those three knots are heavy. The Prophet ﷺ said that if you wake up and recite the دُعَاء of waking — الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ الَّذِي أَحْيَانِي بَعْدَ مَا أَمَاتَنِي وَإِلَيْهِ النُّشُورُ — the first knot is loosened. If you get up and make وُضُوء, the second knot is loosened. And if you then pray, the third knot is loosened and you are freed entirely.

The Prophet ﷺ was also asked about a man who slept straight through until morning without praying — and the fact that the صَحَابَة asked about it shows this was not their norm. He said, ذَاكَ رَجُلٌ بَالَ الشَّيْطَانُ فِي أُذُنِهِ — "That is a man in whose ears شَيْطَان urinated." الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ. Anyone who does not pray the night prayer has شَيْطَان using his ear as a toilet. We should not want that. The way out is simply to do something — get up in the night, pray even a few رَقَائِق for اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ.

<!-- page 243 -->

Some of the scholars said this should be understood حَقِيقَةً — literally — that شَيْطَان literally urinates in the ear. And the principle in Islamic jurisprudence is that الحقيقة لا تُصرف إلى المجاز إلا لضرورة — the literal meaning is not set aside for a metaphorical one unless there is a necessity to do so. Some scholars found no such necessity and took it literally. Others interpreted it metaphorically, saying it means شَيْطَان deprives that person of all the good that comes from spending part of the night in worship. وَاللَّهُ تَعَالَى أَعْلَمُ.

Can you remind us of the دُعَاء? الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ الَّذِي أَحْيَانِي بَعْدَ مَا أَمَاتَنِي وَإِلَيْهِ النُّشُورُ — Praise be to the One who brought me back to life after He caused me to die, and to Him is my return.

<!-- page 244 -->

There is also the practice of reciting the closing verses of سُورَةُ آلِ عِمْرَانَ upon waking. I spent many nights in مُرَبِّي حَجٌّ's tent, and every single night he would get up — usually four, sometimes five hours before فَجْر — and he would wake you with those verses: إِنَّ فِي خَلْقِ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضِ وَاخْتِلَافِ اللَّيْلِ وَالنَّهَارِ لَآيَاتٍ لِأُولِي الْأَلْبَابِ الَّذِينَ يَذْكُرُونَ اللَّهَ قِيَامًا وَقُعُودًا وَعَلَى جُنُوبِهِمْ وَيَتَفَكَّرُونَ فِي خَلْقِ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضِ رَبَّنَا مَا خَلَقْتَ هَذَا بَاطِلًا — and I can still hear his voice reciting them. إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, اللَّهُ give us تَوْفِيق. It is all تَوْفِيق from اللَّهُ — the ability to do these things is itself a gift from Him.

What prevents us from قِيَامُ اللَّيْلِ — what blocks it — is غِيبَة, eating what is حَرَامٌ, and similar violations. These are what close the door. This is a gift from اللَّهُ, and those who have experienced it and then lost it know that when they had it, it truly felt like a gift, and when it was taken away, it felt like a punishment. So make تَوْبَةٌ, ask اللَّهُ to restore it, and إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ He will.

<!-- page 245 -->

The people of the قَوْم used to hold themselves to such rigorous مُحَاسَبَة that whenever something afflicted them they could trace the cause. One scholar said, "I once said something about someone that I should not have said, and I was deprived of تَهَجُّد for forty days because of it." They could see the direct connection between cause and consequence. Another scholar said that near the end of his life he went bankrupt, and he realized it was the result of something that had happened forty years earlier — he had once said to a man, "يَا مُفْلِسُ," meaning "you bankrupt one," and he understood that اللَّهُ had given him exactly what he had called that other man. As the saying goes, the good actions of the righteous are the bad actions of those who are near to اللَّهُ — meaning what is an ordinary slip for most people carries far greater weight for those whose hearts are refined.

<!-- page 246 -->

On the question of وِتْرٌ: do not pray the وِتْرٌ twice. Witr should be the last prayer of the night — that is the correct practice. It is perfectly valid to pray additional prayers after وِتْرٌ, but وِتْرٌ should come last. So if you go to bed early, it is better to pray your وِتْرٌ before you sleep, since وِتْرٌ should be completed before فَجْر. If you wake up and forgot to pray it, you can still make it up after فَجْر, but it is better done before. In the الْمَذْهَبُ الْحَنَفِيُّ, وِتْرٌ is وَاجِب; in the other مَذَاهِب, it is a سُنَّةٌ مُؤَكَّدَةٌ — a strongly emphasized سُنَّة.

The شِفَاءٌ and وِتْرٌ should be prayed before you go to bed. Then if you rise during the night, do not repeat the وِتْرٌ; simply pray تَهَجُّد in pairs — two by two, مَتْنًا مَتْنًا, as the حَدِيث says. You do not pray four together; you pray two, then two.

If you are asking whether you can do تَهَجُّد after وِتْرٌ — yes, you can. The preferable practice is to delay the وِتْرٌ so that it is the last prayer of the night. That is the ideal. But put it this way: if you are as certain as one can reasonably be that you will wake up before فَجْر, then it is better to delay your وِتْرٌ. If your consistent, reliable practice is to wake up هَلْf an hour or an hour before فَجْر and pray — and you are confident in that pattern — then it is better to delay. If, on the other hand, you are not consistent in waking up, then it is better to pray your وِتْرٌ before you go to sleep.

<!-- page 247 -->

And if you do wake up during the night before فَجْر, you pray your تَهَجُّد without ending with وِتْرٌ, since you have already prayed it and there is only one وِتْرٌ in the night.

— Ingrizi nahi malum hai. That's all the Urdu I know. There is an Afghani joke along these lines. An American traveled to Afghanistan having heard that the country was home to great spiritual teachers and saints. He wanted to find the greatest of them. So he approached an Afghani man and asked, the way Americans do — assuming everyone speaks English — "Who is the greatest spiritual teacher in Afghanistan?" The man replied, "Nami fahman." So the American went around asking after Nami Fahman, treating it as a name.

At some point a funeral procession passed by, and he saw a crowd watching solemnly. He went up to one of the Afghans and asked, "Who just died?" The man replied, "Nami fahman." Nami fahman, of course, means "I don't understand" in Persian.

<!-- page 248 -->

On another point — about taking things easy and not driving yourself into the ground — Shaykh Habib said in الْقَصِيدَة that you should travel the path with ease toward yourself. You need yourself to complete the journey. Your horse has to carry you to where you are going, so you treat your horse well. Do not wear it out to the point where it cannot carry you any further. He is not saying be lazy — he is saying do not exhaust yourself so completely that you lose the capacity to continue.

The goal is to move along the path with yearning. If you are not too hard on yourself, your self will actually begin to do these things. If you try to stay up the whole night, it is too much for the self. If you try to stay up هَلْf the night, it is still too much. The self loses energy and gives up entirely. But if you start small, something shifts — you begin to taste the sweetness of it, you feel the benefit, and you start to want it. The self begins to look forward to it. That is the point he is making.

And then he says: two رَقَائِق prayed by a lover are better than a thousand prayed without love. If you pray two رَقَائِق with شَوْق — with genuine longing, with a real desire for اللَّهُ's pleasure — اللَّهُ will love it. But if you are going through the motions thinking, "I have to do this, it is important, I must," with no love behind it — there is no love there. The action is driven by an idea in your head, not by your heart. The whole point is that all of this should arise from a yearning for اللَّهُ, as a مَحَبَّة from اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى.

<!-- page 249 -->

And then he says: وَالصَّمْتُ — silence. One of the diseases he addresses is lust for speech — اَلْكَلَامُ — which is, for most people, a deeply ingrained drive. The Prophet ﷺ said, مَنْ أُوتِيَ صَمْتًا فَقَدْ أُوتِيَ الْحِكْمَةَ — whoever is given silence as part of his nature has been given wisdom. There is wisdom in silence itself, simply in being silent. Even an ignorant person, if he is given the gift of silence, possesses a kind of wisdom — because at the very least, if you are ignorant, silence keeps you from making a fool of yourself.

If you have something to say, speech is better than silence. If you have nothing to say, it is permissible — مُبَاحٌ — but there is no benefit in it. And if that permissible speech amounts to casual pleasantries — how are you, where have you been — that is fine.

But if it turns to "did you hear about so-and-so," it slides toward either مَكْرُوهٌ or حَرَامٌ. Learning to govern the tongue is a serious discipline.

Imam al-Shafi'i said that whenever he was in a gathering and felt moved to speak, he would first look inward and ask himself whether he was speaking out of a desire of the نَفْس — to prove a point or display his knowledge. He would check whether he was content for someone else to say the very same thing. If he could honestly say yes, he would speak. If not, he would stay silent, as a تَرْبِيَة for his نَفْس. He also said that whenever he entered a debate, he would make دُعَاء that اللَّهُ cause the truth to appear on his opponent's tongue, so that he himself could submit to it. He had no wish to win — he would have preferred to lose, because losing would make him humble. That is a رَجُلٌ, that is a man.

The Prophet, peace be upon him, was the most reserved of people. When he spoke, he spoke the truth. He was not what is called فَرْفَدْ. He said, صلى الله عليه وسلم, إِنَّ أَبْعَدَكُمْ مِنِّي وَأَبْعَدَكُمْ إِلَيَّ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ، الْفَرْفَارِينَ، الْمُتَفَيْهِقِينَ — the most distant of you from me, and the most distant to me on the Day of Judgment, are the فَرْفَرٍ and the المُتَفَيْهِقِينَ. The companions recognized the other terms, but did not know المُتَفَيْهِقِينَ, and he explained it as مُتَكَدِّر. فَرْفَدْ is someone who speaks incessantly — فَرْفَرَ، يُفَرْفِرُ — they simply never stop talking. He disliked the chatterbox. If you are teaching or saying something of use, that is another matter entirely. But speech for speech's sake is a waste.

<!-- page 243 -->

Imam Malik, رَحِمَهُ اللَّهُ, said of one of his students: نِعْمَ الرَّجُلُ، إِلَّا أَنَّهُ يَتَكَلَّمُ كَلَامَ الشَّهْرِ فِي يَوْمٍ وَاحِدٍ — he is a fine man, except that he speaks a whole month's worth of words in a single day.

Then there is الْفِكْرَةُ وَهُوَ خَالٍ — reflection in solitude. خَالِي means to be in a place with no one around, engaged in reflection. In English we call this meditation, and it is a genuine practice — not yogic, but simply sitting and meditating. Within the Islamic tradition there are various forms. One is التَّفَكُّرُ فِي الْمَوْتِ, or contemplating the states of the hereafter: you sit and walk through the raising from the grave and the scenes of the Day of Judgment. Another is تَفَكُّر in the divine names: you move through them — يَا اللهُ، يَا رَحْمَٰنُ، يَا رَحِيمُ — and reflect on each attribute. So فِكْرَة in solitude benefits the heart.

Then there is صُحْبَةُ الْأَخْيَارِ — keeping company with the righteous. This practice is rooted in a Quranic injunction: يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَكُونُوا مَعَ الصَّادِقِينَ — O you who believe, have تَقْوَى of اللَّهُ and be with the truthful ones. This is what is called صُحْبَة in the Persian tradition: keeping good company with righteous people in order to purify the heart.

Imam Ibn 'Ata'illah says in the حِكَم: لَا تَصْحَبْ مَن لَا يُنْهِضُكَ حَالُهُ وَلَا يَدُلُّكَ عَلَى اللَّهِ مَقَالُهُ — Do not take as a companion someone whose state does not lift you, and whose speech does not direct you toward اللَّهُ.

Sidi Ahmad Zarruq notes in his commentary that this is not referring to spiritual masters — it is referring to the friends you spend ordinary time with. You should seek people who will elevate your state, not drag it down. And when they elevate yours, their own state rises too.

What this means in practice is that such people are in a good state with اللَّهُ — a state of gratitude. When you are around people who are genuinely grateful, it becomes difficult to complain. They are saying, اَلْحَمْدُ لِلَّٰهِ, سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ, I feel truly grateful, اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ, اللَّهُ has given us so much — and you find yourself sitting there unable to say, sorry, I can't agree, life is hard. They are in a different هَلْ from you. So if you are the one in شُكْر, do not keep company with the one in شَكْوَى. And if you are the one in شَكْوَى, seek out the one in شُكْر, so that you may become like him. Do not drag him down — let him lift you up. That is the teaching.

And as for their speech directing you to اللَّهُ: there are people who, if you did complain to them, would gently say, سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ — وَإِن تَعُدُّوا۟ نِعْمَةَ ٱللَّهِ لَا تُحْصُوهَآ — if you try to enumerate the blessings of اللَّهُ, you cannot count them; that applies to everyone. So perhaps instead of cataloguing your troubles, you might try counting his blessings. They help you shift your focus. And it is remarkable how a person's entire state can be transformed simply by sitting with someone who offers sincere and good counsel. A heart that was utterly dark and negative can be turned around entirely, because the effect was real and valuable — the person's perspective changes, and that is guidance.

That is what you want. And on this, Sidi Abu al-Hasan said: مَن دَلَّكَ عَلَى الدُّنْيَا فَقَدْ غَشَّكَ — whoever directs you toward this world has cheated you. If someone's entire counsel to you is about financial planning, acquiring property, securing your future in this life — that is a sign that he has cheated you, because you could die at any moment.

The example I like to use is this: imagine two salesmen come to you. The first says, "I have a house for sale. The only drawback is that it could disappear at any time — I cannot tell you exactly when, maybe fifty years from now, but it will be gone within seventy at the most, and it could vanish at any point before that." The second says, "I have another house. It will last forever and is guaranteed — but it is more expensive." Which one do you buy? Seriously — which one? Any person of sound intellect is going to buy the second one.

That is the آخِرَة. And you purchase it with your soul. The other house, you also purchase with your soul — but you buy it by destroying the soul, not by preserving it.

So: keeping good company. Then Sidi Abu al-Hasan also said that the one who tells you to rush and pile up deeds has merely exhausted you — if you believe you will reach جَنَّة through your deeds alone, you will wear yourself out. وَمَن دَلَّكَ عَلَى اللَّهِ فَقَدْ نَصَحَكَ — but the one who shows you the path to اللَّهُ has given you sincere counsel. That path is the contentment of اللَّهُ. If you are content with اللَّهُ, a small amount of action will be enough. If you are not, no amount of action will ever suffice.

And finally, he speaks of taking refuge — of returning to the One to whom all affairs return, since اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى says that to اللَّهُ all affairs return. Seeking refuge in اللَّهُ is, above all else, the truest remedy.

This is why a scholar of the path says that the only real cure for all these diseases of the heart is to go before اللَّهُ in a state of complete neediness — please, I need to change, and I need You to help me change — with a genuine sense of urgency. If that is the state a person is in, then إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, اللَّهُ will help them change.

For my own part, I truly believe that my coming to Islam was the result of a state I entered before I was Muslim — a state of complete اِفْتِقَارٌ. I called on اللَّهُ by the name I knew, which was God, and I simply asked: give me guidance, show it to me. I was in a state of utter neediness. Most of the people I have met who became Muslim will confirm something similar: that there was a moment of absolute — just show me what to do. And then the guidance opened up, and it became very clear. خَلَاصٌ. After that it becomes easy, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ.

I believe اللَّهُ is merciful, and that where there is sincerity, even within a person's limited and imperfect understanding, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ that is enough. There is an apocrypهَلْ story — and because it is apocrypهَلْ, no عَقِيدَة is based on it, I want to be clear about that — but it carries a wisdom that has been told: about the Prophet Musa, عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ, encountering a man who is speaking to اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى about wanting to wash His feet, give Him a massage, and tend to Him — a man who is an anthropomorphist without knowing it.

And Musa, moved by righteousness, rebuked the man and explained the true meaning of تَوْحِيد. Yet اللَّهُ reminded Musa that he had been harsh, for the man had spoken those words out of love — he was simply ignorant. The right response to ignorance is to teach. So the point is that everyone is at the level where they are. Even the one bowing before an idol — that is his understanding; that is where he stands. This is why اللَّهُ says:

وَلَا تَجَعَلُوا لِلَّهِ أَنْدَادًا

Do not set up rivals to اللَّهُ —

وَأَنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ

— while you know it is wrong. Once knowledge reaches a person, that is when شِرْك destroys him. If someone has no knowledge, and اللَّهُ's messenger has not yet reached him, اللَّهُ will not punish him:

وَمَا كُنَّا مُعَذِّبِينَ حَتَّىٰ نَبْعَثَ رَسُولًۭا

So if someone does not know Islam and is genuinely seeking guidance, that guidance comes, إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ.

وَاللَّهُ تَعَالَى عَنْهُمْ

Then the author says:

بِأَنْ يَكُونَ كَغَرِيقًا أَوْ كَمَنْ ضَلَّ بِتِيهِنْ لَا يَرَى الْغِيَاثَةِ مِنْ سِوَى الْمُهَيْمَنَ الْعَظِيمِ الْقَدْرِ وَهُوَ الْمُجِيبُ دَعْوَةٍ مُقَرِّي

He is saying that you must be like someone who is drowning, or like someone lost in a barren desert who sees no source of help except the Almighty God. This is precisely what happened to Yusuf Islam. I heard him describe it in a lecture: he was caught in the undertow at Malibu, being dragged out to sea — genuinely terrifying — and in that moment he called on God completely and promised: give me guidance and I will follow it, whatever you ask of me. That is the غَرِيقٌ — the drowning man — with nowhere left to turn.

And then there is Ikrimah ibn Abi Jahl, whose guidance came on a boat carrying Abyssinian Christians. When the waves began to overwhelm the vessel and everyone feared they would drown, Ikrimah started calling on Hubal. The Christians on board laughed at him. They said: you are calling on an idol made of wood, sitting in Mecca, to save you out here? And in that moment it struck him — how absurd it was. He realized he could only call on اللَّهُ. And تَوْحِيد entered his heart right there. That is the state of the drowning man. And the author says this is in fact the most beneficial state — the one that will truly help us. It must reach the point where you are like a man going under.

So that is that.

## Beneficial Actions for Purifying the Heart

Now, on the actions that purify the heart, he says:

وَمَابِهِ لَلْقَلْبِ صَفْوً مِنْ عَمَلٍ أَنْفَعَهُ هُوَ الْمُدَامُ وَلَوْ يَقِلْ

As for the action most beneficial for purifying the heart, none surpasses the one that is consistent — even if it is small. This comes from the hadith:

خَيْرُ الْأَعْمَالِ أَدْوَمُهَا وَإِنْ قَلَّ

The best actions are the ones maintained continuously, even if they are slight.

Think of it this way: if you take a coin and polish it hard with a few vigorous strokes, then set it down, versus taking the same coin and polishing it gently but without stopping — the second coin becomes bright and shiny in a way the first never will. One burst of effort cannot do what quiet consistency achieves over time. The heart works the same way. What encrusts it through accumulated wrong actions is not undone by one intense push. A little effort, maintained steadily, is what gradually breaks that crust away. That is why small deeds done consistently far outweigh large deeds done once.

The proof is in how the Companions conducted themselves. They were always consistent in their practices. The man who asked the Prophet about fasting every other day — the fast of Dawud — came to regret it in his old age. But the scholars took that very regret as evidence: the Companions held even voluntary practices so seriously that they would not abandon what they had taken upon themselves. It was not that the practice was obligatory — it was that they had committed to it.

And if you read the أَحَادِيث, you find that the Prophet used to fast so much that the Companions thought he never broke his fast — and then there were times when they thought he never fasted at all. He used to spend so much of the night in prayer that they thought he never slept — and then there were times when they thought he barely did any قِيَامٌ.

There is something very important to understand about the Messenger of Allah. He is the universal prophet — his being encompasses the possibilities of every good believer. So in him, every good believer has a model. And different believers are opened to different things. There are people for whom قِيَامٌ is not the door Allah opens for them. There are people for whom recitation of the Quran is not their particular opening — their opening may be in hadith. Others are opened through صَدَقَةٌ, not fasting. Others through fasting, not صَدَقَةٌ. Some through wisdom. It is very rare for anyone to be opened through all of them simultaneously.

This is why, when the Prophet described the different gates of paradise — the gate for the fasters, the gate for the مُجَاهِدِينَ — Abu Bakr asked: يَا رَسُولَ اللَّهِ، أَيَدْخُلُ أَحَدٌ هَذِهِ الْأَبْوَابَ كُلَّهَا؟ Can anyone enter through all of them? And the Prophet said: وَأَرْجُو أَنْ تَكُونَ مِنْهُمْ — yes, and I hope you are one of them. As they say, I hope he was. The point is: keep working. That applies to all of us.

So some people are given broad and wide-ranging تَوْفِيق. Imam Malik, for instance, wore fine clothes, used the best scents, burned عُودٌ — and to an outsider, especially among the ascetics, this looked extravagant. One of the زُهْدٌ wrote him a letter saying: I cannot believe your state. You eat the finest flour, you wear expensive clothes, the perfume alone must cost a fortune — you would do better giving all of that up and spending less time with people. He was, after all, teaching extensively in the مَسْجِد.

So the زُهْد was essentially telling Imam Malik: compared to me, you are in a bad state. This is what is called مَرْجَبٌ — essentially a kind of spiritual boasting. And Imam Malik wrote back an extraordinary letter. He said: يَا عَبْدَ اللَّهِ — servant of Allah — Allah has opened for His servants many doors of goodness.

He then says: include action done in the absence of witnesses, and action done out of love for Allah or in awe of Him. So one of the things that benefits the heart is performing acts that no one else can see. Giving charity privately. Praying in a way that no one observes. This protects you from رِيَاءٌ and عُجْب and everything that flows from them.

There is a story — it was actually Fudayl ibn Ayyad, not Bishr al-Hafi — who was once walking down a street and overheard someone say: there goes a man who spends the entire night in prayer. When Fudayl heard it, he burst into tears and said: I cannot remember ever spending a single complete night in prayer. And then he said: how great is the honor Allah has bestowed on me by veiling me and making people think I am better than I am. That idea — of concealing one's state, of hiding — is the better path.

And then action done out of love: that is the highest. The scholars say there are two principal motivations. One is خَوْف — fear — which, when it reaches the level of خَوْفُ مَقَامِهِ, becomes جَلَاءٌ: awe of Allah's greatness, a profound sense of هَيْبَة in the divine presence. The other is مَحَبَّة — love — born of Allah's mercy, yearning for Allah, doing things out of that longing. That is the highest motive, and the author tells you that the final مَقَام is مَحَبَّة.

He also says that the purest actions are those of the زُهْد — the one who is free of worldly wants, who does what he does seeking nothing in return, no thanks, no gratitude, not even reward in the آخِرَة. He does it solely for Allah's sake. That is the highest and best action for the heart. زُهْد is not an outward condition — it is the absence of attachment within the heart. The زُهْد is not like the Christian ascetic who wears rough clothing and practices flagellation. In Islam, the ascetic is the one for whom the world has no grip on the heart. That is why Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf was a زُهْد, even though he was one of the wealthiest men in Mecca.

Then the author says that the actions of those who strive out of hope are higher and more exalted than those driven by fear alone. If fear is your only motivation, that is still valid — Allah tells us to have خَوْف — but hope lifts the action higher. And the Prophet, صلى الله عليه وسلم, is the حَبِيبُ اللهِ — the beloved of Allah — and Allah loved him. That is the pinnacle: mutual love between the servant and his Lord.

He then says: particularly, action whose benefits extend to others. An action that purifies the heart is made greater when it reaches beyond the self. Charity does not only benefit your own soul — it benefits another human being. And in every creature that possesses a moist liver — that is, every living thing — there is reward. Water an animal and there is reward. Plant a tree and every creature that eats from it, for as long as that tree stands, earns you reward. All of these things are elevated precisely because they extend outward.

Then he speaks of fasting in relation to one's character. If you are someone who has poor control over his appetite — if gluttony is your weakness — then fasting is particularly powerful for you. It strikes at the root of that disorder. But for someone who already has reasonable control over his relationship with food, fasting is still good, yet it will not work the same transformation as it does on the one who genuinely needs to discipline that aspect of himself.

Similarly with the miser: for a generous person, giving in charity is natural. For someone who is stingy, giving out is a far greater struggle — and for that very reason, it carries more weight. The hadith on this is direct: the best صَدَقَةٌ is what you give when you are healthy, when you want to hold on to it, and when you fear poverty. That is when giving out really costs you something. That is the صَدَقَةٌ that truly rubs شَيْطَان's nose in it.

Then he says: you earn reward in proportion to your avoidance of wrong. And this brings up a related point. There are people in the أُمَّة who spent the better part of their lives in disobedience, then in old age made تَوْبَةٌ and began lecturing the young. But that person does not carry the same مَقَام as the young person who had every opportunity and every impulse toward disobedience — and chose otherwise. The older person no longer has the energy for most of those wrongs anyway. تَوْبَة comes more easily when the temptation is largely gone.

There is also a real danger in the opposite direction: the heart that has been in disobedience for decades sometimes becomes so encrusted that a genuine return becomes very difficult. And yet الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ, many Muslims do make تَوْبَةٌ as they grow older. They reach forty, forty-five, and something shifts. In Tunisia, they say the year of responsibility is forty — because that is when the وَحْي came to the Prophet. Which has given some people the mistaken impression that you can do whatever you like before forty. This is ignorance, pure and simple. And yet it has become a kind of cultural artifact in parts of the Muslim world — people who say: when I go to حَجٌّ, I will grow my beard and become a proper Muslim. Some of them do come back changed. But these attitudes are a marker of how far we have drifted from our tradition.

What Allah loves is the young person who is raised up in obedience. شَبَابٌ — youth — the Arabs generally put between fifteen and thirty-five, some say up to forty. After forty you are no longer a صَقْلٌ; you are a رَجُلٌ, a man.

Then he says: and also the contribution of a miser — giving out of the best of his wealth, while he is in sound health, seeking the pleasure of Allah — all of these things purify the heart. Moreover, anything done with discretion and concealment is also purifying. And the best of endeavors is the one that purifies the heart. So the best thing you can do with your life is what is genuinely good for your heart.

Likewise — and this is the other side — the worst things are those that harden the heart, especially when a person persists in them or takes pleasure in them. The نَفْس that enjoyed them: those are the things most destructive to the heart.

One thing that hardens the heart is speaking at length without mentioning Allah. This is recorded in the مُوَطَّأ of Imam Malik. سَيِّدِنَا عِيسَى is reported to have said: do not sit in gatherings without remembering Allah, for a gathering in which Allah is not mentioned will harden the heart — and you will not even notice it happening. The hardening comes gradually, spiritually and even, one might say, physically — and you are unaware. But the more the tongue is occupied with remembrance of Allah, the softer the heart becomes. That is simply the reality. The more ذِكْرٌ someone does, the more compassion flows from them, and the more they benefit.

Now this brings us to a truly important point. Allah says that a wrong action that produces in its wake a sense of shame and brokenness before Allah is better than an act of obedience that produces pride and arrogance in the one who performs it. And this is the secret of wrong actions — the wisdom concealed within them. Allah permitted us to stumble so that through the stumbling we might arrive at this state. The Prophet, صلى الله عليه وسلم, said: if you did not commit wrong actions, I would fear for you something worse than wrong actions — namely, being pleased with yourself, admiring yourself, vanity, the feeling that you are something wonderful. That is worse.

Now, Sidi Ahmad Zarruq clarifies this so there is no confusion. He says the good of طَهَارَة is in the very essence of طَهَارَة, and the evil he is describing is what obedience results in when it goes wrong. So do not misread this as saying that there is such a thing as good مَعَاصِيَهْ or bad طَهَارَة. طَهَارَة is good. مَعَاصِيَه is bad. Full stop. What is being said is about the secondary results. If the secondary result of an act of obedience was arrogance — and the secondary result of a disobedience was تَوْبَةٌ — then in terms of what was ultimately produced, the تَوْبَةٌ was the better outcome. The obedience generated poison; the disobedience generated a return to Allah.

Think of people in America who commit terrible crimes, go to prison, and find their way to Allah there. That happens often. The disobedience was genuinely wrong. But it became part of the causal chain through which Allah guided them. It was not a good thing — but it resulted in a good thing. And that is what he is saying: a wrong action that results in humility is preferable to an obedience that results in arrogance, in terms of what they produce in the heart.

Then he says:

وَذَرَّةٌ مِنْ عَمِلَ الْقَلْبِ الْعَلِي مِثْرُ الرِّضَى وَالزُهْدِ وَالتَوَكْرِ أَغْضَرُوا عَنْدَ اللَّهِ مِنْ جِبَالِ شَمَخْنَ مِنْ ظَوَاهِرَ الْعَمَالِ

An atom's weight of a lofty inward action — such as contentment, زُهْد, or trust in Allah — is greater with Allah than mountains of outward deeds performed without any heart. And this is the same principle as:

فَإِنَّ رَكَعَتَيْنِ مِنْ مُحِبٍ خَيْرَ مِنْ أَلْثٍ مِنْ غَيْرِ حُبِّ

Two رَكَعَاتٌ prayed with love are better than a thousand prayed without it. Immeasurably better.

<!-- page 200 -->

Then he mentions what Imam Sahnun, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

وَتَرْكُوا دِرْهَمٍ لِكَوْنِهِ حَظَرَ أَظْدَرُ مِنْ تَصَدُقَاتٍ وَعُمَرَ

Leaving a single دَانِق of what Allah has made حَرَامٌ is better than performing seventy thousand حَجٌّ and عُمْرَة. A دَانِق is the smallest unit of currency in the Arabic tradition — a farthing, a penny.

And they also relate that Imam Ja'far al-Sadiq said there are four things whose very best still carries a kind of diminishment:

أَرْبَعَةٌ عِزُّهَا وَظُلُّ

الدَّيْنُ وَلَوْ دَانِقٌ

A debt, even if it is only a دَانِق.

أَبِنْتُ وَلَوْ مَرْيَمُ

A daughter, even if she is Maryam — not because daughters are lesser than sons in worth, but because in that social context she lacks the physical strength and social protection a man possesses. And the word عَزِيزٌ in Arabic, let us not forget, also means قَوِيٌّ — physically strong, or strong in position. عَزِيزُ النَّصْرِ.

عَزِيزُ النَّصْرِ.

وَسُؤَادْ وَلَوْ كَيفَ الطَّرِيق

Asking a question, even if it is only: which way do I go? There is a humility inherent in asking that is itself a small diminishment of the self — not a wrong thing, just an honest reality.

وَالْوَحْشَةَ وَلَوْ لَيْلًا

And being alone — even for a single night.

<!-- page 201 -->

## The Root of All Diseases of the Heart

Now we come to the root causes of the diseases. And what I will do is cover the treatment of diseases through ذِكْر next week — إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, شَيْخ خَتْرِي will be here, and I will ask him to teach that class. He knows this book deeply. He loves it. He has spent a great deal of time with it. I have read sections of it with him — and I read much of it with شَيْخٌ Abdullah as well. We actually completed a خَتْمٌ of it on حَجٌّ, on the day of عَرَفَات. We went through every disease, named them all, and made دُعَاء asking اللهُ to purify us of all of them. إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ, for all of us.

Now he says: the comprehensive root — in the view of both Imam al-هِلَالi and Imam Ibn Askar — of all these diseases is love of the temporal world, حُبُّ الدُّنْيَا. There is some difference of opinion on this, as we saw: Ibn Abbad considered the root to be طَمَعٌ — desire. But you can see how طَمَعٌ flows from حُبُّ الدُّنْيَا. And when Ibn Askar says the root is حُبُّ الرِّيَاسَةِ — love of status and power — even that is حُبُّ الدُّنْيَا, because why do you want status? You want power, you want the world. So in the end it really does come back to حُبُّ الدُّنْيَا. There is a حَدِيث on this — though it is classified as weak — رَأْسُ كُلِّ خَطِيئَةٍ حُبُّ الْعَاجِلَةِ: the love of this world is at the root of every wrong action.

ابْنُ عَطَاءِ اللهِ, on the other hand, considered the root to be a man's self-satisfaction with himself. That is a distinct and fascinating take. He was a مَالِكِي jurist who died in 709 AH, corresponding to 1309 CE, and he is considered one of the greatest masters of this science. He wrote the حِكَم. Imam al-Banna said that had it been permissible to recite something other than the قُرْآن in prayer, it would have been permissible to recite the حِكَم — a clear hyperbole, but an indication of the profound esteem in which the work was held.

<!-- page 202 -->

In the thirty-fifth aphorism, ابْنُ عَطَاءِ اللهِ says that the source of every disobedience, every indifference, and every passion is self-satisfaction with the self. And the source of every obedience, every vigilance, and every virtue is dissatisfaction with the self. He says: it is better for you to keep company with an ignorant man who is dissatisfied with himself than with a learned man who is satisfied with himself. And then: what knowledge is there in a self-satisfied scholar? And what ignorance is there in an unlearned man who is dissatisfied with himself — and who therefore keeps asking, keeps wanting to learn?

This connects directly to something strange about modern culture. Think of the whole industry of self-help books aimed at getting rid of shame, building confidence, feeling good about yourself. But why is the person standing in that self-help aisle in the first place? Because he is dissatisfied with himself. And if he were completely self-satisfied, he would never pick up a book. The author of the book, incidentally, has no real interest in your becoming self-satisfied — because then you would stop buying books. Most of that industry is built on keeping you just dissatisfied enough to keep purchasing. But the point stands: dissatisfaction with the self is what drives a person to want to change and improve.

If anyone here were truly satisfied with themselves, you would not be in this class studying the diseases of the heart. The very fact that people have these struggles is what moves them to seek help. If you feel you lack guidance, you seek guidance. The one who submits to اللهُ begins seeking because he knows he needs اللهِ direction. But if you are walking through life perfectly content — I am already guided, I have no need — you will never reach out.

<!-- page 203 -->

Now, Sidi Ahmad Zarruq identifies three signs of contentment with the soul.

The first is seeing that the حَقٌّ belongs to oneself. This is interesting to consider in the modern context: we have a bill of rights but no bill of responsibilities. Islam is far more focused on your responsibilities than on your rights. The قُرْآن, in a real sense, is a bill of اللهِ rights over us. And the concept of هُمْan rights, as understood in the secular modern framework, carries a very different set of assumptions than the Islamic one. هَمٌّan beings do have rights — but in the Islamic understanding, those rights originate with اللهُ. They are اللهِ rights embedded in His servants. If someone is protected from oppression, it is because اللهُ has a حَقٌّ that His creation not be oppressed. If someone takes a life in accordance with شَرِيعَة, it is because اللهُ demands His right be fulfilled. The هُمْan being has a right — but it was given by اللهُ, and the other person fulfills it because he fears اللهُ.

This is why تَقْوَى — fear of اللهُ — is the very foundation of a مُسْلِم society. The first sign of self-satisfaction, then, is always seeing the rights as belonging to the self: they wronged me, it is all their fault, I am always the injured party. This is an extremely common psychological pattern.

There is a Moroccan story about a شَيْخٌ and a self-satisfied scholar who disliked him. This scholar came to visit one day and said: the least of my students is better than you. The شَيْخٌ said: show me. Come to my مَدْرَسَة tomorrow. The next day, the شَيْخٌ secretly instructed all his students: when the visiting scholar enters, do not return his سَلَامٌ. And when my student comes in — who had been set up to visit — do not say سَلَامٌ عَلَيْكُمْ to him either.

The self-satisfied scholar walked in, no one returned his greeting, and his reaction was immediate outrage: are these your students? Have they not heأَرْدٌ what اللهُ commands? Have they not heأَرْدٌ His words — return a greeting with what is better, or at least return it in kind?

Then the شَيْخٌ's own student came in. No one returned his سَلَامٌ. He said: سَلَامٌ عَلَيْكُمْ — again. Still nothing. Then he said: O brothers, please forgive me. I must have done something wrong. What did I do? Why have you not responded? Whatever I have done, please excuse me.

Those two responses reveal everything. The self-satisfied man arrived saying: I have a right, and you have violated it. The student arrived thinking: what right of yours did I fail to fulfill? One looked outwأَرْدٌ in demand; the other looked inwأَرْدٌ in concern. That is why, even as the least of the شَيْخٌ's students, the young man showed something the scholar — for all his learning — did not possess. This is the حِكْمَةٌ behind ابْنُ عَطَاءِ اللهِ's words: what knowledge does a self-satisfied scholar truly have? And what real ignorance remains in an unlearned man who is genuinely dissatisfied with himself and wants to be taught?

The second sign of contentment with the self is ignoring one's own faults by interpreting them away — convincing yourself that you are already in a state of purity, even if you have to construct a justification to get there. For instance, a person gets angry and tells himself: well, the Prophet said anger can be from the best of the أُمَّة, and I am from the best of the أُمَّة, so this is fine. He sees the fault but reframes it so that it no longer registers as a fault. This is what the poet meant when he said: the eye of contentment does not see faults. Just as the eye of one in love cannot see the beloved's flaws — and one of the signs of falling out of love is that you begin noticing them all: I never noticed before that one ear is larger than the other.

The third sign of self-satisfaction is excessive self-pity — poor me, I am having such a hأَرْدٌ time — chronic complaining and self-indulgent lamentation.

Those are the three signs of a person who is satisfied with himself. The three signs of the opposite — of a person genuinely dissatisfied with himself — are these:

The first is constant self-scrutiny, a persistent suspicion of one's own intentions. And this is modeled even by سَيِّدِنَا Yusuf, peace be upon him, who is a prophet — مَعْصُوم. There is a scholarly disagreement about the precise meaning of his statement, which Sayyid Ahmad Zarruq and others discuss. But سَيِّدِنَا Yusuf said: I will not declare my نَفْس innocent — for by its nature, the نَفْس commands to evil. So always exأَمِينe your intentions. Ask yourself: am I doing this for رِيَاءٌ? Hazrat Abu Bakr said: the only one who feels safe from نِفَاق is the hypocrite. The true believer is always watchful over himself, always alert to the blemishes of the soul. This is reflected in the دُعَاء the Prophet made: do not leave me to my own soul even for the blink of an eye, or less than that.

The second sign is forcing the self to do difficult things — things it dislikes. Eating less. Giving out money. Going out in the path of اللهُ. These are مَكْرُوهٌ to the نَفْس — the self naturally recoils from them. جِهَادٌ is genuinely hأَرْدٌ. Charity is genuinely hأَرْدٌ. The نَفْس wants to hold on to what it has. Compelling it to do otherwise is one of the marks of a person who has not surrendered to self-satisfaction.

Abu Uthman said: whoever sees anything good about himself has not truly seen the faults of his soul.

Now there is an obvious question here — and it is an important one, because the whole Western discourse around self-help, psychology, and modern wellness is built on the opposite premise. Is this not just self-loathing? Is this not low self-esteem? Is this not exactly what the self-esteem movement told us is harmful?

And the answer is: look at the actual people shaped by this tradition. Look at the real شُيُوخ. They are not miserable. They are not broken. They carry genuine هُمْility alongside genuine dignity.

Consider Aisha, may اللهُ be pleased with her. She was the wife of the best of creation. She was physically beautiful. She was among the most brilliant minds in Islamic history — and in هُمْan history. She came from a noble family, the aristocracy of Quraysh. And yet, when she yearned for اللهُ to declare her innocence in the incident of the slander, she said:

وَكُنتُ أَرَى نَفْسِي حَقِيرًۭا

I considered myself so insignificant that I would never have imagined the قُرْآن would be revealed on my account.

Despite all of that — beauty, lineage, brilliance, position — she did not regأَرْدٌ herself as someone for whom قُرْآن would descend. And yet Aisha was not a woman without self-confidence. She was a woman of enormous strength. She was one of the very few people who questioned the Prophet directly — she cهَلْlenged him on a point of قُرْآن when he said that a person given an easy reckoning would enter paradise:

يَدْخُلُ الْجَنَّةِ بِلَا حِسَابٍ وَلَمْ يَخُلُ اللَّهُ فَسَوْفْ يُحَاسِبُ حِسَابٍ يَسِيرًۭا

She pressed him: but how can you say that, given what اللهُ says? Most of the Companions would not have done that. It takes a person of extraordinary inner confidence to question the Prophet of اللهُ on a matter of قُرْآنic interpretation.

She also led an army out. She later regretted it, but in the moment she said: he is wrong and we are right — let us go resolve this. She rode out in her hawdaj to the Battle of the Camel. And when one of the men addressed her as يَا أُمَّ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ, she replied:

لَا أُمِّنُكَ

Which in the Arabic of the time carried the force of: do not call me your mother — you are no son of mine. That is a sharp, strong response. She was a strong woman.

And yet, with all of that, her sense of her own standing before اللهُ was one of profound smallness. That is what this teaching is about. It is not about collapsing into self-loathing. It is not about erasing confidence. It is about never becoming content with where you are — always knowing there is more, always wanting to improve. Be confident, yes. But do not be complacent.

No matter how accomplished you become, there is always someone better. No matter how much قُرْآن you have mastered, there is always more mastery to pursue. The master calligrapher looks at his work and sees room to improve. If he stops seeing it, he stops improving. And every one of these sciences — every field of knowledge, every spiritual state, every skill — is endless. True mastery belongs not to the one who has arrived, but to the one who never stops. The one committed to constant improvement is the real master, because they are always moving forwأَرْدٌ, always refining their technique. But the moment a person is self-satisfied, the improvement stops — and in a very real sense, so does the life of the heart.

This is what he means when he says that self-dissatisfaction acts as an impetus to seek virtuous character and to remain vigilant about what is unbecoming. So you remain watchful, اَلْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ.

We will stop there. Next week we will finish this section, and then the week after that I will go through the أَذْكَارُ الصَّبَاحِ وَالْمَسَاءِ — the morning and evening remembrances — which everyone should be doing. I will get you a copy of those. And then we will do the خَوَاطِر. That is how the remaining sessions will be structured.

Now there was a question from the previous class about women and male slaves — specifically whether a woman may own a bondsman. Yes, that is permissible in the فِقْه. But they are not مَحْرَم, and all the normal rules regأَرْدٌing seclusion and mixing apply. There can be no sexual relationship between the two. That was the question.

Now before we close, let me do the مَفَاتِيح — the keys — because I did not cover that last week.

مَفَاتِيح is the plural of مِفْتَاح, which means key in Arabic. The root, if you look at the word, is the same root as the verb to open. And the وَزْن — the morphological pattern — it sits on is that of a tool: the thing you use to do the action. مِكْنَسَة is the thing you use to sweep. The مِفْتَاح is the thing you use to open.

<!-- page 209 -->

In Arabic, the word for key does not just mean an object that unlocks something — it means the thing that causes an opening. The metaphor is richer than the English word "key" carries. And it connects directly to one of the names of اللهُ: الْفَتَّاحُ — the One who opens. People who carry spiritual keys have the ability to open things that are otherwise closed. And there are many أَحَادِيث on this: "such and such is the key to such and such." صَبْر is the key to الْفَرَجُ — to the opening, to relief. If you want فَرَجٌ from اللهُ, the key is patience.

The شَرِيعَة, then, is what tells us what is good and what is bad or ugly — the intellect has no independent access to that. It can sometimes arrive at the right answer, but not always, which makes it a relative and limited source of guidance rather than an absolute one. The شَرِيعَة indicates what is حَسَنَةٌ and what is صَيَّاح. And when a person recognizes that his نَفْس will not save him or help him, he turns to what actually will help him — namely, doing what اللهُ has commanded — and that recognition leads him to act in accordance with اللهُ and His message.

The poem's point, the sheikh explains, is that the origin of both heart disease and spiritual well-being is the company one keeps, for the حَدِيث states that a man is upon the دِين of his companion. If you keep company with someone who delays his prayer, who neglects زَكَاةٌ, or who commits wrong actions, you will inevitably drift towأَرْدٌ those same things. Conversely, good company will pull you in a good direction. This is precisely why people must exأَمِينe who they spend time with, because one's companions are a reliable indicator of one's own spiritual state. The sheikh offers a vivid illustration: if you sit at the door of a bar, you will smell the foulness of alcohol; if you sit at the door of a perfumer, you will breathe in the fragrance of perfume. Whatever you are near, you are affected by. Proximity to goodness brings goodness; proximity to corruption brings corruption.

For this reason, لُقْمَانُ الْحَكِيمُ — mentioned in the قُرْآن — is recorded in the مُوَطَّأ of إِمَام مَالِك as having told his son: sit with the عُلَمَاء. Keep their company. The عُلَمَاء are people who possess knowledge and live by it, and sitting with them is like rain falling on dry earth and bringing it back to life. That is what their company does to the earth of your heart — it revives it, and it does so in a way you may not even notice. Simply being in the presence of righteous, knowledgeable people allows the heart to stir back to life, and إِيمَان begins to grow and increase within it.

He says that sitting with the عُلَمَاء — the people of true knowledge, the عَارِفِينَ — will take a person from six things to six things: from doubt to certainty; from doing things for other than اللهُ to doing them with sincerity, with إِخْلَاصٌ, for اللهُ alone; from heedlessness to the remembrance of اللهُ; from desiring this world to desiring the next; from arrogance to repentance; and from corrupt inner character and bad intentions towأَرْدٌ people to sincere, wholesome character and genuine goodwill. ابْنُ عَطَاءِ اللهِ makes a related point in his حِكَم: do not keep company with someone whose state — his هَلْ, his condition — does not elevate you, and whose speech does not direct you towأَرْدٌ اللهُ. If a person's presence neither lifts your هِمَّة nor points you towأَرْدٌ اللهُ, that company is harmful.

Of all the diseases exأَمِينed in the مَطْهَرَة, the comprehensive cure — the one that addresses all of them — is the remembrance of اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى. More than anything else, ذِكْر is what heals the heart. Dhikr includes recitation of قُرْآن, صَلَاح, and all the formulas of praise: سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ, اَلْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ, سُبْحَانَ اللهِ الْعَظِيمِ, لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ, اللَّهُ أَكْبَرُ, لَا حَوْلَ وَلَا قُوَّةَ إِلَّا بِاللَّهِ — as well as the supplications the Prophet ﷺ has transmitted to us. But the highest and best form of ذِكْر is قِرَاءَةُ الْقُرْآنِ. A person who is in a state of ذِكْر will have a طَهَارَة heart — a pure heart.

سَيِّدِي Ahmad ibn Hanbal is reported to have seen اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى ninety-nine times in his dreams. In one of them he was asked: of all the things that draw a slave near to اللهُ, which is the greatest? اللهُ answered him: recitation of قُرْآن. He then asked: Ya اللهُ, with understanding or without understanding? And اللهُ answered, according to the Imam, بِفَهْمٍ أَوْ بِغَيْرِ فَهْمٍ — with understanding or without. This means that even someone who does not understand the words he is reading is still drawing close to اللهُ through the قُرْآن.

In صَحِيحُ الْبُخَارِيِّ, the Messenger of اللهُ ﷺ said that the difference between one who remembers اللهُ and one who does not is like the difference between the living and the dead. Whoever remembers اللهُ — قَلْبُهُ حَيٌّ — his heart is alive, and so he is alive. Whoever does not remember اللهُ — قَلْبُهُ مَيِّتٌ — his heart is dead, and so he is considered dead. A sound حَدِيث also says: make remembrance of اللهُ until people say you are mad. This is because one thing that marks people who are troubled in their minds is that they talk to themselves constantly. Someone who remembers اللهُ all the time may appear, outwأَرْدٌly, very similar — a non-مُسْلِم who sees you walking down the street doing ذِكْر may well think you are talking to yourself. That is what the حَدِيث points to.

There is also the sound حَدِيث, transmitted in various narrations, in which the Prophet ﷺ asks: sهَلْl I not tell you what the best of all your actions is — the purest in the sight of your Lord and King, the highest in rank among all the deeds that carry degrees, better for you than spending gold and silver, and better even than going out to meet your enemy in جِهَادٌ, striking them and being struck, killing them and being killed? They said: of course, tell us, يَا رَسُولَ اللهِ. He said: ذِكْرُ اللهِ تَعَالَى — the remembrance of اللهُ. Some people wonder how remembrance could be better than جِهَادٌ. The عُلَمَاء — may اللهُ have mercy on them — explain that ذِكْرُ اللَّهِ is the غَايَة, the end itself, the very purpose for which we were created. جِهَادٌ and everything else are وَسَائِل — means. And no means, however noble, is greater than the end it serves. We were created to remember اللهُ, which is why ذِكْر is the highest thing in the دُنْيَا.

اللهُ says: اذْكُرُونِي أَذْكُرْكُمْ — remember Me, and I will remember you. Whoever remembers his Lord, اللهُ remembers him. And whoever اللهُ remembers has been enriched in both this world and the next, and stands in need of nothing. Another distinguishing feature of ذِكْر — unlike حَجٌّ, fasting, or even formal prayer — is that it is not bound to a specific time or place. It can accompany every هُمْan act. There are أَذْكَار related to going to the toilet, to eating, to marital relations — every moment of life has a ذِكْر belonging to it. And it is said that whoever is in a state of remembrance of اللهُ is never struck by calamity in a way that harms him, because whatever befalls someone who was remembering اللهُ only raises him in rank. If he dies while remembering اللهُ, he dies as a martyr. This is the treatment for the diseased and foul soul — remembrance of اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى.

When you wish to engage in ذِكْر, you begin with اِسْتِغْفَار — seeking forgiveness — because the purpose of ذِكْر is purification, and the reason purification is needed is the wrong actions one has accumulated. So the process begins by asking اللهُ to remove those wrong actions. Once He removes them, the ذِكْر that follows will then remove their lingering effects, for wrong actions leave traces and effects on the heart.

You then send prayers upon the Prophet ﷺ, because صَلَاح upon the Prophet acts as a مُرَبٍّ — a nurturer — for the heart and as a means of its purification. This is why most of the later عُلَمَاء specifically encouraged these two practices: اِسْتِغْفَار for the wrong actions most of us are engaged in, and صَلَاح upon the Prophet because by the إِجْمَاع of the أُمَّة, it is the only ذِكْر that is absolutely guaranteed to be accepted by اللهُ. Anyone who says أَللَّهُمَّ صَلِّ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَآلِهِ وَسَلِّمْ is مَقْبُول — accepted. اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى commanded this with a command He Himself initiated, which is unique among all of His commands — He does not begin with the action Himself except in the case of prayer upon the Messenger of اللهُ ﷺ. He said: إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ وَمَلَٓئِكَتَهُۥ يُصَلُّونَ عَلَى ٱلنَّبِىِّۚ — and then enjoined the believers: يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا صَلُّوا عَلَيْهِ وَسَلِّمُوا تَسْلِيمًا — pray upon the Messenger of اللهُ and greet him with peace. أَللَّهُمَّ صَلِّ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَآلِهِ وَسَلِّمْ. Whoever prays upon the Prophet ﷺ once, اللهُ prays upon him ten times — this is established in صَحِيح حَدِيث. And whoever اللهُ has prayed upon ten times, what else could he possibly need?

أَلصَّلَاةُ عَلَيْهِ، عَلَيْهِ نُورُهُ فِي قَدْرِهِ، وَنُورُهُ فِي قَدْرِهِ، وَنُورٌ عَلَى الصِّرَاطِ — the Prophet of اللهُ ﷺ said that prayer upon him is light in this world, light in the grave, and light on the صِرَاط. It gives you guidance — and that guidance — when you need it most. It illuminates the grave so that it is light rather than dark. And it illuminates the صِرَاط so that you can see your way across it. Light in the heart, as the Prophet ﷺ said.

Because we have been commanded to pray upon the Prophet ﷺ, it is generally good to begin by reciting the actual آيَةٌ, for in doing so you are fulfilling the command of اللهُ — this is called امْتِثَالُ أَمْرِ اللهِ. That command is obligatory at least once in a lifetime, but you can fulfill it anew every single time you send prayers upon the Prophet ﷺ. The one who prays upon the Prophet should observe the same أَدَب he would observe when remembering اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى: it is مَنْدُوب for him to be in وُضُوء, مَنْدُوب to face the قِبْلَة, and so on — the same courtesies that are مَنْدُوب for prayer apply here as well.

He also mentions اِسْتِحْضَار — that when you pray upon the Messenger of اللهُ ﷺ, you should do so as if you were in his presence. In the same way that إِحْسَان in prayer means worshipping اللهُ as though you see Him, اِسْتِحْضَار in صَلَاح upon the Prophet ﷺ means bringing his presence before you as though he were there — calling to mind the guidance he gave us, all the things we owe him as our وَسِيلَة for teaching us لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ and for conveying to us the knowledge of اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى. And you remember that اللهُ Himself and His angels send prayer upon this Messenger, so in doing so you are participating in something that اللهُ Himself declared He does.

Another thing to be mindful of in ذِكْر is what is called لَحْن — incorrect pronunciation. You should avoid it to the best of your ability. Some people simply cannot pronounce every Arabic sound correctly, but the effort must still be made. He notes that some mispronunciations constitute a مَعْصِيَة or a خَطَأٌ, a sin or an error, while others can reach the level of رِدَّة — potentially taking a person out of Islam. As for those who cannot distinguish between sounds like دَادٌ and ذَكَاءٌ — the أَعْجَمِيَّة — if there is a genuine heaviness in the tongue, a physical impediment that persists despite every effort to correct it, then that person is مَعْذُور: excused by the شَرِيعَة. He should still engage in ذِكْر even if the pronunciation falls short, because the incapacity is real and اللهُ does not burden a soul beyond what it can bear.

He receives the rewأَرْدٌ, and no wrongdoing is entailed by performing the ذِكْر in that way. Among the greatest acts of worship is also meditating on اللهِ creation. اللهُ mentions in the قُرْآن those who remember Him standing, sitting, and reclining on their sides, and who reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth. Dhikr and تَفَكُّر — remembrance and reflection — are thus joined together.

It is said that the action of the heart, which is تَفَكُّر, is better than all the actions of the limbs. This is why one narration states that a single moment of reflection is better than a year of worship. The signs of genuine reflection fall into four directions.

The first is reflecting on the signs of اللهُ. The second is reflecting on اللهِ creation and what those signs point to — and this leads to love of اللهُ, because one sees everything اللهُ has done for the هُمْan being. The third is reflecting on اللهِ promised rewأَرْدٌs and the signs connected to them; each sign kindles a desire for the آخِرَة, so that the mark of this reflection is a yearning, a رَغْبَة, for جَنَّة. The fourth is reflecting on اللهِ promised punishment; the sign of this reflection is that fear takes root in the heart and the person turns away from disobedience.

There is also reflection on the weakness of the self — the harshness of the نَفْس towأَرْدٌ اللهُ despite His unceasing إِحْسَان. When one contemplates all that اللهُ has given and then honestly weighs how little one has offered in return, this reflection produces حَيَاء, a deep sense of shame before اللهُ — shame that He has given so much, while one has given so little in return.

The heart is what distinguishes good from evil, and the source of all these matters lies in the inrushings that come to the heart. These inrushings arrive like a guest descending upon the heart, and they take different forms.

The question Qais raised concerned what people today call the مُتَصَوِّفَة. Imam al-Maqari distinguished between the صُوفِيَّة and the مُتَصَوِّفَة. The مُتَصَوِّفَة are those who merely claim to be Sufis. A common sign of such people is that they engage in abundant ذِكْر while neglecting the وَجِبَتْ — the obligatory duties.

The شَيْخٌ's answer was direct: the first and foremost obligation on every هُمْan being is to acquire knowledge — before anything else. A person who has not learned the فَرَائِدُ الْعَيْنِ, the indiviدُعَاءlly obligatory matters, has no مَقَامٌ before اللهُ. It is as though he stands on the same footing as an animal, or worse, because an animal is not morally responsible, whereas this person is astray and is making no effort to correct himself.

شَيْخٌ 'Abd al-Khadim cited many scholars on this point — Imam al-Sha'rani, Imam al-Suyuti, and all the great imams who mastered both the outwأَرْدٌ sciences and تَصَوُّف — all of them affirm that فِقْه must precede تَصَوُّف. If a person pursues تَصَوُّف without first grounding himself in فِقْه, he will go astray. There is no تَصَوُّف without the knowledge of the فَرَائِضُ. Tasawwuf comes after فِقْه.

Even بِرُّ الْوَالِدَيْنِ — dutiful care of one's parents — though it is the highest virtue اللهُ mentions in the قُرْآن after تَوْحِيد, still comes after learning what اللهُ has made obligatory. If caring for one's parents is preventing one from acquiring that obligatory knowledge, then one is committing a wrong by abandoning what is more right.

Regأَرْدٌing the question about women: a woman in a state of menstrual impurity may perform all forms of ذِكْر that men perform. She may not recite from the قُرْآن extensively, though there are nuances — if she has memorized the قُرْآن, she may recite what is needed to retain her memorization, or she may recite short phrases such as تَعَوُّذ or verses like سُورَةُ الْمُلْكِ in that capacity. She may not touch the physical قُرْآن during that period. Beyond these restrictions, she may freely recite the أَذْكَار, لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ, and the prayers upon the Prophet ﷺ.

Regأَرْدٌing the question about non-مُسْلِمs in America who are drawn to Islam through its spiritual teachings — those who hear about the inner meanings of the tradition and feel themselves drawn closer — the شَيْخٌ's answer was that the best path for all people is submission to the will of اللهُ and acceptance of His commands as He has made them binding. When the Prophet ﷺ called people to Islam, he began with تَوْحِيد and then told them that اللهُ had made five prayers obligatory. He called them first to the unity of اللهُ and then to prayer. That is the methodology of the Messenger of اللهُ ﷺ, and that is what we should be calling people to.

In contemporary culture there is a pervasive false or pseudo-spirituality built on the notion that one can access حَقِيقَة without شَرِيعَة. This is a Dajjalic phenomenon — it misleads people and draws them away from the truth. It is, in effect, anti-spirituality, because genuine spirituality is protected by the شَرِيعَة. The fruit is protected by its peel; if you strip the peel before the fruit has ripened, you ruin it. Likewise, if you reach for spirituality before you have shielded yourself with the شَرِيعَة, you will destroy your soul and become one of those false figures — the مُتَمَلِّئٍ — people who project an outwأَرْدٌ appearance of spiritual attainment while their hearts remain filled with every disease.

The شَيْخٌ also addressed the importance of concealing one's faults. If a person has committed wrong, he should veil it and make تَوْبَةٌ; اللهُ will forgive him. But if he publicizes it openly, the harm multiplies, because the sin spreads as a tribulation beyond himself.

He cited the حَدِيث of the man who came to the mosque and told the Prophet ﷺ that he had committed زِينَة and asked to be given the prescribed punishment. The Prophet turned away from him. The man followed him outside and repeated himself. Finally the Prophet looked at him and said: "Did you not just perform وُضُوء and pray with us?" — meaning: that is what Islam is for, to purify one's wrongs. The man, however, persisted until the punishment was carried out. The Prophet ﷺ also said that whoever commits such offenses should cover himself with اللهِ covering and make تَوْبَةٌ, but whoever comes forwأَرْدٌ and openly admits his sin, the prescribed punishment will be applied.

The شَيْخٌ then turned to the question of companionship. For worldly matters, one should keep company with those who have less, not those who have more — because constantly comparing oneself to those who have bigger houses and better cars feeds love of the دُنْيَا. But for matters of the آخِرَة, one should always seek the company of those who are better. If one only keeps company with people whose دِين is weaker, one begins to think one's own دِين is sufficient and grows complacent.

The people of اللهُ, he noted, tend to see themselves as lesser than everyone around them, so the danger of pride does not really apply to them in the same way. Keeping their company benefits you precisely because their هُمْility makes you feel how much further you still have to go.

This is also why one should never look down on anyone. A person who appears to be in a worse state today might make a تَوْبَةٌ tomorrow that اللهُ accepts and rise to become a وَلِيٌّ of اللهُ — while the one who despised him might later stumble. No one can know another person's standing with اللهُ. So one should never feel superior to anyone in this دُنْيَا, and should always seek out the company of those who are better in their آخِرَة.

Those are rightly guided, if they truly follow what those imams brought — because those four imams وَالْمَغْضُوبِ عَلَيْهِمْ أَرْضَعَهَا وَقَفُوا غَيْرِهُمْ جَمِعُوا مَنَعَهَا — it is مَغْضُوبِ عَلَيْهِمْ; those four imams are مَغْضُوبِ عَلَيْهِمْ, meaning it is an absolute consensus that they are rightly guided. So those who call to something other than that are on shaky ground. In matters of عَقِيدَة, take a simple, sound creed from a straightforwأَرْدٌ source — Ibn Ashir, Imam al-Tahawi, any of those — and do not wade into فُرُوعٌ، فَرْعِيَّاتٌ: where is اللهُ, what is the hand like, all of that. Someone came to me and asked what I say about the verse ٱلرَّحْمَنُ عَلَى ٱلْعَرْشِ ٱسْتَوَىٰ, and I said that the very fact they are asking that question is, by the testimony of the قُرْآن itself, a sign of deviation in the heart — because the قُرْآن states that anyone who pursues those verses has deviation in their heart. So whenever you hear people arguing over the verses of اللهُ that belong to the مُتَشَابِهَاتٌ, the ambiguous ones, just leave them alone. That is guidance.

The question had been: what do we do in a time when شُيُوخ have disappeared and people of تَرْبِيَةٌ are scarce? What شَيْخٌ Hafri — may اللهُ bless him — said was that the very asking of that question is a sign that the person cares about their soul, so it is a good question, and it reflects a real condition. He said that if you are in this country, then things like the classes you are attending now are good — this is the right kind of thing to do. He said to make sure the source of your knowledge is sound. The things we teach, like these مُتُونٌ — these are مُتُونٌ that are ثَابِتٌ, rooted in our tradition, and they come through a chain of شُيُوخ. His شَيْخٌ and my شَيْخٌ is مُرَابِطُ الْحَاجِّ, and whoever has seen مُرَابِطُ الْحَاجِّ knows who he is. His knowledge is pure. The source of the knowledge is pure. No one who has lived with مُرَابِطُ الْحَاجِّ for over seventy years can say they ever saw him do something even مَكْرُوهٌ. He has never been heأَرْدٌ to speak an ill word about anyone by name. His knowledge is pure شَرِيعَة, and he teaches people the remembrance of اللهُ سُبْحَانَهُ وَتَعَالَى, because he is in a state that one of the people described this way: الْجُلُوسُ عِنْدَهُ كَالْجُلُوسِ عِنْدَ حَبْرِ الْغَيْبِ — sitting with him is like sitting with someone who is simultaneously present and absent. He is present in that he will teach you and answer your questions; he is absent in that his heart has moved away from this world — it is no longer in this world.

People like that are rare to find. There is a question here — الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ, he is مُرَابِط; we love him. A woman asked: is it possible for a woman to come and learn from you, to study with you? He said that anyone who wants to go to Mauritania may go. He said the hidden aspiration of a مُؤْمِن is أَقْطَعُ مِنَ السَّيْفِ — more cutting than the sword. If the hidden intention is strong, people can do remarkable things.
